## Chapter 1 <br> Algebra

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- understand the meaning of $|x|$, sketch the graph of $y=|a x+b|$ and use relations such as $|a|=|b| \Leftrightarrow a^{2}=b^{2}$ and $|x-a|<b \Leftrightarrow a-b<x<a+b$ in the course of solving equations and inequalities

- divide a polynomial, of degree not exceeding 4, by a linear or quadratic polynomial, and identify the quotient and remainder (which may be zero)

- use the factor theorem and the remainder theorem.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-001_647_1943_2125_137.jpg)

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| IGCSE $^{\circledR}$ / O Level Mathematics | Perform long division on numbers and find the remainder where necessary. | 1 Using long division, calculate:<br>&#160;&#160;a $4998 \div 14$<br>&#160;&#160;b $10287 \div 27$<br>&#160;&#160;c $4283 \div 32$<br> |
| IGCSE / O Level Mathematics | Sketch straight-line graphs. | 2 Sketch the graph of $y=2 x-5$. |

Why do we need to study algebra?

You have previously learnt the general formula, $x=\frac{-b \pm \sqrt{b^{2}-4 a c}}{2 a}$, for solving quadratic equations. Versions of this formula were known and used by the Babylonians nearly 4000 years ago.

It took until the 16th century for mathematicians to discover formulae for solving cubic equations, $a x^{3}+b x^{2}+c x+d=0$, and quartic equations, $a x^{4}+b x^{3}+c x^{2}+d x+e=0$. (These formulae are very complicated and there is insufficient space to include them here but you might wish to research them on the internet.)

Mathematicians then spent many years trying to discover a general formula for solving quintic equations, $a x^{5}+b x^{4}+c x^{3}+d x^{2}+e x+f=0$. Eventually, in 1824, a mathematician managed to prove that no general formula for this exists.

In this chapter you will develop skills for factorising and solving cubic and quartic equations. These types of equations have various applications in the real world. For example, cubic equations are used in thermodynamics and fluid mechanics to model the pressure/volume/temperature behaviour of gases and fluids.

You will also learn about a new type of function, called the modulus function.

### 1.1 The modulus function

The modulus of a number is the magnitude of the number without a sign attached.

The modulus of 3 is written $|3|$.

$$
|3|=3 \text { and }|-3|=3
$$

It is important to note that the modulus of any number (positive or negative) is always a positive number.

The modulus of a number is also called the absolute value.

The modulus of $x$, written as $|x|$, is defined as:

$$
|x|=\left\{\begin{array}{rll} x & \text { if } & x \geqslant 0 \\
-x & \text { if } & x<0 \end{array}\right.
$$

EXPLORE 1.1

You are given these eight statements:

$1|a+b|=|a|+|b|$

$2|a-b|=|a|-|b|$

$3|a b|=|a| \times|b|$

$4\left|\frac{a}{b}\right|=|a| \div|b|$, if $b \neq 0$

$5\left||a|^{2}=a^{2}\right.$

$6|a|^{n}=a^{n}$, where $n$ is a positive integer

$7|a+b| \leqslant|a|+|b|$

$8|a-b| \leqslant|a-c|+|c-b|$

You must decide whether a statement is

- If you think that a statement is either always true or never true, you must give a clear explanation to justify your answer.

- If you think that a statement is sometimes true you must give an example of when it is true and an example of when it is not true.

The statement $|x|=k$, where $k \geqslant 0$, means that $x=k$ or $x=-k$.

This property is used to solve equations that involve modulus functions.

If you are solving equations of the form $|a x+b|=k$, you can solve the equations using

$$
a x+b=k \quad \text { and } \quad a x+b=-k
$$

If you are solving harder equations of the form $|a x+b|=c x+d$, you can solve the equations using

$$
a x+b=c x+d \quad \text { and } \quad a x+b=-(c x+d)
$$

When solving these more complicated equations you must always check that your answers satisfy the original equation.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.1

Solve:

**a** $|2 x-1|=3$

**b** $|x-4|=2 x+1$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{array}{rlrlrl} |2 x-1| & =3 & & & \\
2 x-1 & =3 & \text { or } & 2 x-1 & =-3 \\
2 x & =4 & & 2 x & =-2 \\
x & =2 & x & =-1 \end{array}
$$

CHECK: $|2 \times 2-1|=3 \checkmark$ and $|2 \times(-1)-1|=3 \checkmark$ The solution is: $x=-1$ or $x=2$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
|x-4| & =2 x+1 & & \\
x-4 & =2 x+1 & \text { or } & x-4 \\
x & =-5 & & 3 x \\
& & x & =1
\end{align}
$$

CHECK: $|-5-4|=2 \times(-5)+1 \times$ and $|1-4|=2 \times 1+1 \checkmark$ The solution is: $x=1$

## EXPLORE 1.2

Using $|a|^{2}=a^{2}$ you can write $a^{2}-b^{2}$ as $|a|^{2}-|b|^{2}$.

Using the difference of two squares you can now write:

$$
a^{2}-b^{2}=(|a|-|b|)(|a|+|b|)
$$

Using the previous statement, explain how these three important results can be obtained:

(The symbol ⇔ means 'is equivalent to'.)

- $|a|=|b| \Leftrightarrow a^{2}=b^{2}$

- $|a|>|b| \Leftrightarrow a^{2}>b^{2}$

- $|a|<|b| \Leftrightarrow a^{2}<b^{2}$, if $b \neq 0$.

Worked example 1.2 shows you how to solve equations of the form $|c x+d|=|e x+f|$.

## KEY POINT 1.1

To solve equations of the form $|c x+d|=|e x+f|$ we can use the rule:

$$
|a|=|b| \Leftrightarrow a^{2}=b^{2}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.2

Solve the equation $|3 x+4|=|x+5|$.

Answer

Method 1

$$
|3 x+4|=|x+5|
$$

$$
\begin{array}{rlrlrl} 3 x+4 & =x+5 & & \text { or } & 3 x+4 & =-(x+5) \\
2 x & =1 & \text { or } & 4 x & =-9 \\
x & =\frac{1}{2} & & x & =-\frac{9}{4} \end{array}
$$

CHECK: $\left|3 \times \frac{1}{2}+4\right|=\left|\frac{1}{2}+5\right| \quad \checkmark,\left|3 \times\left(-\frac{9}{4}\right)+4\right|=\left|-\frac{9}{4}+5\right| \quad \checkmark$ Solution is: $x=\frac{1}{2}$ or $x=-\frac{9}{4}$ Method 2

$$
\begin{align}
|3 x+4| & =|x+5| \\
(3 x+4)^{2} & =(x+5)^{2}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
9 x^{2}+24 x+16 & =x^{2}+10 x+25 \\
8 x^{2}+14 x-9 & =0
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
(2 x-1)(4 x+9) & =0 \\
x=\frac{1}{2} \quad \text { or } \quad & x=-\frac{9}{4}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.3

Solve $|x+3|+|x+5|=10$.

Answer

$$
|x+3|+|x+5|=10 \quad \text { Subtract }|x+5| \text { from both sides. }
$$

$$
\begin{align}
|x+3| & =10-|x+5| \\
x+3 & =10-|x+5|--------- \text { (1) } \tag{1} \\
x+3 & =|x+5|-10 \qquad \text { Split the equation into two parts. } \tag{2}
\end{align}
$$

Using equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
-7+x+|x+5| & =0 \\
|x+5| & =7-x
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{rlrlrlrl} x+5 & =7-x & & \text { or } & & x+5 & =-(7-x) \\
2 x & =2 & \text { or } & 0 & =-12 & & & \\
x & =1 & & & & & & \\
2 x & & & & & & =-12 \text { is false. } \end{array}
$$

Using equation (2):

$$
\begin{align}
|x+5| & =x+13 \\
x+5 & =x+13 \text { or } x+5=-(x+13)
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
0=8 \quad \text { or } \quad 2 x & =-18 \\
x & =-9
\end{align}
$$

The solution is $x=1$ or $x=-9$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.4

Sketch the graph of $y=\left|\frac{1}{2} x-1\right|$, showing the points where the graph meets the axes.

Use your graph to express $\left|\frac{1}{2} x-1\right|$ in an alternative form.

Answer

First sketch the graph of $y=\frac{1}{2} x-1$.

The line has gradient $\frac{1}{2}$ and

**a** $y$-intercept of -1 .

You then reflect in the $x$-axis the part of the line that is below the $x$-axis.

The graph shows that $\left|\frac{1}{2} x-1\right|$ can be written as:

$$
\left|\frac{1}{2} x-1\right|= \begin{cases}\frac{1}{2} x-1 & \text { if } x \geqslant 2 \\
-\left(\frac{1}{2} x-1\right) & \text { if } x<2\end{cases}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-007_452_516_1036_1483.jpg)

These can also be found graphically by finding the $x$-coordinates of the points of intersection of the graphs of $y=|2 x-1|$ and $y=3$ as shown.

### 1.3 Solving modulus inequalities

Two useful properties that can be used when solving modulus inequalities are:

$$
|a| \leqslant b \Leftrightarrow-b \leqslant a \leqslant b \quad \text { and } \quad|a| \geqslant b \Leftrightarrow a \leqslant-b \quad \text { or } \quad a \geqslant b
$$

The following examples illustrate the different methods that can be used when solving modulus inequalities.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.5

Solve $|2 x-5|<3$.

Answer

Method 1: Using algebra

$$
\begin{array}{r} |2 x-5|<3 \\
-3<2 x-5<3 \\
2<2 x<8 \\
1<x<4 \end{array}
$$

Method 2: Using a graph

The graphs of $y=|2 x-5|$ and $y=3$ intersect at the points $A$ and $B$.

$$
|2 x-5|=\left\{\begin{array}{cl} 2 x-5 & \text { if } x \geqslant 2 \frac{1}{2} \\
-(2 x-5) & \text { if } x<2 \frac{1}{2} \end{array}\right.
$$

At $A$, the line $y=-(2 x-5)$ intersects the line $y=3$.

$$
\begin{align}
-(2 x-5) & =3 \\
-2 x+5 & =3 \\
2 x & =2 \\
x & =1
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-009_448_462_845_1550.jpg)

At $B$, the line $y=2 x-5$ intersects the line $y=3$.

$$
\begin{array}{r} 2 x-5=3 \\
2 x=8 \\
x=4 \end{array}
$$

To solve the inequality $|2 x-5|<3$ you must find where the graph of the function $y=|2 x-5|$ is below the graph of $y=3$.

Hence, $1<x<4$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.6

Solve the inequality $|2 x-1| \geqslant|3-x|$.

Answer

Method 1: Using algebra

$$
\begin{align}
|2 x-1| & \geqslant|3-x| \\
(2 x-1)^{2} & \geqslant(3-x)^{2} \\
4 x^{2}-4 x+1 & \geqslant 9-6 x+x^{2} \\
3 x^{2}+2 x-8 & \geqslant 0
\end{align}
$$

$$
(3 x-4)(x+2) \geqslant 0
$$

$$
y=3 x^{2}+2 x-8
$$

Critical values are -2 and $\frac{4}{3}$.

Hence, $x \leqslant-2$ or $x \geqslant \frac{4}{3}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-010_214_393_315_1208.jpg)

Method 2: Using a graph

The graphs of $y=|2 x-1|$ and $y=|3-x|$ intersect at the points $A$ and $B$.

$$
\begin{align}
& |2 x-1|=\left\{\begin{array}{l} 2 x-1 \quad \text { if } x \geqslant \frac{1}{2} \\
-(2 x-1) \text { if } x<\frac{1}{2} \end{array}\right. \\
& |3-x|=|x-3|= \begin{cases}x-3 & \text { if } x \geqslant 3 \\
-(x-3) & \text { if } x<3\end{cases}
\end{align}
$$

At $A$, the line $y=-(2 x-1)$ intersects the line $y=-(x-3)$.

$$
\begin{align}
2 x-1 & =x-3 \\
x & =-2
\end{align}
$$

At $B$, the line $y=2 x-1$ intersects the line $y=-(x-3)$.

$$
\begin{align}
2 x-1 & =-(x-3) \\
3 x & =4 \\
x & =\frac{4}{3}
\end{align}
$$

To solve the inequality $|2 x-1| \geqslant|3-x|$ you must find where the graph of the function $y=|2 x-1|$ is above the graph of $y=|3-x|$.

Hence, $x \leqslant-2$ or $x \geqslant \frac{4}{3}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-010_632_780_1041_1174.jpg)

### 1.4 Division of polynomials

A polynomial is an expression of the form

$$
a_{n} x^{n}+a_{n-1} x^{n-1}+a_{n-2} x^{n-2}+\qquad +a_{2} x^{2}+a_{1} x^{1}+a_{0}
$$

where:

- $x$ is a variable

- $n$ is a non-negative integer

- the coefficients $a_{n}, a_{n-1}, a_{n-2}, \qquad , a_{2}, a_{1}, a_{0}$ are constants

- $a_{n}$ is called the leading coefficient and $a_{n} \neq 0$

- $a_{0}$ is called the constant term.

The highest power of $x$ in the polynomial is called the degree of the polynomial.

For example, $5 x^{3}-6 x^{2}+2 x-6$ is a polynomial of degree 3.

In this section you will learn how to use long division to divide a polynomial by another polynomial. Alternative methods are possible, this is shown in Worked example 1.10.

DID YOU KNOW?

Polynomial functions can be used to predict the mass of an animal from its length.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-011_170_227_1743_1288.jpg)

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.7

Divide $x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6$ by $x-2$.

Answer

Step 1:

$$
\begin{align}
& x - 2 \longdiv { x ^ { 3 } + 2 x ^ { 2 } - 1 1 x + 6 } x^{2} \\
& \frac{x^{3}-2 x^{2}}{4 x^{2}}-11 x
\end{align}
$$

Divide the first term of the polynomial by $x$ : $x^{3} \div x=x^{2}$.

Multiply $(x-2)$ by $x^{2}: x^{2}(x-2)=x^{3}-2 x^{2}$.

Subtract: $\left(x^{3}+2 x^{2}\right)-\left(x^{3}-2 x^{2}\right)=4 x^{2}$.

Bring down the $-11 x$ from the next column.

Step 2: Repeat the process

$$
\begin{align}
x - 2 \longdiv { x ^ { 3 } + 2 x ^ { 2 } - 1 1 x + 6 } & \\
\frac{x^{3}-2 x^{2}}{4 x^{2}}-11 x & \text { Divide } 4 x^{2} \text { by } x: 4 x^{2} \div x=4 x . \\
\frac{4 x^{2}-8 x}{-3 x}+6 & \text { Multiply }(x-2) \text { by } 4 x: 4 x(x-2)=4 x^{2}-8 x . \\
\text { Subtract: }\left(4 x^{2}-11 x\right)-\left(4 x^{2}-8 x\right)=-3 x . & \text { Bring down the } 6 \text { from the next column } .
\end{align}
$$

Step 3: Repeat the process

$$
\begin{array}{lll} x - 2 \longdiv { x ^ { 3 } + 2 x ^ { 2 } - 1 1 x + 6 } & \\
\frac{x^{3}-2 x^{2}}{4 x^{2}-11 x} & & \text { Divide }-3 x \text { by } x:-3 x \div x=-3 . \\
\frac{4 x^{2}-8 x}{-3 x+6} & & \text { Multiply }(x-2) \text { by }-3:-3(x-2)=-3 x+6 . \\
& \text { Subtract: }(-3 x+6)-(-3 x+6)=0 . \end{array}
$$

Hence, $\left(x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6\right) \div(x-2)=x^{2}+4 x-3$.

Each part in the calculation in Worked example 1.7 has a special name:

$$
\left(x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6\right) \div(x-2)=\left(x^{2}+4 x-3\right)
$$

dividend divisor quotient

There was no remainder in this calculation so we say that $x-2$ is a factor of $x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.8

Find the remainder when $2 x^{3}-x+52$ is divided by $x+3$.

Answer

There is no $x^{2}$ term in $2 x^{3}-x+52$ so we write it as $2 x^{3}+0 x^{2}-x+52$.

Step 1:

$$
\begin{gathered} x + 3 \longdiv { 2 x ^ { 3 } + 0 x ^ { 2 } - x + 5 2 } \\
\frac{2 x^{3}+6 x^{2}}{-6 x^{2}}-x \end{gathered}
$$

Divide the first term of the polynomial by $x$ : $2 x^{3} \div x=2 x^{2}$.

Multiply $(x+3)$ by $2 x^{2}: 2 x^{2}(x+3)=2 x^{3}+6 x^{2}$.

Subtract: $\left(2 x^{3}+0 x^{2}\right)-\left(2 x^{3}+6 x^{2}\right)=-6 x^{2}$.

Bring down the $-x$ from the next column.

Step 2: Repeat the process

$$
\begin{array}{r} 2 x^{2}-6 x \\
x + 3 \longdiv { 2 x ^ { 3 } + 0 x ^ { 2 } } - x + 5 2 \\
\frac{2 x^{3}+6 x^{2}}{-6 x^{2}}-x \\
\frac{-6 x^{2}-18 x}{17 x}+52 \end{array}
$$

Divide $-6 x^{2}$ by $x$ : $-6 x^{2} \div x=-6 x$.

Multiply $(x+3)$ by $-6 x:-6 x(x+3)=-6 x^{2}-18 x$.

Subtract: $\left(-6 x^{2}-x\right)-\left(-6 x^{2}-18 x\right)=17 x$.

Bring down the 52 from the next column.

Step 3: Repeat the process

$$
\begin{array}{r} 2 x^{2}-6 x+17 \\
x + 3 \longdiv { 2 x ^ { 3 } + 0 x ^ { 2 } - x + 5 2 } \\
\frac{2 x^{3}+6 x^{2}}{-6 x^{2}}-x \\
-6 x^{2}-18 x \\
\frac{17 x+52}{17 x+51} \end{array}
$$

Divide $17 x$ by $x: 17 x \div x=17$.

Multiply $(x+3)$ by 17: $17(x+3)=17 x+51$.

Subtract: $(17 x+52)-(17 x+51)=1$.

The remainder is 1.

The calculation in Worked example 1.8 can be written as:

$$
\left(2 x^{3}-x+52\right)=(x+3) \times\left(2 x^{2}-6 x+17\right)+1
$$

dividend divisor quotient remainder

## KEY POINT 1.2

The division algorithm for polynomials is:

dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder

### 1.5 The factor theorem

In Worked example 1.7 you found that $x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6$ is exactly divisible by $x-2$.

$$
\left(x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6\right) \div(x-2)=x^{2}+4 x-3
$$

This can also be written as:

$$
x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6=(x-2)\left(x^{2}+4 x-3\right)
$$

If a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x)$ divides exactly by a linear factor $x-c$ to give the polynomial $\mathrm{Q}(x)$, then

$$
\mathrm{P}(x)=(x-c) \mathrm{Q}(x)
$$

Substituting $x=c$ into this formula gives $\mathrm{P}(c)=0$. This is known as the factor theorem.

## KEY POINT 1.3

If for a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x), \mathrm{P}(c)=0$ then $x-c$ is a factor of $\mathrm{P}(x)$.

For example, when $x=2$,

$$
x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6=(2)^{3}+2(2)^{2}-11(2)+6=8+8-22+6=0
$$

Therefore $(x-2)$ is a factor of $x^{3}+2 x^{2}-11 x+6$.

The factor theorem can be extended to:

## KEY POINT 1.4

If for a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x), \mathrm{P}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)=0$ then $a x-b$ is a factor of $\mathrm{P}(x)$.

For example, when $x=\frac{2}{3}$,

$$
3 x^{3}-2 x^{2}-3 x+2=3\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{3}-2\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{2}-3\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)+2=\frac{8}{9}-\frac{8}{9}-2+2=0
$$

Hence $3 x-2$ is a factor of $3 x^{3}-2 x^{2}-3 x+2$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.9

Use the factor theorem to show that $x-3$ is a factor of $x^{3}+2 x^{2}-16 x+3$.

Answer

Let

$$
f(x)=x^{3}+2 x^{2}-16 x+3
$$

If $\mathrm{f}(3)=0$, then $(x-3)$ is a factor.

$$
\begin{align}
f(3) & =(3)^{3}+2(3)^{2}-16(3)+3 \\
& =27+18-48+3 \\
& =0
\end{align}
$$

Hence $x-3$ is a factor of $x^{3}+2 x^{2}-16 x+3$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.10

**a** Given that $3 x^{2}+5 x-2$ is a factor of $3 x^{3}+17 x^{2}+a x+b$, find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$.

**b** Hence, factorise the polynomial completely.

Answer

**a** Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=3 x^{3}+17 x^{2}+a x+b$.

Factorising $3 x^{2}+5 x-2$ gives $(3 x-1)(x+2)$.

Hence, $3 x-1$ and $x+2$ are also factors of $\mathrm{f}(x)$.

Using the factor theorem, $\mathrm{f}\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)=0$ and $\mathrm{f}(-2)=0$.

$$
\begin{align}
f\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)=0 \text { gives } 3\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^{3}+17\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)^{2}+a\left(\frac{1}{3}\right)+b & =0 \\
\frac{1}{9}+\frac{17}{9}+\frac{a}{3}+b & =0 \\
a+3 b & =-6 \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
f(-2)=0 \text { gives } 3(-2)^{3}+17(-2)^{2}+a(-2)+b & =0 \\
-24+68-2 a+b & =0 \\
2 a-b & =44 \tag{2}
\end{align}
$$

(1) +3×(2) gives $7 a=126$

$$
a=18
$$

Substituting in (2) gives $b=-8$.

Hence, $a=18$ and $b=-8$.

**b** Method 1: Using long division

$$
\begin{array}{r} x+4 \\
3 x ^ { 2 } + 5 x - 2 \longdiv { 3 x ^ { 3 } + 1 7 x ^ { 2 } + 1 8 x - 8 } \\
\frac{3 x^{3}+5 x^{2}-2 x}{12 x^{2}+20 x-8} \\
\frac{12 x^{2}+20 x-8}{0} \end{array}
$$

Hence, $3 x^{3}+17 x^{2}+18 x-8=\left(3 x^{2}+5 x-2\right)(x+4)$

$$
=(3 x-1)(x+2)(x+4)
$$

Method 2: Equating coefficients

If $3 x^{2}+5 x-2$ is a factor, $3 x^{3}+17 x^{2}+18 x-8$ can be written as

$$
3 x^{3}+17 x^{2}+18 x-8=\left(3 x^{2}+5 x-2\right)(c x+d)
$$

coefficient of $x^{3}$ is 3, so $c=1$ since $3 \times 1=3 \quad$ constant term is -8 , so $d=4$ since $-2 \times 4=-8$

Hence, $3 x^{3}+17 x^{2}+18 x-8=(3 x-1)(x+2)(x+4)$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.11

Solve $x^{3}+4 x^{2}-19 x+14=0$.

Answer

Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{3}+4 x^{2}-19 x+14$.

The positive and negative factors of 14 are ±1, ±2, ±7 and ±14.

$\mathrm{f}(1)=(1)^{3}+4(1)^{2}-19(1)+14=0$, so $x-1$ is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$.

The other factors of $\mathrm{f}(x)$ can be found by any of the following methods.

Method 1: Trial and error

$\mathrm{f}(2)=(2)^{3}+4(2)^{2}-19(2)+14=0$, so $x-2$ is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$ $\mathrm{f}(-7)=(-7)^{3}+4(-7)^{2}-19(-7)+14=0$, so $x+7$ is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$.

Hence, $\mathrm{f}(x)=(x-1)(x-2)(x+7)$.

Method 2: Long division

$$
\begin{array}{r} x^{2}+5 x-14 \\
x - 1 \longdiv { x ^ { 3 } + 4 x ^ { 2 } - 1 9 x + 1 4 } \\
\frac{x^{3}-x^{2}}{5 x^{2}-19 x} \\
\frac{5 x^{2}-5 x}{-14 x+14} \\
\frac{-14 x+14}{0} \end{array}
$$

Hence,

$$
\begin{align}
\mathrm{f}(x) & =(x-1)\left(x^{2}+5 x-14\right) \\
& =(x-1)(x-2)(x+7)
\end{align}
$$

Method 3: Equating coefficients

Since $x-1$ is a factor, $x^{3}+4 x^{2}-19 x+14$ can be written as:

$$
x^{3}+4 x^{2}-19 x+14=(x-1)\left(a x^{2}+b x+c\right)
$$

coefficient of $x^{3}$ is 1, so $a=1$ since $1 \times 1=1$

constant term is 14, so $c=-14$ since $-1 \times-14=14$

$$
x^{3}+4 x^{2}-19 x+14=(x-1)\left(x^{2}+b x-14\right)
$$

Equating the coefficients of $x^{2}$ gives $4=-1+b$, so $b=5$.

Hence,

$$
\begin{align}
\mathrm{f}(x) & =(x-1)\left(x^{2}+5 x-14\right) \\
& =(x-1)(x-2)(x+7)
\end{align}
$$

The solutions of the equation $x^{3}+4 x^{2}-19 x+14=0$ are: $x=1, x=2$ and $x=-7$.

### 1.6 The remainder theorem

Consider $\mathrm{f}(x)=2 x^{3}-5 x^{2}-2 x+12$.

Substituting $x=2$ in the polynomial gives $\mathrm{f}(2)=2(2)^{3}-5(2)^{2}-2(2)+12=4$.

When $2 x^{3}-5 x^{2}-2 x+12$ is divided by $x-2$, there is a remainder:

$$
\begin{array}{r} 2 x^{2}-x-4 \\
x - 2 \longdiv { 2 x ^ { 3 } - 5 x ^ { 2 } - 2 x + 1 2 } \\
\frac{2 x^{3}-4 x^{2}}{-x^{2}-2 x} \\
\frac{-x^{2}+2 x}{-4 x+12} \\
\frac{-4 x+8}{4} \end{array}
$$

The remainder is 4 . This is the same value as f(2).

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathrm{f}(x)=2 x^{3}-5 x^{2}-2 x+12 \text { can be written as: } \\
& \mathrm{f}(x)=(x-2)\left(2 x^{2}-x-4\right)+4
\end{align}
$$

In general:

If a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x)$ is divided by $x-c$ to give the polynomial $\mathrm{Q}(x)$ and a remainder $R$, then:

$$
\mathrm{P}(x)=(x-c) \mathrm{Q}(x)+R
$$

Substituting $x=c$ into this formula gives $\mathrm{P}(c)=R$.

This leads to the remainder theorem.

## KEY POINT 1.5

The remainder theorem:

If a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x)$ is divided by $x-c$, the remainder is $\mathrm{P}(c)$.

The remainder theorem can be extended to:

## KEY POINT 1.6

If a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x)$ is divided by $a x-b$, the remainder is $\mathrm{P}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.12

Use the factor theorem to find the remainder when $4 x^{3}-2 x^{2}+5 x-8$ is divided by $x+1$.

Answer

Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=4 x^{3}-2 x^{2}+5 x-8$

$$
\begin{align}
\text { Remainder } & =f(-1) \\
& =4(-1)^{3}-2(-1)^{2}+5(-1)-8 \\
& =-4-2-5-8 \\
& =-19
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 1.13

$$
f(x)=6 x^{3}+a x^{2}+b x-4
$$

When $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $x-1$, the remainder is 3.

When $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $3 x+2$, the remainder is -2 .

Find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$.

Answer

$$
f(x)=6 x^{3}+a x^{2}+b x-4
$$

When $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $x-1$ the remainder is 3, hence $\mathrm{f}(1)=3$.

$$
\begin{align}
6(1)^{3}+a(1)^{2}+b(1)-4 & =3 \\
a+b & =1 \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

When $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $3 x+2$ the remainder is -2, hence $\mathrm{f}\left(-\frac{2}{3}\right)=-2$.

$$
\begin{align}
6\left(-\frac{2}{3}\right)^{3}+a\left(-\frac{2}{3}\right)^{2}+b\left(-\frac{2}{3}\right)-4 & =-2 \\
2 a-3 b & =17 \tag{2}
\end{align}
$$

$2 \times(1)-(2)$ gives $5 b=-15$

$$
b=-3
$$

Substituting $b=-3$ in equation (1) gives $a=4$.

$a=4$ and $b=-3$.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

The modulus function

- The modulus function $|x|$ is defined by:

$$
|x|=\left\{\begin{array}{r} x \text { if } x \geqslant 0 \\
-x \text { if } x<0 \end{array}\right.
$$

The division algorithm for polynomials

- $\mathrm{P}(x)=\mathrm{D}(x) \times \mathrm{Q}(x)+\mathrm{R}(x)$ dividend = divisor × quotient + remainder

The factor theorem

- If for a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x), \mathrm{P}(c)=0$ then $x-c$ is a factor of $\mathrm{P}(x)$.

- If for a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x), \mathrm{P}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)=0$ then $a x-b$ is a factor of $\mathrm{P}(x)$.

The remainder theorem

- If a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x)$ is divided by $x-c$, the remainder is $\mathrm{P}(c)$.

- If a polynomial $\mathrm{P}(x)$ is divided by $a x-b$, the remainder is $\mathrm{P}\left(\frac{b}{a}\right)$.

**17** The polynomials $\mathrm{P}(x)$ and $\mathrm{Q}(x)$ are defined as:

$$
\mathrm{P}(x)=x^{3}+a x^{2}+b \text { and } \mathrm{Q}(x)=x^{3}+b x^{2}+a .
$$

It is given that $(x-2)$ is a factor of $\mathrm{P}(x)$ and that when $\mathrm{Q}(x)$ is divided by $(x+1)$ the remainder is -15.

**a** Find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$. [5]

**b** When $a$ and $b$ take these values, find the least possible value of $\mathrm{P}(x)-\mathrm{Q}(x)$ as $x$ varies. [2]

**18** The polynomial $5 x^{3}-13 x^{2}+17 x-7$ is denoted by $\mathrm{p}(x)$.

**a** Find the quotient when $\mathrm{p}(x)$ is divided by $(x-1)$, and show that the remainder is 2. [4]

**b** Hence show that the polynomial $5 x^{3}-13 x^{2}+17 x-9$ has exactly one real root. [3]

**19** The polynomial $4 x^{3}+k x^{2}-65 x+18$ is denoted by $\mathrm{f}(x)$.

**a** Given that $(x+2)$ is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$, find the value of $k$. [2]

**b** When $k$ has this value, solve the equation $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$. [4]

**c** Write down the roots of $\mathrm{f}\left(x^{2}\right)=0$. [1]

**20** The polynomial $2 x^{3}-5 x^{2}+a x+b$, where $a$ and $b$ are constants, is denoted by $\mathrm{f}(x)$. It is given that when $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $(x+2)$ the remainder is 8 and that when $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $(x-1)$ the remainder is 50.

**a** Find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$. [5]

**b** When $a$ and $b$ have these values, find the quotient and remainder when $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $x^{2}-x+2$. [3]

**21** The polynomial $2 x^{3}-9 x^{2}+a x+b$, where $a$ and $b$ are constants, is denoted by $\mathrm{f}(x)$. It is given that ( $x+2$ ) is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$, and that when $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by ( $x+1$ ) the remainder is 30.

**a** Find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$. [5]

**b** When $a$ and $b$ have these values, solve the equation $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$. [4]

**22** The polynomial $x^{3}+3 x^{2}+4 x+2$ is denoted by $\mathrm{f}(x)$.

**i** Find the quotient and remainder when $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by $x^{2}+x-1$. [4]

**ii** Use the factor theorem to show that $(x+1)$ is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$. [2]

**23** The polynomial $4 x^{3}+a x^{2}+9 x+9$, where $a$ is a constant, is denoted by $\mathrm{p}(x)$. It is given that when $\mathrm{p}(x)$ is divided by $(2 x-1)$ the remainder is 10.

**i** Find the value of $a$ and hence verify that $(x-3)$ is a factor of $\mathrm{p}(x)$. [3]

**ii** When $a$ has this value, solve the equation $\mathrm{p}(x)=0$. [4]

Cambridge International AS \& A Level Mathematics 9709 Paper 21 Q5 November 2011

**24** The polynomial $a x^{3}-5 x^{2}+b x+9$, where $a$ and $b$ are constants, is denoted by $\mathrm{p}(x)$. It is given that ( $2 x+3$ ) is a factor of $\mathrm{p}(x)$, and that when $\mathrm{p}(x)$ is divided by ( $x+1$ ) the remainder is 8 .

**i** Find the values of $a$ and $b$. [5]

**ii** When $a$ and $b$ have these values, factorise $\mathrm{p}(x)$ completely. [3]

## Chapter 2 <br> Logarithmic and exponential functions

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- understand the relationship between logarithms and indices, and use the laws of logarithms (excluding change of base)

- understand the definition and properties of $\mathrm{e}^{x}$ and $\ln x$, including their relationship as inverse functions and their graphs

- use logarithms to solve equations and inequalities in which the unknown appears in indices

- use logarithms to transform a given relationship to linear form, and hence determine unknown constants by considering the gradient and/or intercept.

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| IGCSE / O Level Mathematics | Use and interpret positive, negative, fractional and zero indices. | 1 Evaluate.<br>&#160;&#160;a $5^{-2}$<br>&#160;&#160;b $8^{\frac{2}{3}}$<br>&#160;&#160;c $7^{0}$<br>2 Solve $32^{x}=2$.<br> |
| IGCSE / O Level Mathematics | Use the rules of indices. | 3 Simplify.<br>&#160;&#160;a $3 x^{-4} \times \frac{2}{3} x^{\frac{1}{2}}$<br>&#160;&#160;b $\frac{2}{5} x^{\frac{1}{2}} \div 2 x^{-2}$<br>&#160;&#160;c $\left(\frac{2 x^{5}}{3}\right)^{3}$<br> |

What is a logarithm?

At IGCSE / O Level we learnt how to solve some simple exponential equations. For example, $32^{x}=2$ can be solved by first writing 32 as a power of 2. In this chapter we will learn how to solve more difficult exponential equations such as $a^{x}=b$ where there is no obvious way of writing the numbers $a$ and $b$ in index form with the same base number. In order to be able to do this we will first learn about a new type of function called the logarithmic function. A logarithmic function is the inverse of an exponential function.

At IGCSE / O Level we learnt about exponential growth and decay and its applications to numerous real-life problems such as investments and population sizes. Logarithmic functions also have real-life applications such as the amount of energy released during an earthquake (the Richter scale). One of the most fascinating occurrences of logarithms in nature is that of the logarithmic spiral that can be observed in a nautilus shell. We also see logarithmic spiral shapes in spiral galaxies, and in plants such as sunflowers.

2.1 Logarithms to base 10

Consider the exponential function $\mathrm{f}(x)=10^{x}$.

To solve the equation $10^{x}=6$, we could make an estimate by noting that $10^{0}=1$ and $10^{1}=10$, hence $0<x<1$. We could then try values of $x$ between 0 and 1 to find a more accurate value for $x$.

The graph of $y=10^{x}$ could also be used to give a more accurate value for $x$ when $10^{x}=6$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-026_287_631_2319_554.jpg)

From the graph, $x \approx 0.78$.

There is, however, a function that gives the value of $x$ directly:

If $10^{x}=6$ then $x=\log _{10} 6$.

$\log _{10} 6$ is read as ' $\log 6$ to base 10'.

log is short for logarithm.

On a calculator, for logs to the base 10, we use the log or lg key.

So if $10^{x}=6$

then

$$
\begin{align}
& x=\log _{10} 6 \\
& x=0.778 \text { to } 3 \text { significant figures. }
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 2.1

The rule for base 10 is:

If $y=10^{x}$ then $x=\log _{10} y$

This rule can be described in words as:

$\log _{10} y$ is the power that 10 must be raised to in order to obtain $y$.

For example, $\log _{10} 100=2$ since $100=10^{2}$.

$y=10^{x}$ and $y=\log _{10} x$ are inverse functions.

We say that $y=10^{x} \Leftrightarrow x=\log _{10} y$.

This is read as: $y=10^{x}$ is equivalent to saying that $x=\log _{10} y$.

$$

$$
\left.\log _{10} 10=1\right) \quad\left(\log _{10} 1=0\right.
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.1

**a** Convert $10^{x}=58$ to logarithmic form.

**b** Solve $10^{x}=58$ giving your answer correct to 3 significant figures.

Answer

**a** Method 1

$$
10^{x}=58
$$

Step 1: Identify the base and index ⟶ base $=10$, index $=x$

Step 2: Start to write in $\log$ form $\longrightarrow x=\log _{10}$ ?

Step 3: Complete the $\log$ form $\longrightarrow x=\log _{10} 58$

$$
10^{x}=58 \Leftrightarrow x=\log _{10} 58
$$

Method 2

$$
10^{x}=58
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{10} 10^{x} & =\log _{10} 58 \\
x & =\log _{10} 58
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
10^{x} & =58 \\
x & =\log _{10} 58 \\
x & \approx 1.76
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.2

**a** Convert $\log _{10} x=3.5$ to exponential form.

**b** Solve $\log _{10} x=3.5$ giving your answer correct to 3 significant figures.

Answer

**a** Method 1

$$
\log _{10} x=3.5
$$

Step 1: Identify the base and index ⟶ base = 10 , index = 3.5

Step 2: Start to write in exponential form $\longrightarrow 10^{3.5}=$ ?

Step 3: Complete the exponential form ⟶ X = $10^{3.5}$ $\log _{10} x=3.5 \Leftrightarrow x=10^{3.5}$

Method 2

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{10} x & =3.5 \\
10^{\log _{10} x} & =10^{3.5} \\
x & =10^{3.5}
\end{align}
$$

Write each side as an exponent of 10.

Use $10^{\log _{10} x}=x$.

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{10} x & =3.5 \\
x & =10^{3.5} \\
x & \approx 3160
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.3

Find the value of:

**a** $\quad \log _{10} 10000$

**b** $\quad \log _{10} 0.0001$

**c** $\quad \log _{10} 1000 \sqrt{10}$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{10} 10000 & =\log _{10} 10^{4} \\
& =4
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{10} 0.0001 & =\log _{10} 10^{-4} \\
& =-4
\end{align}
$$

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{10} 1000 \sqrt{10} & =\log _{10} 10^{3.5} \qquad Q \frac{W r i t e}{} 1000 \sqrt{10} \text { as a power of } 10 . \\
& =3.5
\end{align}
$$

### 2.2 Logarithms to base $\boldsymbol{a}$

In the last section you learnt about logarithms to the base of 10.

The same principles can be applied to define logarithms in other bases.

KEY POINT 2.2

If $y=a^{x}$ then $x=\log _{a} y$

$$
\begin{array}{ll} \log _{a} a=1 & \log _{a} 1=0 \\
\log _{a} a^{x}=x & a^{\log _{a} x}=x \end{array}
$$

The conditions for $\log _{a} x$ to be defined are:

- $a>0$ and $a \neq 1$

- $x>0$

We say that $y=a^{x} \Leftrightarrow x=\log _{a} y$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.4

Convert $5^{3}=125$ to logarithmic form.

Answer

Method 1

$$
5^{3}=125
$$

Step 1: Identify the base and index ⟶ base = 5, index = 3

Step 2: Start to write in $\log$ form $\longrightarrow 3=\log _{5}$ ?

Step 3: Complete the $\log$ form $\quad \longrightarrow \quad 3=\log _{5} 125$

$$
5^{3}=125 \Leftrightarrow 3=\log _{5} 125
$$

Method 2

$$
5^{3}=125
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{5} 5^{3} & =\log _{5} 125 \\
3 & =\log _{5} 125
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.5

Convert $\log _{3} x=2.5$ to exponential form.

Answer

Method 1

$$
\log _{3} x=2.5
$$

Step 1: Identify the base and index $\longrightarrow \quad$ base $=3$, index $=2.5$ Step 2: Start to write in exponential form $\longrightarrow 3^{2.5}=$ ?

Step 3: Complete the exponential form $\longrightarrow 3^{2.5}=x$

$$
\log _{3} x=2.5 \Leftrightarrow 3^{2.5}=x
$$

Method 2

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{3} x & =2.5 \\
3^{\log _{3} x} & =3^{2.5} \\
x & =3^{2.5}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.6

Find the value of:

**a** $\log _{2} 16$

**b** $\quad \log _{3} \frac{1}{9}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{2} 16 & =\log _{2} 2^{4} \\
& =4
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{3} \frac{1}{9} & =\log _{3} 3^{-2} \\
& =-2
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.7

Simplify $\log _{x}\left(\frac{\sqrt[3]{x}}{x}\right)$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{x}\left(\frac{\sqrt[3]{x}}{x}\right) & =\log _{x}\left(x^{-\frac{2}{3}}\right) \\
& =-\frac{2}{3}
\end{align}
$$

### 2.3 The laws of logarithms

If $x$ and $y$ are both positive and $a>0$ and $a \neq 1$, then the following laws of logarithms can be used.

## KEY POINT 2.3

| Multiplication law | Division law | Power law |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $\log _{a}(x y)=\log _{a} x+\log _{a} y$ | $\log _{a}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)=\log _{a} x-\log _{a} y$ | $\log _{a}(x)^{m}=m \log _{a} x$ |

Proofs:

| Multiplication law | Division law | Power law |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $\begin{aligned} & \log _{a}(x y) \\ & =\log _{a}\left(a^{\log _{a} x} \times a^{\log _{a} y}\right) \\ & =\log _{a}\left(a^{\log _{a} x+\log _{a} y}\right) \\ & =\log _{a} x+\log _{a} y \end{aligned}$ | $\begin{aligned} & \log _{a}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right) \\ & =\log _{a}\left(\frac{a^{\log _{a} x}}{a^{\log _{a} y}}\right) \\ & =\log _{a}\left(a^{\log _{a} x-\log _{a} y}\right) \\ & =\log _{a} x-\log _{a} y \end{aligned}$ | $\begin{aligned} & \log _{a}(x)^{m} \\ & =\log _{a}\left(\left(a^{\log _{a} x}\right)^{m}\right) \\ & =\log _{a}\left(a^{m \log _{a} x}\right) \\ & =m \log _{a} x \end{aligned}$ |

$$
\text { Using the power law, }\begin{align}
\log _{a}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) & =\log _{a} x^{-1} \\
& =-\log _{a} x
\end{align}
$$

Another useful rule to remember:

KEY POINT 2.4

$$
\log _{a}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)=-\log _{a} x
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.8

Use the laws of logarithms to simplify these expressions.

**a** $\log _{2} 3+\log _{2} 5$

**b** $\log _{3} 8-\log _{3} 4$

**c** $2 \log _{5} 2+\log _{5} 3$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{gathered} \log _{2} 3+\log _{2} 5 \\
=\log _{2}(3 \times 5) \\
=\log _{2} 15 \end{gathered}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{gathered} \log _{3} 8-\log _{3} 4 \\
=\log _{3}\left(\frac{8}{4}\right) \\
=\log _{3} 2 \end{gathered}
$$

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
& 2 \log _{5} 2+\log _{5} 3 \\
& =\log _{5} 2^{2}+\log _{5} 3 \\
& =\log _{5}(4 \times 3) \\
& =\log _{5} 12
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.9

Given that $\log _{4} p=x$ and $\log _{4} q=y$, express in terms of $x$ and/or $y$

**a** $\log _{4} p^{5}-\log _{4} q^{2}$

**b** $\log _{4} \sqrt{p}+5 \log _{4} \sqrt[3]{q}$

**c** $\quad \log _{4}\left(\frac{64}{p}\right)$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
& \log _{4} p^{5}-\log _{4} q^{2} \\
& =5 \log _{4} p-2 \log _{4} q \\
& =5 x-2 y
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
& \log _{4} \sqrt{p}+5 \log _{4} \sqrt[3]{q} \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \log _{4} p+\frac{5}{3} \log _{4} q \\
& =\frac{1}{2} x+\frac{5}{3} y
\end{align}
$$

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
& \log _{4}\left(\frac{64}{p}\right) \\
& =\log _{4} 4^{3}-\log _{4} p \\
& =3-x
\end{align}
$$

### 2.4 Solving logarithmic equations

We have already learnt how to solve simple logarithmic equations. In this section we will learn how to solve more complicated equations.

Since $\log _{a} x$ only exists for $x>0$ and for $a>0$ and $a \neq 1$, it is essential when solving equations involving logs that all roots are checked in the original equation.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.10

Solve:

**a** $2 \log _{8}(x+2)=\log _{8}(2 x+19)$

**b** $4 \log _{x} 2-\log _{x} 4=2$

Answer

**a** $2 \log _{8}(x+2)=\log _{8}(2 x+19)$ Use the power law.

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{8}(x+2)^{2} & =\log _{8}(2 x+19) \\
(x+2)^{2} & =2 x+19 \\
x^{2}+4 x+4 & =2 x+19 \\
x^{2}+2 x-15 & =0 \\
(x-3)(x+5) & =0 \\
x=3 \text { or } x & =-5
\end{align}
$$

Check when $x=3: 2 \log _{8}(x+2)=2 \log _{8} 5=\log _{8} 25$ is defined

$$
\log _{8}(2 x+19)=\log _{8} 25 \text { is defined. }
$$

So $x=3$ is a solution, since both sides of the equation are defined and equivalent in value.

Check when $x=-5: 2 \log _{8}(x+2)=2 \log _{8}(-3)$ is not defined.

So $x=-5$ is not a solution of the original equation.

Hence, the solution is $x=3$

**b** $4 \log _{x} 2-\log _{x} 4=2$ Use the power law.

$$
\begin{align}
\log _{x} 2^{4}-\log _{x} 2^{2} & =2 \\
\log _{x} 2^{4-2} & =2 \\
\log _{x} 2^{2} & =2 \\
\log _{x} 4 & =2 \\
x^{2} & =4 \\
x & = \pm 2
\end{align}
$$

Since logarithms only exist for positive bases, $x=-2$ is not a solution.

Check when $x=2$ :

$$
\begin{align}
4 \log _{2} 2-\log _{2} 4 & =4-2 \log _{2} 2 \\
& =4-2 \\
& =2
\end{align}
$$

So $x=2$ satisfies the original equation.

Hence, the solution is $x=2$

### 2.5 Solving exponential equations

At IGCSE / O Level you learnt how to solve exponential equations whose terms could be converted to the same base. In this section you will learn how to solve exponential equations whose terms cannot be converted to the same base.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.11

Solve, giving your answers correct to 3 significant figures.

**a** $5^{2 x+1}=7$

**b** $3^{2 x}=4^{x+5}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
5^{2 x+1} & =7 \\
\log 5^{2 x+1} & =\log 7 \\
(2 x+1) \log 5 & =\log 7 \\
2 x \log 5+\log 5 & =\log 7
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
3^{2 x} & =4^{x+5} \\
\log 3^{2 x} & =\log 4^{x+5} \\
2 x \log 3 & =(x+5) \log 4 \\
2 x \log 3 & =x \log 4+5 \log 4
\end{align}
$$

$$
x(2 \log 3-\log 4)=5 \log 4
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& x=\frac{5 \log 4}{2 \log 3-\log 4} \\
& x=8.55 \text { to } 3 \text { significant figures. }
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.12

Solve the equation $2\left(3^{2 x}\right)+7\left(3^{x}\right)=15$, giving your answers correct to 3 significant figures.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
2\left(3^{2 x}\right)+7\left(3^{x}\right)-15 & =0 \\
2 y^{2}+7 y-15 & =0 \\
(2 y-3)(y+5) & =0 \\
y=1.5 \text { or } y & =-5
\end{align}
$$

When $y=1.5$

$$
3^{x}=1.5 \quad \text { Take logs of both sides } .
$$

$$
\begin{align}
x \log 3 & =\log 1.5 \\
x & =\frac{\log 1.5}{\log 3}=0.369 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

When $y=-5$

$3^{x}=-5$

There are no solutions to this equation since $3^{x}$ is always positive.

Hence, the solution is $x=0.369$ to 3 significant figures.

### 2.6 Solving exponential inequalities

To solve inequalities such as $2^{x}>3$ we take the logarithm of both sides of the inequality. We can take logarithms to any base but care must be taken with the inequality symbol when doing so.

- If the base of the logarithm is greater than 1 then the inequality symbol remains the same.

- If the base of the logarithm is between 0 and 1 then the inequality symbol must be reversed.

This is because $y=\log _{a} x$ is an increasing function if $a>1$ and it is a decreasing function when $0<a<1$.

Hence, when taking logarithms to base 10 of both sides of an inequality the inequality symbol is unchanged.

It is also important to remember that if we divide both sides of an inequality by a negative number then the inequality symbol must be reversed.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.13

Solve the inequality $0.6^{x}<0.7$, giving your answer in terms of base 10 logarithms.

Answer

| $0.6^{x}<0.7$ | Take logs to base 10 of both sides, inequality symbol is unchanged. |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\log 0.6^{x}<\log 0.7$ | Use the power rule for logs. |
| $x \log 0.6<\log 0.7$ | Divide both sides by $\log _{10}$ 0.6. |
| $x>\frac{\log 0.7}{\log 0.6}$ $x>0.698 \text { (correct to } 3 \text { significant figures) }$ | $\log 0.6$ is negative so the inequality symbol is reversed. |

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.14

Solve the inequality $4 \times 3^{2 x-1}>5$, giving your answer in terms of base 10 logarithms.

Answer

| $4 \times 3^{2 x-1}>5$ | Divide both sides by 4 . |
| :--- | :--- |
| $3^{2 x-1}>\frac{5}{4}$ | Take logs to base 10 of both sides and use the power rule. |
| $(2 x-1) \log 3>\log \frac{5}{4}$ | Expand brackets. |
| $(2 \log 3) x-\log 3>\log \frac{5}{4}$ | Rearrange. |
| $\begin{aligned} & x>\frac{\log \frac{5}{4}+\log 3}{2 \log 3} \\ & x>\frac{\log \frac{15}{4}}{\log 9} \end{aligned}$ | Simplify. |

### 2.7 Natural logarithms

There is another type of logarithm to a special base called e.

The number e is an irrational number and $\mathrm{e} \approx 2.718$.

The function $y=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ is called the natural exponential function.

Logarithms to the base of e are called natural logarithms.

$\ln x$ is used to represent $\log _{\mathrm{e}} x$.

KEY POINT 2.5

If $y=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ then $x=\ln y$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-042_353_358_917_181.jpg)

The curve $y=\ln x$ is the reflection of $y=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ in the line $y=x$.

$y=\ln x$ and $y=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ are inverse functions.

All the rules of logarithms that we have learnt so far also apply for natural logarithms.

We say that $y=\mathrm{e}^{x} \Leftrightarrow x=\log _{\mathrm{e}} y$. DID YOU KNOW?

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-042_440_363_1675_184.jpg)

The Scottish mathematician John Napier (1550-1617) is credited with the discovery of logarithms. His original studies involved logarithms to the base of $\frac{1}{\mathrm{e}}$.

DID YOU KNOW?

The number e is also known as Euler's number. It is named after Leonhard Euler (1707-1783), who devised the following formula for calculating the value of e:

$$
e=1+\frac{1}{1}+\frac{1}{1 \times 2}+\frac{1}{1 \times 2 \times 3}+\frac{1}{1 \times 2 \times 3 \times 4}+\frac{1}{1 \times 2 \times 3 \times 4 \times 5}+
$$

## 2．8 Transforming a relationship to linear form

When we collect experimental data for two variables we often wish to find a mathematical relationship connecting the variables．When the data plots on a graph lie on a straight line the relationship can be easily found using the equation $y=m x+c$ ， where $m$ is the gradient and $c$ is the $y$－intercept．It is more common，however，for the data plots to lie on a curve．

Logarithms can be used to convert some curves into straight lines．

This is the case for relationships of the form $y=k x^{n}$ and $y=k\left(a^{x}\right)$ where $k, a$ and $n$ are constants．

Logarithms to any base can be used but it is more usual to use those that are most commonly available on calculators，which are natural logarithms or logarithms to the base 10.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.15

Convert $y=a \mathrm{e}^{b x}$ ，where $a$ and $b$ are constants，into the form $Y=m X+c$ ．

Answer

$$
y=a \mathrm{e}^{b x}
$$

Taking natural logarithms of both sides gives：

$$
\begin{align}
& \ln y=\ln \left(a \mathrm{e}^{b x}\right) \\
& \ln y=\ln a+\ln \mathrm{e}^{b x} \\
& \ln y=\ln a+b x \\
& \ln y=b x+\ln a
\end{align}
$$

Now compare $\ln y=b x+\ln a$ with $Y=m X+c$ ：

$$
\begin{align}
& \underline{\ln y}=b \text { (X) }+\ln a \\
& \stackrel{\text { 十个 }}{\text { 十 }} \stackrel{\triangle}{\text { 十 }} \\
& Y=m X+c
\end{align}
$$

The non－linear equation $y=a \mathrm{e}^{b x}$ becomes the linear equation：

$$
Y=m X+c \text {, where } Y=\ln y, X=x, \quad m=b \text { and } c=\ln a
$$

It is important to note：

－The variables $X$ and $Y$ in $Y=m X+c$ must contain only the original variables $x$ and $y$ ． （They must not contain the unknown constants $a$ and $b$ ．） －The constants $m$ and $c$ must contain only the original unknown constants $a$ and $b$ ．（They must not contain the variables $x$ and $y$ ．）

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.16

The variables $x$ and $y$ satisfy the equation $y=a\left(\mathrm{e}^{-b x^{2}}\right)$ ，where $a$ and $b$ are constants．The graph of $\ln y$ against $x^{2}$ is a straight line passing through the points（0．55，0．84）and（1．72，0．26）as shown in the diagram．

Find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$ correct to 2 decimal places．

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-044_336_449_2306_1494.jpg)

Answer

$$
y=a \times \mathrm{e}^{-b x^{2}}
$$

Taking natural logarithms of both sides gives:

$$
\begin{align}
& \ln y=\ln \left(a \times \mathrm{e}^{-b x^{2}}\right) \\
& \ln y=\ln a+\ln \mathrm{e}^{-b x^{2}} \\
& \ln y=\ln a-b x^{2} \\
& \ln y=-b x^{2}+\ln a
\end{align}
$$

Now compare $\ln y=-b x^{2}+\ln a$ with $Y=m X+c$ :

$$
\begin{align}
\ln y & =-b\left(x^{2}\right)+\ln a \\
\stackrel{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}{\boldsymbol{\uparrow}} \stackrel{-}{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}} & +\stackrel{\boldsymbol{\Upsilon}}{\boldsymbol{\uparrow}} \\
Y & =m X+c
\end{align}
$$

Gradient $=m=\frac{0.26-0.84}{1.72-0.55}=-0.4957$

$\therefore b=0.50$ to 2 decimal places

Using $Y=m X+c, X=0.55, Y=0.84$ and $m=-0.4957$

$$
\begin{align}
0.84 & =-0.4957 \qquad \times 0.55+c \\
c & =1.1126 \qquad \\
\therefore \ln a & =1.1126 \qquad \\
a & =\mathrm{e}^{1.1126 }
\end{align}
$$

Hence $a=3.04$ and $b=0.50$ to 2 decimal places.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 2.17

| $x$ | 5 | 10 | 20 | 40 | 80 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $y$ | 2593 | 1596 | 983 | 605 | 372 |

The table shows experimental values of the variables $x$ and $y$.

**a** By plotting a suitable straight-line graph, show that $x$ and $y$ are related by the equation $y=k \times x^{n}$, where $k$ and $n$ are constants.

**b** Use your graph to estimate the value of $k$ and the value of $n$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
y & =k \times x^{n} \\
\ln y & =\ln \left(k \times x^{n}\right) \\
\ln y & =\ln k+\ln x^{n} \\
\ln y & =n \ln x+\ln k
\end{align}
$$

Now compare $\ln y=n \ln x+\ln k$ with $Y=m X+c$ :

$$
\ln y=n(\ln x)+\ln k
$$

$$
\stackrel{\uparrow}{Y}=\stackrel{\uparrow}{m} \stackrel{\uparrow}{X}+\stackrel{\uparrow}{c}
$$

Hence the graph of $\ln y$ against $\ln x$ needs to be drawn where:

- gradient $=n$

- intercept on vertical axis $=\ln k$

Table of values is

| $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { l n }} \boldsymbol{x}$ | 1.61 | 2.30 | 3.00 | 3.69 | 4.38 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $\ln y$ | 7.86 | 7.38 | 6.89 | 6.41 | 5.92 |

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-046_521_566_926_770.jpg)

The points form an approximate straight line, so $x$ and $y$ are related by the equation $y=k \times x^{n}$.

**b** $n=$ gradient $\approx \frac{5.92-7.86}{4.38-1.61} \approx-0.70$ to 2 significant figures.

ln $k=$ intercept on vertical axis

$$
\begin{align}
\ln k & \approx 9 \\
k & \approx \mathrm{e}^{9} \\
k & \approx 8100 \text { to } 2 \text { significant figures. }
\end{align}
$$

DID YOU KNOW?

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-046_302_401_2124_181.jpg)

The distributions of a diverse range of natural and man-made phenomena approximately follow the power law $y=a x^{b}$. These include the size of craters on the moon, avalanches, species extinction, body mass, income and populations of cities.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

The rules of logarithms

- If $y=a^{x}$ then $x=\log _{a} y$.

- $\log _{a} a=1, \log _{a} 1=0, \log _{a} a^{x}=x, a^{\log _{a} x}=x$

- Product rule: $\log _{a}(x y)=\log _{a} x+\log _{a} y$

- Division rule: $\log _{a}\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)=\log _{a} x-\log _{a} y$

- Power rule: $\log _{a}(x)^{m}=m \log _{a} x$

- Special case of power rule: $\log _{a}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)=-\log _{a} x$

Natural logarithms

- Logarithms to the base of e are called natural logarithms.

- $\mathrm{e} \approx 2.718$

- $\ln x$ is used to represent $\log _{\mathrm{e}} x$.

- If $y=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ then $x=\ln y$.

- All the rules of logarithms apply for natural logarithms.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-049_353_357_883_1218.jpg)

Transforming a relationship to linear form

- Logarithms can be used to convert relationships of the form $y=k x^{n}$ and $y=k\left(a^{x}\right)$, where $k, a$ and $n$ are constants, into straight lines of the form $Y=m X+c$, where $X$ and $Y$ are functions of $x$ and of $y$.

## Chapter 3

## Trigonometry

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- understand the relationship of the secant, cosecant and cotangent functions to cosine, sine and tangent, and use properties and graphs of all six trigonometric functions for angles of any magnitude

- use trigonometrical identities for the simplification and exact evaluation of expressions and in the course of solving equations, and select an identity or identities appropriate to the context, showing familiarity in particular with the use of:

- $\sec ^{2} \theta=1+\tan ^{2} \theta$ and $\operatorname{cosec}^{2} \theta=1+\cot ^{2} \theta$

- the expansion of $\sin (A \pm B), \cos (A \pm B)$ and $\tan (A \pm B)$

- the formulae for $\sin 2 A, \cos 2 A$ and $\tan 2 A$

- the expression of $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta$ in the forms $R \sin (\theta \pm \alpha)$ and $R \cos (\theta \pm \alpha)$.

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |

| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 5 | Find exact values of the sine, cosine and tangent of 30°, 45° and 60° and related angles. | 2 Find the exact values of:<br>a $\sin 120^{\circ}$<br>b $\cos 225^{\circ}$<br>c $\tan 150^{\circ}$<br> |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 5 | Solve trigonometric equations using the identities $\tan \theta \equiv \frac{\sin \theta}{\cos \theta}$ and $\sin ^{2} \theta+\cos ^{2} \theta \equiv 1$. | 3 Solve for $0^{\circ} \leqslant x \leqslant 360^{\circ}$.<br>a $5 \sin x=3 \cos x$<br>b $2 \cos ^{2} x-\sin x=1$<br> |

## Why do we study trigonometry?

In addition to the three main trigonometrical functions (sine, cosine and tangent) there are three more commonly used trigonometrical functions (cosecant, secant and cotangent) that we will learn about. These are sometimes referred to as the reciprocal trigonometrical functions.

We will also learn about the compound angle formulae, which form a basis for numerous important mathematical techniques. These techniques include solving equations, deriving further trigonometric identities, the addition of different sine and cosine functions and deriving rules for differentiating and integrating trigonometric functions, which we will cover later in this book.

As you know, scientists and engineers represent oscillations and waves using trigonometric functions. These functions also have further uses in navigation, engineering and physics.

### 3.1 The cosecant, secant and cotangent ratios

There are a total of six trigonometric ratios. You have already learnt about the ratios sine, cosine and tangent. In this section you will learn about the other three ratios, which are cosecant (cosec), secant (sec) and cotangent (cot).

KEY POINT 3.1

The three reciprocal trigonometric ratios are defined as:

$$
\operatorname{cosec} \theta=\frac{1}{\sin \theta} \quad \sec \theta=\frac{1}{\cos \theta} \quad \cot \theta=\frac{1}{\tan \theta}\left(=\frac{\cos \theta}{\sin \theta}\right)
$$

Consider the right-angled triangle:

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-053_224_330_2421_1060.jpg)

$$
\begin{array}{rlrl} \sin \theta & =\frac{y}{r} & \cos \theta & =\frac{x}{r} \\
\operatorname{cosec} \theta & =\frac{r}{y} & \sec \theta & =\frac{r}{x} \\
\cot \theta & =\frac{x}{y} \end{array}
$$

The following identities can be found from this triangle:

$$
\begin{align}
x^{2}+y^{2} & =r^{2} & & \text { Divide both sides by } x^{2} . \\
1+\left(\frac{y}{x}\right)^{2} & =\left(\frac{r}{x}\right)^{2} & & \text { Use } \frac{y}{x}=\tan \theta \text { and } \frac{r}{x}=\sec \theta . \\
1+\tan ^{2} \theta & \equiv \sec ^{2} \theta & &
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{array}{rlrl} x^{2}+y^{2} & =r^{2} & & \text { Divide both sides by } y^{2} . \\
\left(\frac{x}{y}\right)^{2}+1 & =\left(\frac{r}{y}\right)^{2} & \text { Use } \frac{x}{y}=\cot \theta \text { and } \frac{r}{y}=\operatorname{cosec} \theta . \\
\cot ^{2} \theta+1 & \equiv \operatorname{cosec}^{2} \theta & & \end{array}
$$

We will need these important identities to solve trigonometric equations later in this section.

KEY POINT 3.2

$$
\begin{align}
& 1+\tan ^{2} \theta \equiv \sec ^{2} \theta \\
& \cot ^{2} \theta+1 \equiv \operatorname{cosec}^{2} \theta
\end{align}
$$

The graphs of $y=\sin \theta$ and $y=\operatorname{cosec} \theta$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-054_249_924_1444_181.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-054_563_700_1287_1294.jpg)

The function $y=\operatorname{cosec} \theta$ is the reciprocal of the function $y=\sin \theta$.

The function $y=\sin \theta$ is zero when $\theta=0^{\circ}, 180^{\circ}, 360^{\circ},$, this means that $y=\operatorname{cosec} \theta$ is not defined at each of these points because $\frac{1}{0}$ is not defined. Hence vertical asymptotes need to be drawn at each of these points.

The graphs of $\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }} \boldsymbol{\theta}$ and $\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{\operatorname { s e c }} \boldsymbol{\theta}$ We can find the graph of $y=\sec \theta$ in a similar way from the graph of $y=\cos \theta$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-055_266_944_562_218.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-055_564_698_421_1328.jpg)

The graphs of $\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{\operatorname { t a n }} \boldsymbol{\theta}$ and $\boldsymbol{y}=\boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o t }} \boldsymbol{\theta}$ We can find the graph of $y=\cot \theta$ from the graph of $y=\tan \theta$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-055_426_706_1173_222.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-055_422_697_1178_1326.jpg)

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.1

Find the exact value of $\operatorname{cosec} 240^{\circ}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\operatorname{cosec} 240^{\circ} & =\frac{1}{\sin 240^{\circ}} \\
& =\frac{1}{-\sin 60^{\circ}} \\
& =\frac{1}{-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}} \\
& =-\frac{2}{\sqrt{3}}
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-055_338_351_1921_1247.jpg)

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.2

Solve $\sec ^{2} x-\tan x-3=0$ for $0^{\circ} \leqslant x \leqslant 360^{\circ}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\sec ^{2} x-\tan x-3 & =0 \\
\tan x^{2} x-\tan x-2 & =0 \\
(\tan x-2)(\tan x+1) & =0 \\
\tan x=2 \text { or } \tan x & =-1 \\
\tan x=2 \Rightarrow x & =63.4^{\circ}(\text { calculator }) \\
\text { or } x & =63.4^{\circ}+180^{\circ}=243.4^{\circ} \\
\tan x=-1 \Rightarrow x & =-45^{\circ}(\text { not in required range }) \\
\text { or } x & =-45^{\circ}+180^{\circ}=135^{\circ} \\
\text { or } x & =135^{\circ}+180^{\circ}=315^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

The values of $x$ are 63.4°, 135°, 243.4°, 315°.

Use $1+\tan ^{2} x \equiv \sec ^{2} x$.

Factorise.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-056_473_935_695_1042.jpg)

### 3.2 Compound angle formulae

EXPLORE 3.1

**1** Sunita says that: $\sin (A+B)=\sin A+\sin B$

Use a calculator with $A=20^{\circ}$ and $B=50^{\circ}$ to prove that Sunita is wrong.

**2** The diagram shows $\triangle P Q R$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-058_327_540_617_1124.jpg)

$R X$ is perpendicular to $P Q$.

$\angle A$ and $\angle B$ are acute angles.

$Q R=p, P R=q$ and $R X=h$.

**a** Write down an expression for $h$ in terms of:

**i** $q$ and $A$

**ii** $\quad p$ and $B$

**b** Using the formula area of triangle $=\frac{1}{2} a b \sin C$, we can write:

Area of $\Delta P Q R=\frac{1}{2} p q \sin (A+B)$.

Find an expression in terms of $p, q, A$ and $B$ for the area of:

**i** $\triangle P R X$

**ii** $\triangle Q R X$

**c** Use your expressions for the areas of triangles $P Q R, P R X$ and $Q R X$ to prove that:

$$
\sin (A+B) \equiv \sin A \cos B+\cos A \sin B
$$

**3** In the identity $\sin (A+B) \equiv \sin A \cos B+\cos A \sin B$, replace $B$ by $-B$ to show that:

$$
\sin (A-B) \equiv \sin A \cos B-\cos A \sin B
$$

**4** In the identity $\sin (A-B) \equiv \sin A \cos B-\cos A \sin B$, replace $A$ by ( $90^{\circ}-A$ ) to show that:

$$
\cos (A+B) \equiv \cos A \cos B-\sin A \sin B
$$

**5** In the identity $\cos (A+B) \equiv \cos A \cos B-\sin A \sin B$, replace $B$ by $-B$ to show that:

$$
\cos (A-B) \equiv \cos A \cos B+\sin A \sin B
$$

**6** By writing $\tan (A+B)$ as $\frac{\sin (A+B)}{\cos (A+B)}$ and $\tan (A-B)$ as $\frac{\sin (A-B)}{\cos (A-B)}$, show that:

$$
\tan (A+B) \equiv \frac{\tan A+\tan B}{1-\tan A \tan B} \text { and } \tan (A-B) \equiv \frac{\tan A-\tan B}{1+\tan A \tan B}
$$

KEY POINT 3.3

Summarising, the six compound angle formulae are:

$$
\begin{align}
\sin (A+B) & \equiv \sin A \cos B+\cos A \sin B \\
\sin (A-B) & \equiv \sin A \cos B-\cos A \sin B \\
\cos (A+B) & \equiv \cos A \cos B-\sin A \sin B \\
\cos (A-B) & \equiv \cos A \cos B+\sin A \sin B \\
\tan (A+B) & \equiv \frac{\tan A+\tan B}{1-\tan A \tan B} \\
\tan (A-B) & \equiv \frac{\tan A-\tan B}{1+\tan A \tan B}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.3

Find the exact value of:

**a** $\sin 105^{\circ}$

**b** $\cos 42^{\circ} \cos 12^{\circ}+\sin 42^{\circ} \sin 12^{\circ}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\sin 105^{\circ} & =\sin \left(60^{\circ}+45^{\circ}\right) \\
& =\sin 60^{\circ} \cos 45^{\circ}+\cos 60^{\circ} \sin 45^{\circ} \\
& =\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) \\
& =\frac{\sqrt{3}+1}{2 \sqrt{2}}
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
& \cos 42^{\circ} \cos 12^{\circ}+\sin 42^{\circ} \sin 12^{\circ} \\
& =\cos \left(42^{\circ}-12^{\circ}\right) \\
& =\cos 30^{\circ} \\
& =\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.4

Given that $\sin A=\frac{4}{5}$ and $\cos B=\frac{5}{13}$, where $A$ is obtuse and $B$ is acute, find the value of:

**a** $\sin (A+B)$

**b** $\cos (A-B)$

**c** $\tan (A-B)$

Answer

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-059_339_348_1629_263.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-059_337_350_1624_1232.jpg)

$\sin A=\frac{4}{5}, \cos A=-\frac{3}{5}, \tan A=-\frac{4}{3}$ $\sin B=\frac{12}{13}, \cos B=\frac{5}{13}, \tan B=\frac{12}{5}$

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\sin (A+B) & =\sin A \cos B+\cos A \sin B \\
& =\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)\left(\frac{5}{13}\right)+\left(-\frac{3}{5}\right)\left(\frac{12}{13}\right) \\
& =-\frac{16}{65}
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\cos (A-B) & =\cos A \cos B+\sin A \sin B \\
& =\left(-\frac{3}{5}\right)\left(\frac{5}{13}\right)+\left(\frac{4}{5}\right)\left(\frac{12}{13}\right) \\
& =\frac{33}{65}
\end{align}
$$

**c** $\tan (A-B)=\frac{\tan A-\tan B}{1+\tan A \tan B}$

$$
\begin{align}
& =\frac{\left(-\frac{4}{3}\right)-\left(\frac{12}{5}\right)}{1+\left(-\frac{4}{3}\right)\left(\frac{12}{5}\right)} \\
& =\frac{56}{33}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.5

Solve the equation $\sin \left(60^{\circ}-x\right)=2 \sin x$ for $0^{\circ}<x<360^{\circ}$.

Answer

| $\sin \left(60^{\circ}-x\right)=2 \sin x$ | Expand $\sin \left(60^{\circ}-x\right)$. |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\sin 60^{\circ} \cos x-\cos 60^{\circ} \sin x=2 \sin x$ | Use $\sin 60^{\circ}=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}$ and $\cos 60^{\circ}=\frac{1}{2}$. |
| $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} \cos x-\frac{1}{2} \sin x=2 \sin x$ | Multiply both sides by 2. |
| $\sqrt{3} \cos x-\sin x=4 \sin x$ | Add $\sin x$ to both sides. |
| $\sqrt{3} \cos x=5 \sin x$ | Rearrange to find $\tan x$. |
| $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{5}=\tan x$ |  |
| $x=19.1^{\circ}$ or $x=180^{\circ}+19.1^{\circ}$ |  |

The values of $x$ are 19.1° and 199.1°.

### 3.3 Double angle formulae

We can use the compound angle formulae to derive three new identities.

Consider

$$
\sin (A+B) \equiv \sin A \cos B+\cos A \sin B
$$

If $B=A$,

$$
\begin{align}
\sin (A+A) & \equiv \sin A \cos A+\cos A \sin A \\
\therefore \sin 2 A & \equiv 2 \sin A \cos A
\end{align}
$$

Consider

$$
\cos (A+B) \equiv \cos A \cos B-\sin A \sin B
$$

If $B=A$,

$$
\begin{align}
\cos (A+A) & \equiv \cos A \cos A-\sin A \sin A \\
\therefore \cos 2 A & \equiv \cos ^{2} A-\sin ^{2} A
\end{align}
$$

Consider

$$
\tan (A+B) \equiv \frac{\tan A+\tan B}{1-\tan A \tan B}
$$

If $B=A$,

$$
\begin{align}
\tan (A+A) & \equiv \frac{\tan A+\tan A}{1-\tan A \tan A} \\
\therefore \tan 2 A & \equiv \frac{2 \tan A}{1-\tan ^{2} A}
\end{align}
$$

These three formulae are known as the double angle formulae. We can use the identity $\sin ^{2} A+\cos ^{2} A \equiv 1$ to write the identity $\cos 2 A \equiv \cos ^{2} A-\sin ^{2} A$ in two alternative forms:

Using $\sin ^{2} A \equiv 1-\cos ^{2} A$ gives: $\cos 2 A \equiv 2 \cos ^{2} A-1$ Using $\cos ^{2} A \equiv 1-\sin ^{2} A$ gives: $\cos 2 A \equiv 1-2 \sin ^{2} A$ Summarising, the double angle formulae are:

KEY POINT 3.4

$$
\sin 2 A \equiv 2 \sin A \cos A
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\cos 2 A & \equiv \cos ^{2} A-\sin ^{2} A \quad \tan 2 A \equiv \frac{2 \tan A}{1-\tan ^{2} A} \\
& \equiv 2 \cos ^{2} A-1 \\
& \equiv 1-2 \sin ^{2} A
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.6

Given that $\sin x=-\frac{3}{5}$ and that $180^{\circ}<x<270^{\circ}$, find the exact value of:

**a** $\sin 2 x$

**b** $\cos 2 x$

**c** $\tan \frac{x}{2}$

Answer

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-062_340_350_1717_224.jpg)

From the diagram:

$\sin x=-\frac{3}{5}, \cos x=-\frac{4}{5}$ and $\tan x=\frac{3}{4}$

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\sin 2 x & =2 \sin x \cos x \\
& =2\left(-\frac{3}{5}\right)\left(-\frac{4}{5}\right)=\frac{24}{25}
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\cos 2 x & =\cos ^{2} x-\sin ^{2} x \\
& =\left(-\frac{4}{5}\right)^{2}-\left(-\frac{3}{5}\right)^{2}=\frac{7}{25}
\end{align}
$$

**c** $\tan x=\frac{2 \tan \frac{x}{2}}{1-\tan ^{2} \frac{x}{2}}$ Use $2 A=x$ in the double angle formula.

$$
\begin{align}
& \frac{3}{4}=\frac{2 \tan \frac{x}{2}}{1-\tan ^{2} \frac{x}{2}} \\
& 3 \tan ^{2} \frac{x}{2}+8 \tan \frac{x}{2}-3=0 \\
& \left(3 \tan \frac{x}{2}-1\right)\left(\tan \frac{x}{2}+3\right)=0 \\
& \quad \tan \frac{x}{2}=\frac{1}{3} \text { or } \tan \frac{x}{2}=-3
\end{align}
$$

If $180^{\circ}<x<270^{\circ}$, then $90^{\circ}<\frac{x}{2}<135^{\circ}$, which means that $\frac{x}{2}$ is in the second quadrant.

$$
\therefore \tan \frac{x}{2}=-3
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.7

Solve the equation $\sin 2 x=\sin x$ for $0^{\circ} \leqslant x \leqslant 360^{\circ}$.

Answer

| $\sin 2 x=\sin x$ | Use $\sin 2 x=2 \sin x \cos x$. |
| :--- | :--- |
| $2 \sin x \cos x=\sin x$ | Rearrange. |
| $2 \sin x \cos x-\sin x=0$ | Factorise. |

$$
\begin{align}
& \quad \sin x(2 \cos x-1)=0 \\
& \sin x=0 \text { or } \cos x=\frac{1}{2} \\
& \sin x=0 \Rightarrow x=0^{\circ}, 180^{\circ}, 360^{\circ} \\
& \cos x=\frac{1}{2} \Rightarrow x=60^{\circ} \\
& \text { or } x=360^{\circ}-60^{\circ}=300^{\circ} \\
& \text { The values of } x \text { are } 0^{\circ}, 60^{\circ}, 180^{\circ}, 300^{\circ}, 360^{\circ} .
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-063_424_819_1854_1149.jpg)

When solving equations involving $\cos 2 x$ it is important that we choose the most suitable expansion of $\cos 2 x$ for solving that particular equation.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.8

Solve the equation $\cos 2 x+3 \sin x=2$ for $0 \leqslant x \leqslant 2 \pi$.

Answer

$\cos 2 x+3 \sin x=2 \qquad \cup$ Use $\cos 2 x=1-2 \sin ^{2} x$.

$1-2 \sin ^{2} x+3 \sin x=2$ Rearrange.

$2 \sin ^{2} x-3 \sin x+1=0$ Factorise.

$(2 \sin x-1)(\sin x-1)=0$

$\sin x=\frac{1}{2}$ or $\sin x=1$

$\sin x=\frac{1}{2} \Rightarrow x=\frac{\pi}{6}$

or $x=\pi-\frac{\pi}{6}=\frac{5 \pi}{6}$

$\sin x=1 \Rightarrow x=\frac{\pi}{2}$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-064_421_809_786_1112.jpg)

The values of $x$ are $\frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{5 \pi}{6}$.

### 3.4 Further trigonometric identities

This section builds on our previous work by using the compound angle formulae and the double angle formulae to prove trigonometric identities.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.9

Prove the identity $\tan 3 x \equiv \frac{3 \tan x-\tan ^{3} x}{1-3 \tan ^{2} x}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\mathrm{LHS} & \equiv \tan (2 x+x) \\
& \equiv \frac{\tan 2 x+\tan x}{1-\tan 2 x \tan x} \\
& \equiv \frac{\frac{2 \tan x}{1-\tan ^{2} x}+\tan x}{1-\tan x\left(\frac{2 \tan x}{1-\tan ^{2} x}\right)} \\
& \equiv \frac{2 \tan x+\tan x\left(1-\tan ^{2} x\right)}{1-\tan ^{2} x-2 \tan ^{2} x} \\
& \equiv \frac{3 \tan x-\tan ^{3} x}{1-3 \tan ^{2} x} \\
& \equiv \mathrm{RHS}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.10

Prove the identity $\frac{2 \sin (x-y)}{\cos (x-y)-\cos (x+y)} \equiv \cot y-\cot x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\mathrm{LHS} & \equiv \frac{2 \sin (x-y)}{\cos (x-y)-\cos (x+y)} \\
& \equiv \frac{2 \sin x \cos y-2 \cos x \sin y}{(\cos x \cos y+\sin x \sin y)-(\cos x \cos y-\sin x \sin y)} \qquad \text {. Simplify denominator. } \\
& \equiv \frac{2 \sin x \cos y-2 \cos x \sin y}{2 \sin x \sin y} \quad \text { Separate fractions. } \\
& \equiv \frac{2 \sin x \cos y}{2 \sin x \sin y}-\frac{2 \cos x \sin y}{2 \sin x \sin y} \\
& \equiv \frac{\cos y}{\sin y}-\frac{\cos x}{\sin x} \\
& \equiv \cot y-\cot x \\
& \equiv \text { RHS }
\end{align}
$$

### 3.5 Expressing $\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }} \theta+\boldsymbol{b} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }} \theta$ in the form $\boldsymbol{R} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }}(\theta \pm \boldsymbol{\alpha})$ or $\boldsymbol{R} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }}(\theta \pm \boldsymbol{\alpha})$

In this section you will learn how to solve equations of the form $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta=c$.

EXPLORE 3.3

Use graphing software to confirm that the graph of $y=3 \sin \theta+4 \cos \theta$ for $-90^{\circ} \leqslant \theta \leqslant 360^{\circ}$ is:

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-068_255_653_627_757.jpg)

**1** What are the amplitude and period of this graph?

**2** Discuss with your classmates how this graph can be obtained by transforming the graph of:

**a** $y=\sin \theta$

**b** $y=\cos \theta$

**3** Use your knowledge of transformations of functions to write the function $y=3 \sin \theta+4 \cos \theta$ in the form:

**a** $y=R \sin (\theta+\alpha)$

**b** $R \sin (\theta+\beta)$

(You will need to use your graph to find the approximate values of $\alpha$ and $\beta$.)

The formula for replacing $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta$ by $R \sin (\theta+\alpha)$ is derived as follows.

Let $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta=R \sin (\theta+\alpha)$ where $R>0$ and $0^{\circ}<\alpha<90^{\circ}$.

$$
\begin{align}
R \sin (\theta+\alpha) & =R(\sin \theta \cos \alpha+\cos \theta \sin \alpha) \\
\therefore a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta & =R \sin \theta \cos \alpha+R \cos \theta \sin \alpha
\end{align}
$$

Equating coefficients of $\sin \theta: \quad R \cos \alpha=a$

(1)

Equating coefficients of $\cos \theta: \quad R \sin \alpha=b$

(2)

(2) ÷ (1): $\frac{\sin \alpha}{\cos \alpha}=\frac{b}{a} \Rightarrow \tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$ Squaring equations (1) and (2) and then adding gives:

$$
\begin{align}
a^{2}+b^{2} & =R^{2} \cos ^{2} \alpha+R^{2} \sin ^{2} \alpha \\
& =R^{2}\left(\cos ^{2} \alpha+\sin ^{2} \alpha\right) \\
& =R^{2}
\end{align}\quad \begin{array}{r} \text { Use } \cos ^{2} \alpha+\sin ^{2} \alpha=1 \end{array}
$$

Hence, $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$

$\therefore a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta$ can be written as $R \sin (\theta+\alpha)$ where $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and $\tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$

EXPLORE 3.4

Using a similar approach, show that:

$1 a \sin \theta-b \cos \theta \equiv R \sin (\theta-\alpha)$ where $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and $\tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$ $2 a \cos \theta+b \sin \theta \equiv R \cos (\theta-\alpha)$ where $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and $\tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$ $3 a \cos \theta-b \sin \theta \equiv R \cos (\theta+\alpha)$ where $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and $\tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$

Summarising the results from Explore 3.4:

KEY POINT 3.5

$a \sin \theta \pm b \cos \theta \equiv R \sin (\theta \pm \alpha)$ and $a \cos \theta \pm b \sin \theta \equiv R \cos (\theta \mp \alpha)$ where $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}, \tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$ and $0^{\circ}<\alpha<90^{\circ}$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.11

**a** Express $2 \sin \theta-3 \cos \theta$ in the form $R \sin (\theta-\alpha)$, where $R>0$ and $0^{\circ}<\alpha<90^{\circ}$.

Give the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places.

**b** Hence solve the equation $2 \sin \theta-3 \cos \theta=\sqrt{2}$ for $0^{\circ}<\theta<360^{\circ}$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
2 \sin \theta-3 \cos \theta & =R \sin (\theta-\alpha) \\
\therefore 2 \sin \theta-3 \cos \theta & =R \sin \theta \cos \alpha-R \cos \theta \sin \alpha
\end{align}
$$

Equating coefficients of $\sin \theta: R \cos \alpha=2$

(1)

Equating coefficients of $\cos \theta: R \sin \alpha=3$

(2)

(2) $\div(1): \tan \alpha=\frac{3}{2} \Rightarrow \alpha=56.31^{\circ}$ Squaring equations (1) and (2) and then adding gives:

$$
3^{2}+2^{2}=R^{2} \Rightarrow R=\sqrt{13}
$$

$\therefore 2 \sin \theta-3 \cos \theta=\sqrt{13} \sin \left(\theta-56.31^{\circ}\right)$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
2 \sin \theta-3 \cos \theta & =\sqrt{2} \\
\sqrt{13} \sin \left(\theta-56.31^{\circ}\right) & =\sqrt{2}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\sin \left(\theta-56.31^{\circ}\right)=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{13}}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\theta-56.31^{\circ} & =23.09^{\circ}, 180^{\circ}-23.09^{\circ} \\
& =23.09^{\circ}, 156.91^{\circ} \\
\theta & =79.4^{\circ}, 213.2^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

We can find the maximum and minimum values of the expression $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta$ by writing the expression in the form $R \sin (\theta+\alpha)$.

From the Pure Mathematics Coursebook 1, Chapter 5, Section 5.4, we know that $-1 \leqslant \sin (\theta+\alpha) \leqslant 1$, hence $-R \leqslant R \sin (\theta+\alpha) \leqslant R$.

This can be summarised as:

KEY POINT 3.6

The maximum value of $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta$ is $\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and occurs when $\sin (\theta+\alpha)=1$.

The minimum value of $a \sin \theta+b \cos \theta$ is $-\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and occurs when $\sin (\theta+\alpha)=-1$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 3.12

Express $2 \cos \theta-\sin \theta$ in the form $R \cos (\theta+\alpha)$, where $R>0$ and $0^{\circ}<\alpha<90^{\circ}$.

Hence, find the maximum and minimum values of the expression $2 \cos \theta-\sin \theta$ and the values of $\theta$ in the interval $0^{\circ}<\theta<360^{\circ}$ for which these occur.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
2 \cos \theta-\sin \theta & =R \cos (\theta+\alpha) \\
\therefore 2 \cos \theta-\sin \theta & =R \cos \theta \cos \alpha-R \sin \theta \sin \alpha
\end{align}
$$

Equating coefficients of $\cos \theta: R \cos \alpha=2$

(1)

Equating coefficients of $\sin \theta: R \sin \alpha=1$

(2)

(2) ÷ (1): $\tan \alpha=\frac{1}{2} \Rightarrow \alpha=26.57^{\circ}$

Squaring equations (1) and (2) and then adding gives:

$$
2^{2}+1^{2}=R^{2} \Rightarrow R=\sqrt{5}
$$

$\therefore 2 \cos \theta-\sin \theta=\sqrt{5} \cos \left(\theta+26.57^{\circ}\right)$ Maximum value is $\sqrt{2^{2}+(-1)^{2}}$ and occurs when $\cos \left(\theta+26.57^{\circ}\right)=1$

$$
\begin{align}
\theta+26.57^{\circ} & =360^{\circ} \\
\theta & =333.4^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

Minimum value is $-\sqrt{2^{2}+(-1)^{2}}$ and occurs when $\cos \left(\theta+26.57^{\circ}\right)=-1$

$$
\begin{align}
\theta+26.57^{\circ} & =180^{\circ} \\
\theta & =153.4^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

∴ Max value $=\sqrt{5}$ when $\theta=333.4^{\circ}$, min value $=-\sqrt{5}$ when $\theta=153.4^{\circ}$

DID YOU KNOW?

The French mathematician Joseph Fourier (1768-1830) found a way to represent any wave-like function as the sum (possibly infinite) of simple sine waves. The sum of sine functions is called

**a** Fourier series. These series can be applied to vibration problems. Fourier is also credited with discovering the greenhouse effect.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-070_148_885_2494_428.jpg)

## Checklist of learning and understanding

Cosecant, secant and cotangent

$$
\operatorname{cosec} \theta=\frac{1}{\sin \theta}
$$

$$
\sec \theta=\frac{1}{\cos \theta}
$$

$$
\cot \theta=\frac{1}{\tan \theta}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-073_458_577_502_218.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-073_457_571_502_822.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-073_460_576_502_1421.jpg)

Trigonometric identities

- $1+\tan ^{2} x \equiv \sec ^{2} x$

- $1+\cot ^{2} x \equiv \operatorname{cosec}^{2} x$

Compound angle formula

- $\sin (A+B) \equiv \sin A \cos B+\cos A \sin B$

- $\sin (A-B) \equiv \sin A \cos B-\cos A \sin B$

- $\cos (A+B) \equiv \cos A \cos B-\sin A \sin B$

- $\cos (A-B) \equiv \cos A \cos B+\sin A \sin B$

- $\tan (A+B) \equiv \frac{\tan A+\tan B}{1-\tan A \tan B}$

- $\tan (A-B) \equiv \frac{\tan A-\tan B}{1+\tan A \tan B}$

Double angle formulae

- $\sin 2 A \equiv 2 \sin A \cos A$

-

$$
\begin{align}
\cos 2 A & \equiv \cos ^{2} A-\sin ^{2} A \\
& \equiv 1-2 \sin ^{2} A \\
& \equiv 2 \cos ^{2} A-1
\end{align}
$$

- $\tan 2 A \equiv \frac{2 \tan A}{1-\tan ^{2} A}$

Expressing $\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }} \boldsymbol{\theta}+\boldsymbol{b} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }} \boldsymbol{\theta}$ in the form $\boldsymbol{R} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }}(\boldsymbol{\theta} \pm \boldsymbol{\alpha})$ or $\boldsymbol{R} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }}(\boldsymbol{\theta} \pm \boldsymbol{\alpha})$

- $a \sin \theta \pm b \cos \theta=R \sin (\theta \pm \alpha)$

- $a \cos \theta \pm b \sin \theta=R \cos (\theta \mp \alpha)$

where $R=\sqrt{a^{2}+b^{2}}$ and $\tan \alpha=\frac{b}{a}$
