## Chapter 4 <br> Differentiation

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- differentiate products and quotients

- use the derivatives of $\mathrm{e}^{x}, \ln x, \sin x, \cos x, \tan x$, together with constant multiples, sums, differences and composites

- find and use the first derivative of a function, which is defined parametrically or implicitly.

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 7 | Differentiate $x^{n}$ together with constant multiples, sums and differences. | 1 Differentiate with respect to $x$.<br>&#160;&#160;a $y=5 x^{3}-\frac{3}{x^{2}}+2 \sqrt{x}$<br>&#160;&#160;b $y=\frac{x^{8}-4 x^{5}+x^{2}}{2 x^{3}}$<br> |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 7 | Differentiate composite functions using the chain rule. | 2 Differentiate with respect to $x$.<br>&#160;&#160;a $(3 x-5)^{4}$<br>&#160;&#160;b $\frac{4}{\sqrt{1-2 x}}$<br> |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 7 | Find tangents and normals to curves. | 3 Find the equation of the normal to the curve $y=x^{3}-5 x^{2}+2 x-1$ at the point (1, -3). |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 8 | Find stationary points on curves and determine their nature. | 4 Find the stationary points on the curve $y=x^{3}-3 x^{2}+2$ and determine their nature. |

## Why do we study differentiation?

In this chapter we will learn how to differentiate the product and the quotient of two simple functions. We will also learn how to find and use the derivatives of exponential, logarithmic and trigonometric functions.

All of the functions that we have differentiated so far have been of the form $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$. In this chapter we will also learn how to differentiate functions that cannot be written in the form $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$. For example, the function $y^{2}+2 x y=4$.

Lastly, we will learn how to find and use the derivative of a function where the variables $x$ and $y$ are given as a function of a third variable.

Although these topics might seem like they are just for Pure Mathematics problems, differentiation is used for calculations to do with quantum mechanics and field theories in Physics.

### 4.1 The product rule

The function $y=(x+1)^{4}(3 x-2)^{3}$ can be considered as the product of two separate functions:

$$
y=u v \text { where } u=(x+1)^{4} \text { and } v=(3 x-2)^{3}
$$

To differentiate the product of two functions we can use the product rule.

KEY POINT 4.1

The product rule is:

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(u v)=u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}+v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}
$$

Some people find it easier to remember this rule as:

'(first function × derivative of second function) + (second function × derivative of first function)' So for $y=(x+1)^{4}(3 x-2)^{3}$,

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\underbrace{(x+1)^{4}}_{\text {first }} \underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(3 x-2)^{3}}_{\text {differentiate second }}+\underbrace{(3 x-2)^{3}}_{\text {second }} \underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(x+1)^{4}}_{\text {differentiate first }} \\
& =(x+1)^{4} \times 9(3 x-2)^{2}+(3 x-2)^{3} \times 4(x+1)^{3} \\
& =(x+1)^{3}(3 x-2)^{2}[9(x+1)+4(3 x-2)] \\
& =(x+1)^{3}(3 x-2)^{2}(21 x+1)
\end{align}
$$

## The product rule from first principles

Consider the function $y=u v$ where $u$ and $v$ are functions of $x$.

A small increase $\delta x$ in $x$ leads to corresponding small increases $\delta y, \delta u$ and $\delta v$ in $y, u$ and $v$.

$$
\begin{array}{rlrl} y+\delta y & =(u+\delta u)(v+\delta v) & & \text { Expand brackets and replace } y \text { with } u v . \\
u v+\delta y & =u v+u \delta v+v \delta u+\delta u \delta v & & \text { Subtract } u v \text { from both sides. } \\
\delta y & =u \delta v+v \delta u+\delta u \delta v & & \text { Divide both sides by } \delta x . \\
\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} & =u \frac{\delta v}{\delta x}+v \frac{\delta u}{\delta x}+\delta u \frac{\delta v}{\delta x}----------(1) \tag{1} \end{array}
$$

As $\delta x \rightarrow 0$, then so do $\delta y, \delta u$ and $\delta v$ and

$$
\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} \rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}, \frac{\delta u}{\delta x} \rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \text { and } \frac{\delta v}{\delta x} \rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} .
$$

Equation (1) becomes:

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}+v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}+0 \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \\
\therefore \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}+v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.1

Find $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ when $y=(2 x-1) \sqrt{4 x+5}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
y & =(2 x-1)(4 x+5)^{\frac{1}{2}} \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\underbrace{(2 x-1)}_{\text {first }} \underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[(4 x+5)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]}_{\text {differentiate second }}+\underbrace{(4 x+5)^{\frac{1}{2}}}_{\text {second }} \underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 x-1)}_{\text {differentiate first }}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& =(2 x-1) \underbrace{\left[\frac{1}{2}(4 x+5)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(4)\right]}_{\text {Chain rule. }}+(\sqrt{4 x+5})(2) \\
& =\frac{2(2 x-1)}{\sqrt{4 x+5}}+2 \sqrt{4 x+5} \\
& =\frac{2(2 x-1)+2(4 x+5)}{\sqrt{4 x+5}} \\
& =\frac{12 x+8}{\sqrt{4 x+5}}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.2

Find the $x$-coordinate of the points on the curve $y=(2 x-3)^{2}(x+5)^{3}$ where the gradient is 0.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
y & =(2 x-3)^{2}(x+5)^{3} \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\underbrace{(2 x-3)^{2}}_{\text {first }} \underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[(x+5)^{3}\right]}_{\text {differentiate second }}+\underbrace{(x+5)^{3}}_{\text {second }} \underbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[(2 x-3)^{2}\right]}_{\text {differentiate first }} \\
& =(2 x-3)^{2} \underbrace{\left[3(x+5)^{2}(1)\right]}_{\text {Chain rule }}+(x+5)^{3} \underbrace{\left[2(2 x-3)^{1}(2)\right]}_{\text {Chain rule }} \\
& =3(2 x-3)^{2}(x+5)^{2}+4(2 x-3)(x+5)^{3} \qquad \text {. Factorise. } \\
& =(2 x-3)(x+5)^{2}[3(2 x-3)+4(x+5)] \qquad \text { Simplify. } \\
& =(2 x-3)(x+5)^{2}(10 x+11)
\end{align}
$$

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=0 \text { when }(2 x-3)(x+5)^{2}(10 x+11)=0
$$

$$
\begin{array}{rlrlrl} 2 x-3 & =0 & x+5 & =0 & 10 x+11 & =0 \\
x & =\frac{3}{2} & x & =-5 & x & =-1.1 \end{array}
$$

### 4.2 The quotient rule

We can differentiate the function $y=\frac{x^{2}-5}{2 x+1}$ by writing the function in the form $y=\left(x^{2}-5\right)(2 x+1)^{-1}$ and then by applying the product rule.

Alternatively, we can consider $y=\frac{x^{2}-5}{2 x+1}$ as the division (quotient) of two separate functions:

$$
y=\frac{u}{v} \text { where } u=x^{2}-5 \text { and } v=2 x+1 .
$$

To differentiate the quotient of two functions we can use the quotient rule.

## KEY POINT 4.2

The quotient rule is:

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)=\frac{v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}-u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}}{v^{2}}
$$

Some people find it easier to remember this rule as

$$
\frac{(\text { denominator } \times \text { derivative of numerator })-(\text { numerator } \times \text { derivative of denominator })}{(\text { denominator })^{2}}
$$

## The quotient rule from first principles

Consider the function $y=\frac{u}{v}$ where $u$ and $v$ are functions of $x$.

A small increase $\delta x$ in $x$ leads to corresponding small increases $\delta y, \delta u$ and $\delta v$ in $y, u$ and $v$.

$$
\begin{align}
y+\delta y & =\frac{u+\delta u}{v+\delta v} & & \text { Subtract } y \text { from both sides. } \\
\delta y & =\frac{u+\delta u}{v+\delta v}-y & & \text { Replace } y \text { with } \frac{u}{v} . \\
\delta y & =\frac{u+\delta u}{v+\delta v}-\frac{u}{v} & & \text { Combine fractions. } \\
\delta y & =\frac{v(u+\delta u)-u(v+\delta v)}{v(v+\delta v)} & & \text { Expand brackets and simplify. } \\
\delta y & =\frac{v \delta u-u \delta v}{v^{2}+v \delta v} & & \text { Divide both sides by } \delta x . \\
\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} & =\frac{v \frac{\delta u}{\delta x}-u \frac{\delta v}{v^{2}+v \delta v}}{} & & \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

As $\delta x \rightarrow 0$, then so do $\delta y, \delta u$ and $\delta v$ and $\frac{\delta y}{\delta x} \rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}, \frac{\delta u}{\delta x} \rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ and $\frac{\delta v}{\delta x} \rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}$.

Equation (1) becomes

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)=\frac{v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}-u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}}{v^{2}}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.3

Find the derivative of $y=\frac{x^{2}-5}{2 x+1}$.

Answer

$$
y=\frac{x^{2}-5}{2 x+1}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{\overbrace{(2 x+1)}^{\text {denominator }} \times \overbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{2}-5\right)}^{\text {differentiate numerator }}-\left(x^{2}-5\right) \times \overbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 x+1)}^{\text {numerator }}}{\underbrace{(2 x+1)^{2}}_{\text {denominator squared }}} \\
& =\frac{(2 x+1)(2 x)-\left(x^{2}-5\right)(2)}{(2 x+1)^{2}} \\
& =\frac{4 x^{2}+2 x-2 x^{2}+10}{(2 x+1)^{2}} \\
& =\frac{2\left(x^{2}+x+5\right)}{(2 x+1)^{2}}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.4

Find the derivative of $y=\frac{(x+2)^{3}}{\sqrt{x-1}}$.

Answer

$$
y=\frac{(x+2)^{3}}{\sqrt{x-1}}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{\overbrace{\sqrt{x-1}}^{\text {denominator }} \times \overbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[(x+2)^{3}\right]}^{\text {differentiate numerator }}-(x+2)^{3}}{\underbrace{(\sqrt{x-1})^{2}}_{\text {denominator squared }} \times \overbrace{\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\sqrt{x-1}]}^{\text {numerator }}} \\
& =\frac{(\sqrt{x-1})\left[3(x+2)^{2}(1)\right]-(x+2)^{3}\left[\frac{1}{2}(x-1)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1)\right]}{x-1} \\
& =\frac{3(x+2)^{2} \sqrt{x-1}-\frac{(x+2)^{3}}{2 \sqrt{x-1}}}{x-1} \\
& =\frac{6(x+2)^{2}(x-1)-(x+2)^{3}}{2(x-1) \sqrt{x-1}} \\
& =\frac{(x+2)^{2}[6(x-1)-(x+2)]}{2(x-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}} \\
& =\frac{(x+2)^{2}(5 x-8)}{2(x-1)^{\frac{3}{2}}}
\end{align}
$$

## EXPLORE 4.1

In Worked examples 4.3 and 4.4 we differentiated the functions $y=\frac{x^{2}-5}{2 x+1}$ and $y=\frac{(x+2)^{3}}{\sqrt{x-1}}$ using the quotient rule. Now write each of these functions as a product, such as $y=\left(x^{2}-5\right)(2 x+1)^{-1}$, and then use the product rule to differentiate them.

Do you obtain the same answers?

Discuss with your classmates which method you prefer for functions such as these: the quotient rule or the product rule.

### 4.3 Derivatives of exponential functions

## The derivative of $\mathbf{e}^{\boldsymbol{x}}$

In Chapter 2, we learnt about the natural exponential function $\mathrm{f}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x}$. This function has a very special property. If we use graphing software to draw the graph of $\mathrm{f}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ together with the graph of its gradient function we find that the two curves are identical.

## KEY POINT 4.3

Hence, we have the rule:

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\mathrm{e}^{x}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{x}
$$

An explanation of how this rule can be obtained is as follows.

Consider the function $\mathrm{f}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x}$ and two points whose $x$-coordinates are $x$ and $x+\delta x$ where $\delta x$ is a small increase in $x$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-088_246_466_464_637.jpg)

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\lim _{\delta x \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathrm{f}(x+\delta x)-\mathrm{f}(x)}{(x+\delta x)-x}=\lim _{\delta x \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{x+\delta x}-\mathrm{e}^{x}}{\delta x}=\lim _{\delta x \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{x}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\delta x}-1\right)}{\delta x}
$$

Now consider $\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\delta x}-1}{\delta x}$ for small values of $\delta x$.

| $\delta x$ | 0.1 | 0.01 | 0.001 | 0.0001 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $\frac{\mathrm{e}^{\delta x}-1}{\delta x}$ | 1.051709 | 1.005017 | 1.000500 | 1.000050 |

From the table, we can see that as $\delta x \rightarrow 0, \frac{\mathrm{e}^{\delta x}-1}{\delta x} \rightarrow 1$.

$$
\therefore \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{e}^{x}
$$

The derivative of $\mathbf{e}^{\mathbf{f}(x)}$

Consider the function $y=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{f}(x)}$.

Let $y=\mathrm{e}^{u} \quad$ where $\quad u=\mathrm{f}(x)$

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u}=\mathrm{e}^{u} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)
$$

Using the chain rule:

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u} \times \frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \\
& =\mathrm{e}^{u} \times \mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x) \\
& =\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x) \times \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{f}(x)} \\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{f}(x)}\right] & =\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x) \times \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{f}(x)}
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 4.4

In particular,

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\mathrm{e}^{a x+b}\right]=a \mathrm{e}^{a x+b}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.5

Differentiate with respect to $x$.

**a** $\mathrm{e}^{2 x}$

**b** $x \mathrm{e}^{-5 x}$

**c** $\frac{\mathrm{e}^{3 x}}{x^{2}}$

Answer

**a** $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\left(\mathrm{e}^{2 x}\right)=\underbrace{2}_{\text {differentiate index }} \times \underbrace{\mathrm{e}^{2 x}}_{\text {original function }}=2 \mathrm{e}^{2 x}$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x \mathrm{e}^{-5 x}\right) & =x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-5 x}\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-5 x} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(x) \qquad \text { Product rule. } \\
& =x \times\left(-5 \mathrm{e}^{-5 x}\right)+\mathrm{e}^{-5 x} \times(1) \\
& =-5 x \mathrm{e}^{-5 x}+\mathrm{e}^{-5 x} \\
& =\mathrm{e}^{-5 x}(1-5 x)
\end{align}
$$

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{3 x}}{x^{2}}\right) & =\frac{x^{2} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\mathrm{e}^{3 x}\right)-\mathrm{e}^{3 x} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{2}\right)}{x^{4}} \\
& =\frac{x^{2} \times 3 \mathrm{e}^{3 x}-\mathrm{e}^{3 x} \times 2 x}{x^{4}} \\
& =\frac{\mathrm{e}^{3 x}(3 x-2)}{x^{3}}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.6

The equation of a curve is $y=\mathrm{e}^{2 x}-9 \mathrm{e}^{x}+7 x$. Find the exact value of the $x$-coordinate at each of the stationary points and determine the nature of each stationary point.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
y & =\mathrm{e}^{2 x}-9 \mathrm{e}^{x}+7 x \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =2 \mathrm{e}^{2 x}-9 \mathrm{e}^{x}+7 \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d}^{2} y}{\mathrm{~d} x^{2}} & =4 \mathrm{e}^{2 x}-9 \mathrm{e}^{x}
\end{align}
$$

Stationary points occur when $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=0$.

$$
\begin{align}
2 \mathrm{e}^{2 x}-9 \mathrm{e}^{x}+7 & =0 \\
\left(2 \mathrm{e}^{x}-7\right)\left(\mathrm{e}^{x}-1\right) & =0 \\
\left(2 \mathrm{e}^{x}-7\right)=0 \text { or }\left(\mathrm{e}^{x}-1\right) & =0 \\
\mathrm{e}^{x}=\frac{7}{2} \text { or } \mathrm{e}^{x} & =1 \\
x=\ln \left(\frac{7}{2}\right) \text { or } x & =0
\end{align}
$$

Factorise.

When $x=0, \frac{\mathrm{~d}^{2} y}{\mathrm{~d} x^{2}}$ is negative ⇒ maximum point.

When $x=\ln \left(\frac{7}{2}\right), \frac{\mathrm{d}^{2} y}{\mathrm{~d} x^{2}}$ is positive ⇒ minimum point.

### 4.4 Derivatives of natural logarithmic functions

EXPLORE 4.2

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-091_592_740_1122_539.jpg)

Here is a sketch of the graph of $y=\ln x$. Can you sketch a graph of its derivative (gradient function)? Discuss your sketch with your classmates.

Now sketch the graphs using graphing software. Can you suggest a formula for the derivative of $\ln x$ ? (It might be helpful to look at the coordinates of some of the points on the graph of the derivative.)

## The derivative of $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { l n }} \boldsymbol{x}$

In Chapter 2, you learnt that if $y=\ln x$, then $\mathrm{e}^{y}=x$.

In Section 4.3, you learnt that if $y=\mathrm{e}^{x}$, then $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{e}^{x}$.

Using these two results we can find the rule for differentiating $\ln x$.

$$
\begin{align}
y & =\ln x & & \\
\mathrm{e}^{y} & =x & & \text { Differentiate both sides with respect to } y . \\
\mathrm{e}^{y} & =\frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} y} & & \text { Rearrange and replace } \mathrm{e}^{y} \text { by } x . \\
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{1}{x} & &
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 4.5

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln x)=\frac{1}{x}
$$

The derivative of $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { l n }} \mathbf{f}(\boldsymbol{x})$ Consider the function $y=\ln \mathrm{f}(x)$.

Let $y=\ln u \quad$ where $\quad u=\mathrm{f}(x)$

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u}=\frac{1}{u} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)
$$

Using the chain rule:

$$
\text { ule: }\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u} \times \frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \\
& =\frac{1}{u} \times \mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x) \\
& =\frac{\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)} \\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln \mathrm{f}(x)] & =\frac{\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)}
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 4.6

In particular,

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln (a x+b)]=\frac{a}{a x+b}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.7

Differentiate with respect to $x$.

**a** $\ln 2 x$

**b** $\ln (4 x-3)$

**c** $\quad \ln \sqrt{5-x}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 2 x) & =\frac{2}{2 x} \\
& =\frac{1}{x}
\end{align}
$$

**b** $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}[\ln (4 x-3)]=\frac{4}{4 x-3}$ 'Inside' differentiated.

'Inside'

**c** Method 1:

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln \sqrt{5-x}] & =\frac{\frac{1}{2}(5-x)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(-1)}{\sqrt{5-x}} \\
& =-\frac{1}{2(5-x)}
\end{align}
$$

Method 2: using the rules of logarithms before differentiating.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln \sqrt{5-x}] & =\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\ln (5-x)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right] \\
& =\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\frac{1}{2} \ln (5-x)\right] \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln (5-x)] \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \times \frac{-1}{5-x} \\
& =-\frac{1}{2(5-x)}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.8

Differentiate with respect to $x$.

**a** $2 x^{4} \ln 5 x$

**b** $\frac{\ln 2 x}{x^{3}}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(2 x^{4} \ln 5 x\right) & =2 x^{4} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 5 x)+\ln 5 x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(2 x^{4}\right) \qquad \text { Product rule. } \\
& =2 x^{4} \times \frac{5}{5 x}+\ln 5 x \times 8 x^{3} \\
& =2 x^{3}+8 x^{3} \ln 5 x
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\frac{\ln 2 x}{x^{3}}\right) & =\frac{x^{3} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 2 x)-\ln 2 x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{3}\right)}{\left(x^{3}\right)^{2}} \quad \because \quad \text { Quotient rule. } \\
& =\frac{x^{3} \times \frac{2}{2 x}-\ln 2 x \times 3 x^{2}}{x^{6}} \\
& =\frac{x^{2}-3 x^{2} \ln 2 x}{x^{6}} \\
& =\frac{1-3 \ln 2 x}{x^{4}}
\end{align}
$$

### 4.5 Derivatives of trigonometric functions

EXPLORE 4.3

Graphing software has been used to draw the graphs of $y=\sin x$ and $y=\cos x$ for $0 \leqslant x \leqslant 2 \pi$ together with their gradient (derived) functions.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-095_302_641_901_259.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-095_300_636_904_912.jpg)

Discuss with your classmates why, for the function $y=\sin x$, you would expect the graph of the gradient function to have this shape. Do the same for the graph of $y=\cos x$.

Can you suggest how to complete the following formulae?

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\sin x)=\quad \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\cos x)=
$$

The derivative of $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }} \boldsymbol{x}$ Consider the function $\mathrm{f}(x)=\sin x$, where $x$ is measured in radians, and two points whose $x$-coordinates are $x$ and $x+\delta x$ where $\delta x$ is a small increase in $x$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-095_264_610_1815_263.jpg)

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\lim _{\delta x \rightarrow 0} \frac{\mathrm{f}(x+\delta x)-\mathrm{f}(x)}{(x+\delta x)-x} & & \\
& =\lim _{\delta x \rightarrow 0} \frac{\sin (x+\delta x)-\sin x}{\delta x} & & \text { Expand } \sin (x+\delta x) \\
& =\lim _{\delta x \rightarrow 0} \frac{\sin x \cos \delta x+\cos x \sin \delta x-\sin x}{\delta x} & & \text { As } \delta x \rightarrow 0, \cos \delta x \rightarrow 1 \\
& =\cos x & & \text { and } \sin \delta x \rightarrow \delta x .
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 4.7

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\sin x)=\cos x
$$

Similarly, we can show that

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\cos x)=-\sin x
$$

We can find the derivative of $\tan x$ using these two results together with the quotient rule.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\tan x) & =\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\frac{\sin x}{\cos x}\right) & & \text { Use the quotient rule. } \\
& =\frac{\cos x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\sin x)-\sin x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\cos x)}{(\cos x)^{2}} & & \\
& =\frac{\cos x \times \cos x-\sin x \times(-\sin x)}{\cos ^{2} x} & & \\
& =\frac{\cos ^{2} x+\sin ^{2} x}{\cos ^{2} x} & & \text { Use } \cos ^{2} x+\sin ^{2} x=1 . \\
& =\frac{1}{\cos ^{2} x} & & \text { Use } \frac{1}{\cos x}=\sec x . \\
& =\sec ^{2} x & &
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 4.8

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\tan x)=\sec ^{2} x
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.9

Differentiate with respect to $x$.

**a** $2 \sin x$

**b** $x \cos x$

**c** $\frac{\tan x}{x^{2}}$

**d** $(3+2 \sin x)^{5}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 \sin x) & =2 \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\sin x) \\
& =2 \cos x
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(x \cos x) & =x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\cos x)+\cos x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(x) \\
& =-x \sin x+\cos x
\end{align}
$$

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\frac{\tan x}{x^{2}}\right) & =\frac{x^{2} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\tan x)-\tan x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{2}\right)}{x^{4}} \\
& =\frac{x^{2} \times \sec ^{2} x-\tan x \times 2 x}{x^{4}} \\
& =\frac{x \sec ^{2} x-2 \tan x}{x^{3}}
\end{align}
$$

**d**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[(3+2 \sin x)^{5}\right] & =5(3+2 \sin x)^{4} \times 2 \cos x \\
& =10 \cos x(3+2 \sin x)^{4}
\end{align}
$$

Derivatives of $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }}(\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b}), \boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }}(\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b})$ and $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { t a n }}(\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b})$ Consider the function $y=\sin (a x+b)$ where $x$ is measured in radians.

Let

$$
\begin{align}
y & =\sin u & \text { where } & u \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u} & =\cos u & \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =a
\end{align}
$$

Using the chain rule:

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u} \times \frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \\
& =\cos u \times a \\
& =a \cos (a x+b)
\end{align}
$$

## KEY POINT 4.9

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\sin (a x+b)]=a \cos (a x+b)
$$

Similarly, it can be shown that:

$$
\begin{align}
& \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\cos (a x+b)]=-a \sin (a x+b) \\
& \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\tan (a x+b)]=a \sec ^{2}(a x+b)
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.10

Differentiate with respect to $x$.

**a** $2 \sin 3 x$

**b** $4 x \tan 2 x$

**c** $\frac{\cos \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{x^{2}}$

**d** $(3-2 \cos 5 x)^{4}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 \sin 3 x) & =2 \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\sin 3 x) \\
& =2 \times \cos 3 x \times(3) \\
& =6 \cos 3 x
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(4 x \tan 2 x) & =4 x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\tan 2 x)+\tan 2 x \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(4 x) \\
& =4 x \times \sec ^{2} 2 x(2)+\tan 2 x \times(4) \\
& =8 x \sec ^{2} 2 x+4 \tan 2 x
\end{align}
$$

Product rule.

**c**

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\frac{\cos \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{x^{2}}\right]=\frac{x^{2} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\cos \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)\right]-\cos \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right) \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[x^{2}\right]}{\left(x^{2}\right)^{2}} \qquad \text { Quotient rule. }
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& =\frac{-2 x^{2} \sin \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)-2 x \cos \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{x^{4}} \\
& =\frac{-2 x \sin \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)-2 \cos \left(2 x-\frac{\pi}{4}\right)}{x^{3}}
\end{align}
$$

**d**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[(3-2 \cos 5 x)^{4}\right] & =4(3-2 \cos 5 x)^{3} \times 10 \sin 5 x \\
& =40 \sin 5 x(3-2 \cos 5 x)^{3}
\end{align}
$$

Chain rule.

### 4.6 Implicit differentiation

All the functions that we have differentiated so far have been of the form $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$. These are called explicit functions as $y$ is given explicitly in terms of $x$. However, many functions cannot be expressed in this form, for example $x^{3}+5 x y+y^{3}=16$.

When a function is given as an equation connecting $x$ and $y$, where $y$ is not the subject, it is called an implicit function.

The chain rule $\left(\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} u} \times \frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}\right)$ and the product rule $\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(u v)=u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}+v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}\right)$ are used extensively to differentiate implicit functions.

This is illustrated in the following Worked examples.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.11

Differentiate each of these expressions with respect to $x$.

**a** $y^{3}$

**b** $4 x^{2} y^{5}$

**c** $x^{3}+5 x y$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{3}\right) & =\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} y}\left(y^{3}\right) \times \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \\
& =3 y^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}
\end{align}
$$

Chain rule.

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(4 x^{2} y^{5}\right) & =4 x^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{5}\right)+y^{5} \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(4 x^{2}\right) \qquad \text { Product rule. } \\
& =4 x^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{5}\right)+8 x y^{5} \qquad \text { Chain rule: } \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{5}\right)=5 y^{4} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} . \\
& =20 x^{2} y^{4} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}+8 x y^{5}
\end{align}
$$

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{3}+5 x y\right) & =\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{3}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(5 x y) \\
& =3 x^{2}+5 x \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(y)+y \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(5 x) \\
& =3 x^{2}+5 x \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}+5 y
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.12

Find $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ for the curve $x^{3}+y^{3}=4 x y$. Hence find the gradient of the curve at the point (2, 2).

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
x^{3}+y^{3} & =4 x y \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(x^{3}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{3}\right) & =\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(4 x y) \\
3 x^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} y}\left(y^{3}\right) \times \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =4 x \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(y)+y \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(4 x) \qquad \text { Differentiate with respect to } x . \\
3 x^{2}+3 y^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =4 x \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}+4 y \\
3 y^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}-4 x \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =4 y-3 x^{2} \\
\left(3 y^{2}-4 x\right) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =4 y-3 x^{2} \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{4 y-3 x^{2}}{3 y^{2}-4 x}
\end{align}
$$

When $x=2$ and $y=2$,

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{4(2)-3(2)^{2}}{3(2)^{2}-4(2)}=-1
$$

Gradient of the curve at the point $(2,2)$ is -1.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.13

The equation of a curve is $3 x^{2}+2 x y+y^{2}=6$. Find the coordinates of the two stationary points on the curve.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& 3 x^{2}+2 x y+y^{2}=6 \\
& \text { Differentiate with respect to } x \text {. } \\
& \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(3 x^{2}\right)+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 x y)+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{2}\right)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(6) \\
& \text { Use product rule for } \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 x y) \text {. } \\
& 6 x+2 x \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(y)+y \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(2 x)+\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{2}\right)=0 \\
& \text { Use chain rule for } \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(y^{2}\right) \text {. } \\
& 6 x+2 x \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}+2 y+2 y \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=0 \\
& \text { Divide by } 2 \text { and rearrange. } \\
& x \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}+y \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=-y-3 x \\
& \text { Factorise. } \\
& (x+y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=-y-3 x \\
& \text { Rearrange. } \\
& \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{-y-3 x}{x+y}
\end{align}
$$

Stationary points occur when

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =0 . \\
-y-3 x & =0 \\
y & =-3 x
\end{align}
$$

Substituting $y=-3 x$ into the equation of the curve gives:

$$
\begin{align}
3 x^{2}+2 x(-3 x)+(-3 x)^{2} & =6 \\
6 x^{2} & =6 \\
x & = \pm 1
\end{align}
$$

The stationary points are (-1, 3) and (1, -3).

### 4.7 Parametric differentiation

Sometimes variables $x$ and $y$ are given as a function of a third variable $t$.

For example, $x=1+4 \sin t$ and $y=2 \cos t$.

The variable $t$ is called a parameter and the two equations are called the parametric equations of the curve.

We can find the curve given by the parametric equations $x=1+4 \sin t, y=2 \cos t$ for $0 \leqslant t \leqslant 2 \pi$ by finding the values of $x$ and $y$ for particular values of $t$.

| $t$ | 0 | $\frac{1}{4} \pi$ | $\frac{1}{2} \pi$ | $\frac{3}{4} \pi$ | $\pi$ | $\frac{5}{4} \pi$ | $\frac{3}{2} \pi$ | $\frac{7}{4} \pi$ | $2 \pi$ |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $x$ | 1 | 3.83 | 5 | 3.83 | 1 | -1.83 | -3 | -1.83 | 1 |
| $y$ | 2 | 1.41 | 0 | -1.41 | -2 | -1.41 | 0 | 1.41 | 2 |

Plotting the coordinates on a grid gives the following graph.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-103_436_701_1034_570.jpg)

**a** $x=t^{3}, \quad y=t^{2}$

**b** $x=t^{2}, \quad y=t^{3}-t$

**c** $x=t+\frac{1}{t}, y=t-\frac{1}{t}$

**2** Use graphing software to draw the curves given by these parametric equations.

**a** $x=\sin t, \quad y=\sin 2 t$

**b** $x=\sin 2 t, \quad y=\sin 3 t$

**c** $x=\sin 4 t, \quad y=\sin 3 t$

**3** The curves in question 2 are called Lissajous curves. Use the internet to find out more about these curves. (A Lissajous curve is shown at the start of this chapter.)

When a curve is given in parametric form, in terms of the parameter $t$, we can use the chain rule to find $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ in terms of $t$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.14

The parametric equations of a curve are $x=t^{2}, y=5-2 t$.

**a** Find $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ in terms of the parameter $t$.

**b** Find the equation of the tangent to the curve at the point where $t=2$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{array}{ll} x=t^{2} & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=2 t \\
y=5-2 t & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} t}=-2 \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} t} \times \frac{\mathrm{d} t}{\mathrm{~d} x} \\
& =-2 \times \frac{1}{2 t} \\
& =-\frac{1}{t}
\end{align}
$$

**b** When $t=2$, gradient $=-\frac{1}{2}, x=4$ and $y=1$.

Using $y-y_{1}=m\left(x-x_{1}\right)$

$$
\begin{align}
y-1 & =-\frac{1}{2}(x-4) \\
y & =-\frac{1}{2} x+3
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 4.15

The parametric equations of a curve are $x=1+2 \sin ^{2} \theta, y=4 \tan \theta$ for $\frac{\pi}{2}<\theta<\frac{3 \pi}{2}$.

**a** Find $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ in terms of the parameter $\theta$.

**b** Find the coordinates of the point on the curve where the tangent is parallel to the $y$-axis.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{array}{ll} x=1+2 \sin ^{2} \theta & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} x}{\mathrm{~d} \theta}=4 \sin \theta \cos \theta \\
y=4 \tan \theta \quad \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} \theta}=4 \sec ^{2} \theta=\frac{4}{\cos ^{2} \theta} \end{array}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{d y}{d x} & =\frac{d y}{d \theta} \times \frac{d \theta}{d x} \\
& =\frac{4}{\cos ^{2} \theta} \times \frac{1}{4 \sin \theta \cos \theta} \\
& =\frac{1}{\sin \theta \cos ^{3} \theta}
\end{align}
$$

**b** Tangent is parallel to the $y$-axis when:

$$
\begin{align}
\sin \theta \cos ^{3} \theta & =0 \\
\sin \theta=0 \text { or } \cos \theta & =0 \\
\sin \theta=0 \Rightarrow \theta & =\pi \Rightarrow(1,0)
\end{align}
$$

There are no solutions to $\cos \theta=0$ in the range $\frac{\pi}{2}<\theta<\frac{3 \pi}{2}$.

∴ The point where the tangent is parallel to the $y$-axis is $(1,0)$.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

Product rule

- $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(u v)=u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}+v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}$

Quotient rule

- $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\left(\frac{u}{v}\right)=\frac{v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}-u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}}{v^{2}}$

Exponential functions

- $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\left(\mathrm{e}^{x}\right)=\mathrm{e}^{x} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\mathrm{e}^{a x+b}\right]=a \mathrm{e}^{a x+b} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{f}(x)}\right]=\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x) \times \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{f}(x)}$

Logarithmic functions

- $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(\ln x)=\frac{1}{x} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln (a x+b)]=\frac{a}{a x+b} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln (\mathrm{f}(x))]=\frac{\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)}$

Trigonometric functions

- $\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(\sin x)=\cos x \mathrm{~d} x \mathrm{~s}(a x+b)\right]=a \cos (a x+b)$

- $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(\cos x)=-\sin x \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\cos (a x+b)]=-a \sin (a x+b)$

- $\left.\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(\tan x)=\sec ^{2} x \mathrm{~d} x \mathrm{tan}(a x+b)\right]=a \sec ^{2}(a x+b)$

## Why do we study integration?

In this chapter we will learn how to integrate exponential, logarithmic and trigonometrical functions and how to apply integration to solve problems.

It is important that you have a good grasp of the work covered in Chapter 3 on trigonometry, as you will be expected to know and use the trigonometrical relationships when solving integration problems.

Lastly, we will learn how to find an estimate for $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$ using a numerical method. This is used when we are not able to find the value of $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$ using an algebraic method.

### 5.1 Integration of exponential functions

Since integration is the reverse process of differentiation, the rules for integrating exponential functions are:

KEY POINT 5.1

$$
\int \mathrm{e}^{x} \mathrm{~d} x=\mathrm{e}^{x}+c \quad \int \mathrm{e}^{a x+b} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a} \mathrm{e}^{a x+b}+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.1

Find:

**a** $\int \mathrm{e}^{3 x} \mathrm{~d} x$

**b** $\int \mathrm{e}^{-2 x} \mathrm{~d} x$

**c** $\int \mathrm{e}^{5 x-4} \mathrm{~d} x$

Answer

**a** $\int \mathrm{e}^{3 x} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{3} \mathrm{e}^{3 x}+c$

**b** $\int \mathrm{e}^{-2 x} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{-2} \mathrm{e}^{-2 x}+c$ $=-\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{-2 x}+c$

**c** $\int \mathrm{e}^{5 x-4} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{5} \mathrm{e}^{5 x-4}+c$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.2

Evaluate $\int_{1}^{2} 9 \mathrm{e}^{3 x-1} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{1}^{2} 9 \mathrm{e}^{3 x-1} \mathrm{~d} x & =\left[\frac{9}{3} \mathrm{e}^{3 x-1}\right]_{1}^{2} \\
& =\left(3 \mathrm{e}^{5}\right)-\left(3 \mathrm{e}^{2}\right) \\
& =3 \mathrm{e}^{2}\left(\mathrm{e}^{3}-1\right)
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.3

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-113_274_392_1007_263.jpg)

Find the area of the shaded region.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\text { Area } & =\int_{1}^{2}\left(x^{2}+\mathrm{e}^{-2 x}\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\left[\frac{x^{3}}{3}-\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{-2 x}\right]_{1}^{2} \\
& =\left(\frac{8}{3}-\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{-4}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{-2}\right) \\
& =\frac{7}{3}+\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{-2}-\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{-4} \\
& \approx 2.39 \text { units }^{2}
\end{align}
$$

### 5.2 Integration of $\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b}}$

Since integration is the reverse process of differentiation, the rules for integration are:

KEY POINT 5.2

$$
\int \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=\ln x+c, x>0 \quad \int \frac{1}{a x+b} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a} \ln (a x+b)+c, a x+b>0
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.4

Find each of these integrals and state the values of $x$ for which the integral is valid.

**a** $\int \frac{2}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$

**b** $\int \frac{4}{2 x+1} \mathrm{~d} x$

**c** $\int \frac{6}{2-3 x} \mathrm{~d} x$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\int \frac{2}{x} \mathrm{~d} x & =2 \int \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =2 \ln x+c, x>0
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\int \frac{4}{2 x+1} \mathrm{~d} x & =4 \int \frac{1}{2 x+1} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =4\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) \ln (2 x+1)+c \\
& =2 \ln (2 x+1)+c, x>-\frac{1}{2}
\end{align}
$$

## REWIND

In Chapter 4, we learnt the following rules for differentiating logarithmic functions:

$$
\begin{align}
& \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln x)=\frac{1}{x}, x>0 \\
& \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\ln (a x+b)]= \\
& \frac{a}{a x+b}, a x+b>0
\end{align}
$$

It is important to remember that $\ln x$ is only defined for $x>0$.

c

$$
\begin{align}
\int \frac{6}{2-3 x} \mathrm{~d} x & =6 \int \frac{1}{2-3 x} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =6\left(\frac{1}{-3}\right) \ln (2-3 x)+c \qquad \text { Valid for } 2-3 x>0 . \\
& =-2 \ln (2-3 x)+c, x<\frac{2}{3}
\end{align}
$$

EXPLORE 5.1

Fiona is asked to find $\int x^{-1} \mathrm{~d} x$.

She tries to use the formula $\int x^{n} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{n+1} x^{n+1}+c$ to obtain her answer.

Fiona is also asked to find $\int(2 x+3)^{-1} \mathrm{~d} x$.

She tries to use the formula $\int(a x+b)^{n} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a(n+1)}(a x+b)^{n+1}+c$ to obtain her answer.

Discuss with your classmates why Fiona's methods do not work.

EXPLORE 5.2

Rafiu and Fausat are asked to find $\int \frac{1}{2(3 x+1)} \mathrm{d} x$.

Rafiu writes: $\int \frac{1}{2(3 \mathrm{x}+1)} \mathrm{dx}=\int \frac{1}{6 \mathrm{x}+2} \mathrm{dx}$

$$
=\frac{1}{6} \ln (6 x+2)+c
$$

Fausat writes: $\int \frac{1}{2(3 x+1)} d x=\frac{1}{2} \int \frac{1}{3 x+1} d x$

$$
\begin{align}
& =\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(\frac{1}{3}\right) \ln (3 x+1)+c \\
& =\frac{1}{6} \ln (3 x+1)+c
\end{align}
$$

Decide who is correct and discuss the reasons for your decision with your classmates.

Consider the integrals $\int_{2}^{3} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$ and $\int_{-3}^{-2} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$.

From the symmetry properties of the graph of $y=\frac{1}{x}$ we can see that the shaded areas that represent the two integrals are equal in magnitude. However, one of the areas is below the $x$-axis, which suggests that $\int_{-3}^{-2} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=-\int_{2}^{3} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-116_322_454_2219_1543.jpg)

Evaluating $\int_{2}^{3} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$ gives:

$$
\int_{2}^{3} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=[\ln x]_{2}^{3}=\ln 3-\ln 2=\ln \frac{3}{2}
$$

This implies that $\int_{-3}^{-2} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=-\ln \frac{3}{2}=\ln \frac{2}{3}$.

If we try using integration to find the value of $\int_{-3}^{-2} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$ we obtain:

$$
\int_{-3}^{-2} \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=[\ln x]_{-3}^{-2}=\ln (-2)-\ln (-3)=\ln \frac{2}{3}
$$

There is, however, a problem with this calculation in that $\ln x$ is only defined for $x>0$ so $\ln (-2)$ and $\ln (-3)$ do not actually exist.

Hence for $x<0$, we say that $\int \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=\ln |x|+c$.

In conclusion, the integration formulae are written as:

## KEY POINT 5.3

$$
\int \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=\ln |x|+c \quad \int \frac{1}{a x+b} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a} \ln |a x+b|+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.5

Find the value of $\int_{2}^{3} \frac{6}{2-3 x} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{2}^{3} \frac{6}{2-3 x} \mathrm{~d} x & =\left[\left(\frac{6}{-3}\right) \ln |2-3 x|\right]_{2}^{3} \qquad \text { Substitute limits. } \\
& =(-2 \ln 7)-(-2 \ln 4) \\
& =-2 \ln 7+2 \ln 4 \\
& =2 \ln \frac{4}{7}
\end{align}
$$

### 5.3 Integration of $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { s i n }}(\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b}), \boldsymbol{\operatorname { c o s }}(\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b})$ and $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { s e c }}^{\mathbf{2}}(\boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{x}+\boldsymbol{b})$

In Chapter 4, we learnt how to differentiate some trigonometric functions:

$$
\begin{array}{ll} \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\sin x)=\cos x & \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\sin (a x+b)]=a \cos (a x+b) \\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\cos x)=-\sin x & \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\cos (a x+b)]=-a \sin (a x+b) \\
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\tan x)=\sec ^{2} x & \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}[\tan (a x+b)]=a \sec ^{2}(a x+b) \end{array}
$$

## KEY POINT 5.4

Since integration is the reverse process of differentiation, the rules for integrating are:

$$
\begin{array}{ll} \int \cos x d x=\sin x+c & \int \cos (a x+b) d x=\frac{1}{a} \sin (a x+b)+c \\
\int \sin x d x=-\cos x+c & \int \sin (a x+b) d x=-\frac{1}{a} \cos (a x+b)+c \\
\int \sec ^{2} x d x=\tan x+c & \int \sec ^{2}(a x+b) d x=\frac{1}{a} \tan (a x+b)+c \end{array}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.6

Find:

**a** $\int \sin 2 x \mathrm{~d} x$

**b** $\int \cos 3 x \mathrm{~d} x$

**c** $\int 4 \sec ^{2} \frac{x}{2} \mathrm{~d} x$

Answer

**a** $\int \sin 2 x \mathrm{~d} x=-\frac{1}{2} \cos 2 x+c$

**b** $\int \cos 3 x \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{3} \sin 3 x+c$

**c** $\int 4 \sec ^{2} \frac{x}{2} \mathrm{~d} x=4 \int \sec ^{2} \frac{x}{2} \mathrm{~d} x$

$$
\begin{align}
& =4 \times\left(2 \tan \frac{x}{2}\right)+c \\
& =8 \tan \frac{x}{2}+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.7

Find the exact value of $\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}}(3-2 \sin 2 x) \mathrm{d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}}(3-2 \sin 2 x) \mathrm{d} x & =\left[3 x+\frac{2}{2} \cos 2 x\right]_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}} \\
& =\left(\frac{3 \pi}{4}+\cos \frac{\pi}{2}\right)-(0+\cos 0) \\
& =\left(\frac{3 \pi}{4}+0\right)-(0+1) \\
& =\frac{3 \pi}{4}-1
\end{align}
$$

### 5.4 Further integration of trigonometric functions

Sometimes, when it is not immediately obvious how to integrate a trigonometric function, we can use trigonometric identities to rewrite the function in a form that we can then integrate.

To integrate $\sin ^{2} x$ and $\cos ^{2} x$ we must use the double angle identities:

$$
\cos 2 x \equiv 1-2 \sin ^{2} x \text { and } \cos 2 x \equiv 2 \cos ^{2} x-1
$$

Rearranging these two identities gives:

$$
\sin ^{2} x \equiv \frac{1}{2}(1-\cos 2 x) \text { and } \cos ^{2} x \equiv \frac{1}{2}(1+\cos 2 x)
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.8

Find:

**a** $\int \sin ^{2} x \mathrm{~d} x$

**b** $\int 3 \cos ^{2} 2 x \mathrm{~d} x$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\int \sin ^{2} x \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{1}{2} \int(1-\cos 2 x) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2}\left(x-\frac{1}{2} \sin 2 x\right)+c \\
& =\frac{1}{2} x-\frac{1}{4} \sin 2 x+c
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\int 3 \cos ^{2} 2 x \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{3}{2} \int(1+\cos 4 x) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\frac{3}{2}\left(x+\frac{1}{4} \sin 4 x\right)+c \\
& =\frac{3}{2} x+\frac{3}{8} \sin 4 x+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.9

Find $\int \sin ^{4} x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\sin ^{4} x & =\left(\sin ^{2} x\right)^{2} \\
& =\left[\frac{1}{2}(1-\cos 2 x)\right]^{2} \\
& =\frac{1}{4}\left(1-2 \cos 2 x+\cos ^{2} 2 x\right) \qquad \text { Use }^{2} \sin ^{2} x \equiv \frac{1}{2}(1-\cos 2 x) \\
& =\frac{1}{4}\left(1-2 \cos 2 x+\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{2} \cos 4 x\right) \\
& =\frac{3}{8}-\frac{1}{2} \cos 2 x+\frac{1}{8} \cos 4 x
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\int \sin ^{4} x \mathrm{~d} x & =\int\left(\frac{3}{8}-\frac{1}{2} \cos 2 x+\frac{1}{8} \cos 4 x\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\frac{3}{8} x-\frac{1}{4} \sin 2 x+\frac{1}{32} \sin 4 x+c
\end{align}
$$

To integrate $\tan ^{2} x$ we use the identity $1+\tan ^{2} x \equiv \sec ^{2} x$.

Rearranging this identity gives $\tan ^{2} x \equiv \sec ^{2} x-1$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.10

Find the exact value of $\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}} 5 \tan ^{2} x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}} 5 \tan ^{2} x \mathrm{~d} x & =\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}}\left(5 \sec ^{2} x-5\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =[5 \tan x-5 x]_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{4}} \\
& =\left(5-\frac{5 \pi}{4}\right)-(0-0) \\
& =5-\frac{5 \pi}{4}
\end{align}
$$

Worked example 5.11 uses the principle that integration is the reverse process of differentiation.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.11

**a** Show that if $y=\sec x$ then $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\tan x \sec x$.

**b** Hence find the exact value of $\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi}(\cos 2 x+5 \tan x \sec x) \mathrm{d} x$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
y & =\frac{1}{\cos x} \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{(\cos x)(0)-(1)(-\sin x)}{\cos ^{2} x} \\
& =\frac{\sin x}{\cos ^{2} x} \\
& =\tan x \sec x
\end{align}
$$

**b** $\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi}(\cos 2 x+5 \tan x \sec x) \mathrm{d} x$ Use $\int \tan x \sec x \mathrm{~d} x=\sec x$.

$$
\begin{align}
& =\left[\frac{1}{2} \sin 2 x+5 \sec x\right]_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi} \\
& =\left(\frac{1}{2}+5 \sqrt{2}\right)-(0+5) \\
& =5 \sqrt{2}-\frac{9}{2}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.12

**a** Prove that $2 \operatorname{cosec} 2 x \tan x \equiv \sec ^{2} x$.

**b** Hence find the exact value of $\int_{-\frac{1}{3} \pi}^{\frac{1}{3} \pi}(5+\operatorname{cosec} 2 x \tan x) \mathrm{d} x$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
2 \operatorname{cosec} 2 x \tan x & \equiv \frac{2 \tan x}{\sin 2 x} \\
& \equiv \frac{2 \sin x}{(2 \sin x \cos x) \cos x} \qquad \infty \qquad \text { Use } \tan x=\frac{\sin x}{\cos x} \text { and } \\
& \equiv \frac{1}{\cos ^{2} x} \\
& \equiv \sec ^{2} x
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{-\frac{1}{3} \pi}^{\frac{1}{3} \pi}(5+\operatorname{cosec} 2 x \tan x) \mathrm{d} x & =\int_{-\frac{1}{3} \pi}^{\frac{1}{3} \pi}\left(5+\frac{1}{2} \sec ^{2} x\right) \mathrm{d} x \qquad \text { Use part a. } \\
& =\left[5 x+\frac{1}{2} \tan x\right]_{-\frac{1}{3} \pi}^{\frac{1}{3} \pi} \qquad \text { Simplify. } \\
& =\left(\frac{5 \pi}{3}+\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)-\left(-\frac{5 \pi}{3}-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) \\
& =\frac{10 \pi}{3}+\sqrt{3}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.13

**a** Use the expansions of $\cos (3 x-x)$ and $\cos (3 x+x)$ to show that:

$$
\cos 2 x-\cos 4 x \equiv 2 \sin 3 x \sin x
$$

**b** Hence show that $\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi} \sin 3 x \sin x \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{4}$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\cos 2 x-\cos 4 x & \equiv \cos (3 x-x)-\cos (3 x+x) \\
& \equiv(\cos 3 x \cos x+\sin 3 x \sin x)-(\cos 3 x \cos x-\sin 3 x \sin x) \\
& \equiv 2 \sin 3 x \sin x
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi} \sin 3 x \sin x \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{1}{2} \int_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi}(\cos 2 x-\cos 4 x) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{1}{2} \sin 2 x-\frac{1}{4} \sin 4 x\right]_{0}^{\frac{1}{4} \pi} \qquad \text { Use part a. } \\
& =\frac{1}{2}\left[\left(\frac{1}{2}-0\right)-(0-0)\right] \\
& =\frac{1}{4}
\end{align}
$$

### 5.5 The trapezium rule

We already know that the area, $A$, under the curve $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$ between $x=a$ and $x=b$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-126_332_383_986_1612.jpg)

can be found by evaluating $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$. Sometimes we might not be able to find the value of $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$ by the integration methods that we have covered so far or maybe the function cannot be integrated algebraically.

In these situations an approximate answer can be found using a numerical method called the trapezium rule.

This numerical method involves splitting the area under the curve $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$ between $x=a$ and $x=b$ into equal width strips.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-126_307_790_1523_469.jpg)

The area of each trapezium-shaped strip can be found using the formula:

$$
\text { area }=\frac{1}{2}(\text { sum of parallel sides }) \times \text { width }
$$

The sum of the areas of all the strips gives an approximate value for the area under the curve and hence an approximate value for $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$.

For the previous diagram, there are 6 strips, each of width $h$, where $h=\frac{b-a}{6}$ and the length of the vertical edges (ordinates) of the strips are $y_{0}, y_{1}, y_{2}, y_{3}, y_{4}, y_{5}$ and $y_{6}$.

The sum of the areas of the trapezium-shaped strips is:

$$
\begin{align}
& \frac{h}{2}\left(y_{0}+y_{1}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{1}+y_{2}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{2}+y_{3}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{3}+y_{4}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{4}+y_{5}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{5}+y_{6}\right) \\
& =\frac{h}{2}\left[y_{0}+y_{6}+2\left(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}+y_{4}+y_{5}\right)\right]
\end{align}
$$

When there are $n$ strips and the ordinates are $y_{0}, y_{1}, y_{2}, y_{3}, \qquad , y_{n-1}, y_{n}$ then the sum of the areas of the strips is:

$$
\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{0}+y_{1}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{1}+y_{2}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{2}+y_{3}\right)+\qquad +\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{n-2}+y_{n-1}\right)+\frac{h}{2}\left(y_{n-1}+y_{n}\right)
$$

Hence we can find an approximate value for $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$ using:

## KEY POINT 5.5

$$
\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x \approx \frac{h}{2}\left[y_{0}+y_{n}+2\left(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}+\qquad +y_{n-1}\right)\right] \text { where } h=\frac{b-a}{n} \text {. }
$$

An easy way to remember this rule in terms of the ordinates is:

$$
\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x \approx \text { half width of strip × (first }+ \text { last }+ \text { twice the sum of all the others). }
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.14

The diagram shows the curve $y=x-2 \ln x$.

Use the trapezium rule with 2 intervals to estimate the shaded area, giving your answer correct to 2 decimal places. State, with a reason, whether the trapezium rule gives an under-estimate or an over-estimate of the true value.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-127_328_455_1150_1119.jpg)

Answer

$$
a=0.5, b=3.5 \text { and } h=1.5
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-127_303_456_1624_468.jpg)

| $\boldsymbol{x}$ | 0.5 | 2 | 3.5 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $y$ | 1.8863 | 0.6137 | 0.9945 |
|  | $y_{0}$ | $y_{1}$ | $y_{2}$ |

$$
\begin{align}
\text { Area } & \approx \frac{h}{2}\left[y_{0}+y_{2}+2 y_{1}\right] \\
& \approx \frac{1.5}{2}[1.8863+0.9945+2 \times 0.6137] \\
& \approx 3.08115 \qquad \\
& \approx 3.08 \text { (to } 2 \text { decimal places) }
\end{align}
$$

It can be seen from the diagram that this is an over-estimate since the top edges of the strips all lie above the curve.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 5.15

The diagram shows the curve $y=2 \sqrt{4 x-x^{2}}$.

Use the trapezium rule with 4 intervals to estimate the value of $\int_{0}^{4} 2 \sqrt{4 x-x^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x$ giving your answer correct to 2 decimal places. State, with a reason, whether the trapezium rule gives an under-estimate or an over-estimate of the true value.

Answer

$$
a=0, b=4 \text { and } h=1
$$

| $\boldsymbol{x}$ | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 | 4 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $y$ | 0 | $2 \sqrt{3}$ | 4 | $2 \sqrt{3}$ | 0 |
|  | $y_{0}$ | $y_{1}$ | $y_{2}$ | $y_{3}$ | $y_{4}$ |

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-128_219_468_859_1056.jpg)

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{0}^{4} 2 \sqrt{4 x-x^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x & \approx \frac{h}{2}\left[y_{0}+y_{4}+2\left(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}\right)\right] \\
& \approx \frac{1}{2}[0+0+2(2 \sqrt{3}+4+2 \sqrt{3})] \\
& \approx 4 \sqrt{3}+4 \\
& \approx 10.93 \text { (to } 2 \text { decimal places) }
\end{align}
$$

It can be seen from the diagram that this is an under-estimate since the top edges of the strips all lie below the curve.

Worked example 5.14 involves a 'concave' curve and the diagram shows that the trapezium rule gives an over-estimate for the area.

Worked example 5.15 involves a 'convex' curve and the diagram shows that the trapezium rule gives an under-estimate for the area.

If a curve is partially convex and partially concave over the required interval it is not so easy to predict whether the trapezium rule will give an under-estimate or an over-estimate for the area.

The more strips that are used, the more accurate the estimate will be.

| $y$ | 0 | $2 \sqrt{3}$ | 4 | $2 \sqrt{3}$ | 0 |

DID YOU KNOW?

If you double the number of strips in a trapezium rule approximation, the error reduces to approximately one quarter the previous error.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

Integration formulae

- $\int \mathrm{e}^{x} \mathrm{~d} x=\mathrm{e}^{x}+c$ $\int \mathrm{e}^{a x+b} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a} \mathrm{e}^{a x+b}+c$

- $\int \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x=\ln |x|+c$ $\int \frac{1}{a x+b} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a} \ln |a x+b|+c$

- $\int \cos x \mathrm{~d} x=\sin x+c$ $\int \cos (a x+b) \mathrm{d} x=\frac{1}{a} \sin (a x+b)+c$

- $\int \sin x \mathrm{~d} x=-\cos x+c$ $\int \sin (a x+b) \mathrm{d} x=-\frac{1}{a} \cos (a x+b)+c$

- $\int \sec ^{2} x \mathrm{~d} x=\tan x+c$ $\int \sec ^{2}(a x+b) \mathrm{d} x=\frac{1}{a} \tan (a x+b)+c$

The trapezium rule

- The trapezium rule can be used to find an approximate value for $\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x$. If the region under the curve is divided into $n$ strips each of width $h$, then:

$$
\int_{a}^{b} \mathrm{f}(x) \mathrm{d} x \approx \frac{h}{2}\left[y_{0}+y_{n}+2\left(y_{1}+y_{2}+y_{3}+\qquad +y_{n-1}\right)\right] \text { where } h=\frac{b-a}{n} \text {. }
$$

## Chapter 6 <br> Numerical solutions of equations

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- locate approximately a root of an equation, by means of graphical considerations and/or searching for a sign change

- understand the idea of, and use the notation for, a sequence of approximations that converges to a root of an equation

- understand how a given simple iterative formula of the form $x_{n+1}=\mathrm{F}\left(x_{n}\right)$ relates to the equation being solved, and use a given iteration, or an iteration based on a given rearrangement of an equation, to determine a root to a prescribed degree of accuracy.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-133_606_1942_2166_137.jpg)

PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| IGCSE / O Level Mathematics | Substitute numbers for letters in complicated formulae and algebraic expressions, including those involving logarithmic functions, exponential functions and major and minor trigonometric functions. | 1 Evaluate each of the following expressions when $x=1.5$.<br>a $\frac{2}{3}\left(x+\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)$<br>b $100 \sin x-99 x$, ( $x$ in radians)<br>c $\mathrm{e}^{-x}$<br>d $\ln x+5$<br> |
| IGCSE / O Level Mathematics | Rearrange complicated formulae and equations. | 2 Rearrange each formula so that $x$ is the subject.<br>a $y=4 x^{2}+13$, given $x>0$<br>b $0=\frac{y-3 x+7}{w}$<br>c $3=\sqrt[3]{\frac{1+y}{x^{5}}}$<br> |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapters 1 and 2 | Understand the relationship between a graph and its associated algebraic equation, and use the relationship between points of intersection of graphs and solutions of equations. | 3 Show that the $x$-coordinates of the solutions to the simultaneous equations: $\begin{aligned} & y=x^{2}-6 x+10 \\ & y=x \end{aligned}$ <br> also satisfy the equation $x^{2}-7 x+10=0 .$ |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 1 | Know that the values of $x$ that make both sides of an equation equal are called the roots of the equation. <br> Also, the roots of $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$ are the $x$-intercepts on the graph of $y=\mathrm{f}(x) .$ | 4 Find the roots of the equation $x^{2}-7 x+10=0$. |
| IGCSE / O Level Mathematics Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 5 | Sketch graphs of quadratic or cubic functions or the trigonometric functions sine, cosine, tangent. | 5 Sketch the graph, $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$, of each function.<br>a $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{3}+1$<br>b $\mathrm{f}(x)=\cos 2 x-3$<br>c $\mathrm{f}(x)=4 x^{2}-16 x$<br> |
| Chapters 2 and 3 | Sketch graphs of exponential or logarithmic functions or the trigonometric functions cosecant, secant, cotangent. | 6 Sketch the graph, $y=\mathrm{g}(x)$, of each function.<br>a $\mathrm{g}(x)=\frac{\mathrm{e}^{x}}{2}$<br>b $\mathrm{g}(x)=\ln (x-1)$<br>c $\mathrm{g}(x)=\operatorname{cosec} x$<br> |

Why solve equations numerically?

You are used to solving equations using direct, algebraic methods such as factorising or using the quadratic formula.

You might be surprised to learn that not all equations can be solved using a direct, algebraic process.

Numerical methods are ways of calculating approximate solutions to equations. They are extremely powerful problem-solving tools and many are available. They are widely used in engineering, computing, finance and many other applications.

A well-programmed computer is quickly able to solve a complicated equation using a numerical method. So why do we need to understand how the method works?

Knowing how a numerical method works helps us to:

- understand when the results are likely to be reasonable

- understand how to use available software correctly

- select an appropriate method when choices are available

- write our own programs when we need to do so, if we have the programming skills!

To be a powerful problem-solving tool, the method must be used correctly. This might mean the method needs to work quickly or work to obtain a particular level of accuracy. When used incorrectly, numerical methods might be very slow or even fail to work, as we will see later in this chapter.

In this chapter we will focus on one numerical method, the numerical solution of an equation using an iterative formula.

### 6.1 Finding a starting point

As we will see throughout this chapter, a good starting point is very important.

We need a method to find an approximate solution to an equation first.

We might find this using, for example, a graphical approach or the change of sign approach.

For suitable functions, each of these approaches will produce two values: one above and one below the solution that we are finding.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.1

By sketching graphs of $y=x^{3}$ and $y=4-x$, show that the equation $x^{3}+x-4=0$ has a root $\alpha$ between 1 and 2.

Answer

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-135_522_799_2107_512.jpg)

There is one point of intersection and its $x$-coordinate is between 1 and 2.

At this point $x^{3}=4-x$.

This means that the equation $x^{3}=4-x$ has one root between 1 and 2.

Rearranging this equation gives $x^{3}+x-4=0$.

Hence $x^{3}+x-4=0$ has a root, $\alpha$, between 1 and 2.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.2

Show, by calculation, that the equation $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{5}+x-1=0$ has a root $\alpha$ between 0 and 1 .

Answer

| Find the value of $\alpha$ such that $\mathrm{f}(\alpha)=0$. | Why? Think about it and check the explanation that follows. |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\mathrm{f}(0)=0^{5}+0-1=-1$ | As $\alpha$ is between 0 and 1 , then $\mathrm{f}(0)$ might have the opposite sign to f(1). |
| :--- | :--- |
| $\mathrm{f}(1)=1^{5}+1-1=1$ |  |

The change of sign indicates the presence of a root, so $0<\alpha<1$.

This conclusion is important as it completes the argument.

To see why the change of sign method works in this case, look at the graph of $y=\mathrm{f}(x)$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-136_467_604_1526_786.jpg)

Since it starts below the $x$-axis at $x=0$ and finishes above the $x$-axis at $x=1$, it must cross the $x$-axis somewhere in between, say at $x=\alpha$, where $0<\alpha<1$. So $\alpha$ is a root of the equation, that is, $\mathrm{f}(\alpha)=0$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.3

**a** By sketching a suitable pair of graphs, show that the equation $\cos x=2 x-1$ (where $x$ is in radians) has only one root for $0 \leqslant x \leqslant \frac{1}{2} \pi$.

**b** Verify by calculation that this root lies between $x=0.8$ and $x=0.9$.

Answer

**a** Draw the graphs of $y=\cos x$ and $y=2 x-1$ Why are these graphs suitable?

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-137_515_626_749_415.jpg)

The graphs intersect once only and so the equation $\cos x=2 x-1$ has only one root for $0 \leqslant x \leqslant \frac{1}{2} \pi$.

**b** $\cos x=2 x-1$ so $\cos x-2 x+1=0$

Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=\cos x-2 x+1$ and so $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$ then

$$
\begin{align}
& f(0.8)=\cos 0.8-2(0.8)+1=0.0967 \qquad \\
& f(0.9)=\cos 0.9-2(0.9)+1=-0.1783 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates the presence of a root.

## DID YOU KNOW?

Interestingly, cubic and quartic equations can be solved algebraically. This has been known since the 16th century and was shown by the Italian mathematicians Niccolò Fontana (Tartaglia) and Lodovico Ferrari. Quintic equations cannot be solved algebraically in general. This was shown by the Norwegian mathematician Niels Abel and the French mathematician Évariste Galois in the early 19th century.

### 6.2 Improving your solution

Once we have an approximate location for a solution, we can improve it.

One common way to do this is to use an iterative process, finding values that are closer and closer to $\alpha$, the root we are looking to find.

For example, we have shown, by calculation, that the equation $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{5}+x-1=0$ has a root $\alpha$ between 0 and 1 . This is a reasonable starting point but probably not accurate enough if we wanted a solution to this equation in the real world. What if we needed to know the value correct to 1 significant figure? How could we find it?

Let us consider a rearrangement of $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{5}+x-1=0$.

The root we are trying to find is a value of $x$, so rearranging $x^{5}+x-1=0$ into the form $x=\mathrm{F}(x)$ seems reasonable (in other words, $x=$ some function of $x$ ).

One possible rearrangement of this form is $x=\sqrt[5]{1-x}$.

As we already know that $0<\alpha<1$, a good starting point is $x=0.5$.

Now we can test our value to see how accurate it is. How?

- Work out the value of $\sqrt[5]{1-x}$ for the starting value.

$$
\sqrt[5]{1-0.5}=\sqrt[5]{0.5}=0.87055
$$

- Compare it with $x$.

| $x$ | $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})=\sqrt[\mathbf{5}]{\mathbf{1}-\boldsymbol{x}}$ | Are $\boldsymbol{x}$ and $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})$ the same correct to 1 significant figure? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 0.5 | 0.87055 ... | No |

- Are the values the same to the required number of significant figures or decimal places?

Yes - then use this value as the estimate for the root.

No - then find a more accurate value.

Look at the graphs of $y=x$ and $y=\sqrt[5]{1-x}$ on the same diagram.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-140_862_744_1802_178.jpg)

The graphs show that the values $x$ and $\mathrm{F}(x)$ are not very close together as the vertical distance between the line and curve is quite large.

How do we improve the accuracy of the estimate for the root?

We can see in the following diagram that $0.87055$ is a better approximation for the root $\alpha$ than 0.5.

Is 0.870 55... close enough?

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-141_912_743_529_218.jpg)

The vertical distance between the line and curve is now smaller, so the values are converging.

Testing $x=0.87055$ gives $\sqrt[5]{1-x}=\sqrt[5]{1-0.87055} \qquad =0.66438$

| $x$ | $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})=\sqrt[\mathbf{5}]{\mathbf{1}-\boldsymbol{x}}$ | Are $\boldsymbol{x}$ and $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})$ the same correct to 1 significant figure? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 0.5 | 0.87055... | No |
| $0.87055$ | 0.66438... | No |

We can see that $0.66438$ is a better approximation of the root $\alpha$ than $0.87055$ because the values are closer together. Is 0.66438... close enough?

When we continue testing and comparing values, we find:

| $x$ | $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})=\sqrt[\mathbf{5}]{\mathbf{1}-\boldsymbol{x}}$ | Are $\boldsymbol{x}$ and $\mathbf{F}(\boldsymbol{x})$ the same correct to 1 significant figure? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| 0.66438... | 0.80383... | No |
| 0.80383... | 0.72197... | No |
| 0.72197... | 0.77413... | No |
| 0.77413... | 0.742 62... | No |
| 0.742 62... | 0.76227... | No |
| 0.76227... | 0.75026... | Yes |

Each test is called an iteration. Each iteration gives a value of $x$ that is closer to the actual value of the root than the previous value of $x$.

It seems as if the value of $\alpha$ is 0.8 correct to 1 significant figure.

This (enlarged) diagram shows what is happening with each new value.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-142_1437_1140_345_183.jpg)

Each value that comes from $\mathrm{F}(x)$ is used as the next value for $x$. The spiral is often called the cobweb pattern.

Summary

When trying to solve $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$, rearrange the equation to the form $x=\mathrm{F}(x)$.

When $\alpha$ is the value of $x$ such that $x=\mathrm{F}(x)$ then $\alpha$ is also a solution of $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$.

Subscript notation and formulae

In order to avoid unnecessary repetition and to make sure the process is recorded accurately, the method outlined is usually described using subscript notation.

The first approximation of the root is called $x_{1}$ (sometimes it is called $x_{0}$ ).

The next approximation of the root is called $x_{2}$, and so on.

A relationship such as $x=\mathrm{F}(x)$ where $\mathrm{F}(x)=\sqrt[5]{1-x}$ is the basis for an iterative formula:

$$
x_{n+1}=\mathrm{F}\left(x_{n}\right)=\sqrt[5]{1-x_{n}}
$$

Looking at the values we already have we can see how much easier this is to record:

| $\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{0 . 5}$ | $\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}+\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{F}\left(\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}}\right)=\sqrt[\mathbf{5}]{\mathbf{1}-\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}}}$ | Are they the same correct to 1 significant figure? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
|  | $x_{2}=0.87055$ | No |
|  | $x_{3}=0.66438$ | No |
|  | $x_{4}=0.80383$ | No |
|  | $x_{5}=0.72197$ | No |
|  | $x_{6}=0.77413$ | No |
|  | $x_{7}=0.74262$ | No |
|  | $x_{8}=0.76227$ | No |
|  | $x_{9}=0.75026$ | Yes |

In this format we test the accuracy of the values by comparing each value of $x_{n}$ in the sequence with the previous value.

TIP

Does your calculator have an Ans key?

Using the Ans key reduces the number of key strokes needed to use an iterative process.

- Type the value for $x_{1}$ into your calculator and press .

$$
\text { e.g. } 0.5=
$$

- Type the formula into your calculator using Ans instead of $x_{n}$.

$$
\text { e.g. } \sqrt[5]{1-\text { Ans }}
$$

Now each time you press the calculator gives you the next value of $x_{n}$ generated (so $x_{2}, x_{3}, x_{4},$ ).

Try this now and check that you generate the values that are given in the table.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.4

The equation $x^{2}+x-3=0$ has a negative root, $\alpha$.

**a** Show that this equation can be rearranged as $x=\frac{3}{x}-1$.

**b** Use the iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\frac{3}{x_{n}}-1$ with a starting value of $x_{1}=-2.5$ to find $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. Give the result of each iteration to 4 decimal places, where appropriate.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
& x^{2}+x-3=0 \\
& \frac{x^{2}}{x}+\frac{x}{x}-\frac{3}{x}=\frac{0}{x} \\
& x+1-\frac{3}{x}=0 \\
& x=\frac{3}{x}-1 \qquad \quad \text { Divide through by } x . \\
& \quad \text { Isolate } x .
\end{align}
$$

**b**

| $\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathbf{1}}=-\mathbf{2 . 5}$ | $\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}+\mathbf{1}}=\frac{\mathbf{3}}{\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}}}-\mathbf{1}$ | Are these the same correct to 2 decimal places? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
|  | $x_{2}=\frac{3}{-2.5}-1=-2.2$ | No |
|  | $x_{3}=\frac{3}{-2.2}-1=-2.3636$ | No |
|  | $x_{4}=-2.2692$ | No |
|  | $x_{5}=-2.3220$ | No |
|  | $x_{6}=-2.2920$ | No |
|  | $x_{7}=-2.3089$ | No |
|  | $x_{8}=-2.2993$ | No |
|  | $x_{9}=-2.3047$ | Yes |

The root seems to be $\alpha=-2.30$ to 2 decimal places.

We can prove this, using a change of sign test:

When $\alpha=-2.30$ then $-2.305<\alpha<-2.295$ and so

$$
\begin{align}
& f(-2.305)=(-2.305)^{2}+(-2.305)-3=0.00802 \qquad \\
& f(-2.295)=(-2.295)^{2}+(-2.295)-3=-0.0279 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root, therefore $\alpha=-2.30$, to 2 decimal places.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.5

The equation $\mathrm{e}^{x}-1=2 x$ has a root $\alpha=0$.

**a** Show by calculation that this equation also has a root, $\beta$, such that $1<\beta<2$.

**b** Show that this equation can be rearranged as $x=\ln (2 x+1)$.

**c** Use an iteration process based on the equation in part b, with a suitable starting value, to find $\beta$ correct to 3 significant figures.

Give the result of each step of the process to 5 significant figures.

Answer

**a** Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x}-1-2 x=0$ and look for a change of sign.

$$
\begin{align}
& f(1)=e-1-2=-0.2817 \qquad \\
& f(2)=e^{2}-1-2(2)=2.38905 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**b** $\mathrm{e}^{x}-1=2 x$ Isolate e.

$$
\mathrm{e}^{x}=2 x+1
$$

Take logs to base e.

$$
x=\ln (2 x+1)
$$

✓

**c** A suitable iterative formula is $x_{n+1}=\ln \left(2 x_{n}+1\right)$ with a starting value of $x_{1}=1.5$.

(Any value between 1 and 2 would be an acceptable starting value.)

| $\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathbf{1}} \boldsymbol{=} \mathbf{1 . 5}$ | $\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}+\mathbf{1}} \boldsymbol{=} \boldsymbol{\operatorname { l n }} \boldsymbol{(} \mathbf{2} \boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}} \boldsymbol{+} \mathbf{1} \boldsymbol{)}$ | Are these the same correct to 3 significant figures? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
|  | $x_{2}=\ln (2 \times 1.5+1)=1.38629 \qquad =1.3863$ | No |
|  | $x_{3}=\ln (2 \times 1.38629 \qquad +1)=1.32776 \qquad =1.3278$ | No |
|  | $x_{4}=1.29623 \qquad =1.2962$ | No |
|  | $x_{5}=1.27884 \qquad =1.2788$ | No |
|  | $x_{6}=1.26910 \qquad =1.2691$ | No |
|  | $x_{7}=1.26362 \qquad =1.2636$ | No |
|  | $x_{8}=1.26051 \qquad =1.2605$ | Yes |

The root seems to be $\beta=1.26$ correct to 3 significant figures.

We can prove this, using a change of sign test:

When $\beta=1.26$ then $1.255<\beta<1.265$ and so:

$$
\begin{align}
& f(1.255)=e^{1.255}-1-2(1.255)=-0.0021616 \qquad \\
& f(1.265)=e^{1.265}-1-2(1.265)=0.01309 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root, therefore $\beta=1.26$ correct to 3 significant figures.

You might notice that the pattern of convergence is different in this example. This is clear when you look at the graphs of $y=x$ and $y=\ln (2 x+1)$ on the same grid. The type of convergence shown in the graph on the right is often called a staircase pattern.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-145_614_1361_1923_448.jpg)

### EXERCISE 6B

**1** The equation $x^{3}+5 x-7=0$ has a root $\alpha$ between 1 and 1.2.

The iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\frac{7-x_{n}^{3}}{5}$ can be used to find the value of $\alpha$.

**a** Using a starting value of $x_{1}=1.1$, find and write down, correct to 4 decimal places, the value of each of $x_{2}, x_{3}, x_{4}, x_{5}, x_{6}$.

**b** Write the value of $x_{6}$ correct to 2 decimal places and prove that this is the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places.

**2** **a** Show that the equation $\ln (x+1)+2 x-4=0$ has a root $\alpha$ between $x=1$ and $x=2$.

**b** Use the iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\frac{4-\ln \left(x_{n}+1\right)}{2}$ with an initial value of $x_{1}=1.5$ to find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 3 decimal places.

**3** The equation $(x-0.5) \mathrm{e}^{3 x}=1$ has a root $\alpha$.

**a** Show, by sketching the graph of $y=\mathrm{e}^{3 x}$ and one other suitable graph, that $\alpha$ is the only root of this equation.

**b** Use the iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\mathrm{e}^{-3 x_{n}}+0.5$ with $x_{1}=0.5$ to find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. Give the value of each of your iterations to 4 decimal places.

**4** **a** By sketching a suitable pair of graphs, show that the equation $x^{3}+10 x=x+5$ has only one root that lies between 0 and 1.

**b** Use the iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\frac{5-x_{n}^{3}}{9}$, with a suitable value for $x_{1}$, to find the value of this root correct to 4 decimal places. Give the result of each iteration to 6 decimal places.

**5** The equation $\cos ^{-1} 3 x=1-x$ has a root $\alpha$.

**a** Show, by calculation, that $\alpha$ is between $\frac{\pi}{15}$ and $\frac{\pi}{12}$.

**b** Show that the given equation can be rearranged into the form $x=\frac{1}{3} \cos (1-x)$.

**c** Using the iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\frac{1}{3} \cos \left(1-x_{n}\right)$ with a suitable starting value, $x_{1}$, find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 3 decimal places. Give the result of each iteration to 5 decimal places.

**6** The terms of a sequence with first term $x_{1}=1$ are defined by the iterative formula:

$$
x_{n+1}=\sqrt{\frac{5-2 x_{n}-x_{n}^{3}}{5}}
$$

The terms converge to the value $\alpha$.

**a** Use this formula to find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. Give the value of each term you calculate to 4 decimal places.

**b** The value $\alpha$ is the root of an equation of the form $a x^{3}+b x^{2}+c x+d=0$, where $a, b, c$ and $d$ are integers. Find this equation.

**7** The equation $x^{4}-1-x=0$ has a root, $\alpha$, between $x=1$ and $x=2$.

**a** Show that $\alpha$ also satisfies the equation $x=\sqrt[4]{1+x}$.

**b** Write down an iterative formula based on the equation in part a.

**c** Use your iterative formula, with a starting value of $x_{1}=1.5$, to find $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. Give the result of each iteration to 4 decimal places.

**8** The equation $\operatorname{cosec} x=x^{2}$ has a root, $\alpha$, between 1 and 2.

The equation can be rearranged either as $x=\sin ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{x^{2}}\right)$ or $x=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\sin x}}$.

**a** Write down two possible iterative formulae, one based on each given rearrangement.

Use the starting value 1.5.

**b** Show that one of the formulae from part a fails to converge.

**c** Show that the other formula from part a converges to $\alpha$ and find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 3 decimal places. Give the result of each iteration to 5 decimal places.

**9** The terms of a sequence, defined by the iterative formula $x_{n+1}=\ln \left(x_{n}^{2}+4\right)$, converge to the value $\alpha$. The first term of the sequence is 2.

**a** Find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. Give each term of the sequence you find to 4 decimal places.

**b** The value $\alpha$ is a root of an equation of the form $x^{2}=\mathrm{f}(x)$. Find this equation.

**10** The equation $\frac{\sin (x-1)}{2 x-3}+1=0$ has a root, $\alpha$, between $x=1$ and $x=1.4$.

**a** Show that $\alpha$ also satisfies the equation $x=\frac{3-\sin (x-1)}{2}$.

**b** Using an iterative formula based on the equation from part a, with a suitable starting value, find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 3 significant figures. Give the result of each iteration to 5 significant figures.

**11** The graphs of $y=\left(\frac{3}{2}\right)^{x}-1$ and $y=x$ intersect at the points $O(0,0)$ and $A$.

**a** Sketch these graphs on the same diagram.

**b** Using logarithms, find a suitable iterative formula that can be used to find the coordinates of the point $A$.

**c** Calculate the length of the line $O A$, giving your answer correct to 2 significant figures. Give the value of any iterations you calculate to a suitable number of significant figures.

**12** The diagram shows a container in the shape of a cone with a cylinder on top.

The height of the cylinder is 3 times its base radius, $r$.

The volume of the container must be $5500 \mathrm{~cm}^{3}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-147_423_383_1752_1639.jpg)

The base of the cone has a radius of $r \mathrm{~cm}$.

**a** Write down an expression for the height of the cone in terms of $r$.

**b** Show that $2 \pi r^{3}+11 \pi r^{2}-5500=0$.

**c** The equation in part b has a root, $\alpha$.

Show that $\alpha$ is also a root of the equation $r=\sqrt[3]{\frac{5500-11 \pi r^{2}}{2 \pi}}$.

**d** Using an iterative formula based on the equation in part c, and a starting value of 8, find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 3 decimal places. Give the value of each of your iterations to an appropriate degree of accuracy.

**e** Explain what information is given by your answer to part d, in the context of the question.

**13** The equation $x^{3}-7 x^{2}+1=0$ has two positive roots, $\alpha$ and $\beta$, which are such that $\alpha$ lies between 0 and 1 and $\beta$ lies between 6 and 7.

By deriving two suitable iterative formulae from the given equation, carry out suitable iterations to find the value of $\alpha$ and of $\beta$, giving each correct to 2 decimal places. Give the value of each of your iterations to 4 decimal places.

**14** **a** Show graphically that the equation $\cot x=\sin x$ has a root, $\alpha$, which is such that $0<\alpha<\frac{\pi}{2}$.

**b** Show that the equation in part a can be rearranged as $x=\sin ^{-1} \sqrt{\cos x}$.

**c** Using an iterative formula based on the equation in part b, with an initial value of 0.9, find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. Give the value of each iteration to an appropriate number of decimal places.

**d** The equation given in part a can be rearranged in other ways.

Find an example of a formula, based on a different rearrangement of the equation given in part a, that also converges to the value of $\alpha$, with an initial value of 0.9 , in fewer iterations than the formula used in part c.
