### 6.3 Using iterative processes to solve problems involving other areas of mathematics

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.6

**a** Show that $\left(\frac{1+\cos x}{2 \sin x}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1-\cos x}{2 \sin x}\right)^{2}=\cot ^{2} x+\frac{1}{2}$.

**b** Hence, given that $\alpha$ is a root of the equation $\left(\frac{1+\cos x}{2 \sin x}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1-\cos x}{2 \sin x}\right)^{2}=x$, show that $\alpha$ is also a root of the equation $x=\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2 x-1}}$ for $0<x<\frac{\pi}{2}$.

**c** It is given that $\alpha$ is the only root of the equation $x=\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2 x-1}}$ for $0<x<\frac{\pi}{2}$. Verify by calculation that the value of $\alpha$ lies between 0.9 and 1.0.

**d** Using an iterative formula based on the equation in part c, find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 3 significant figures.

Answer

**a** $\left(\frac{1+\cos x}{2 \sin x}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{1-\cos x}{2 \sin x}\right)^{2}$ Square.

$$
\begin{align}
& =\frac{1+2 \cos x+\cos ^{2} x}{4 \sin ^{2} x}+\frac{1-2 \cos x+\cos ^{2} x}{4 \sin ^{2} x} \\
& =\frac{2+2 \cos ^{2} x}{4 \sin ^{2} x} \\
& =\frac{1+\cos ^{2} x}{2 \sin ^{2} x} \\
& =\frac{1}{2 \sin ^{2} x}+\frac{\cos ^{2} x}{2 \sin ^{2} x} \\
& =\frac{\operatorname{cosec}^{2} x}{2}+\frac{\cot ^{2} x}{2} \text { Collect terms. } \\
& =\frac{1+\cot ^{2} x}{2}+\frac{\cot ^{2} x}{2} \\
& =\cot ^{2} x+\frac{1}{2}
\end{align}
$$

**b** $\cot ^{2} x+\frac{1}{2}=x$ Rearrange.

$$
\begin{align}
\cot ^{2} x & =x-\frac{1}{2} \\
\cot x & =\sqrt{x-\frac{1}{2}} \\
\frac{1}{\tan x} & =\sqrt{\frac{2 x-1}{2}} \\
\tan x & =\sqrt{\frac{2}{2 x-1}} \\
x & =\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2 x-1}}
\end{align}
$$

**c** Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=x-\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2 x-1}}$ and so $\mathrm{f}(x)=0$ then:

$$
\begin{align}
& f(0.9)=0.9-\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2(0.9)-1}}=-0.10685 \qquad \\
& f(1)=1-\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2(1)-1}}=0.44683 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates the presence of a root.

| $\boldsymbol{x}_{\mathbf{1}}=\mathbf{0 . 9 5}$ | $\boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}+\mathbf{1}}=\boldsymbol{\operatorname { t a n }}^{-\mathbf{1}} \sqrt{\frac{\mathbf{2}}{\mathbf{2} \boldsymbol{x}_{\boldsymbol{n}}-\mathbf{1}}}$ | Are they the same correct to 3 significant figures? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
|  | $x_{2}=\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2(0.95)-1}}=0.97992$ | No |
|  | $x_{3}=\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2(0.97992 \qquad )-1}}=0.96494$ | No |
|  | $x_{4}=0.97234$ | No |
|  | $x_{5}=0.96866$ | No |
|  | $x_{6}=0.97048$ | No |
|  | $x_{7}=0.96958$ | Yes |

$\alpha$ seems to be 0.970 correct to 3 significant figures.

We can prove this, using a change of sign test:

When $\alpha=0.970$ then $0.9695<\alpha<0.9705$ and so

$$
\begin{align}
& f(0.9695)=0.9695-\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2(0.9695)-1}}=-0.000571 \qquad \\
& f(0.9705)=0.9705-\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{\frac{2}{2(0.9705)-1}}=0.000924 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root.

Therefore $\alpha=0.970$ correct to 3 significant figures.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 6.7

The diagram shows the design of a company logo, $A B C$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-150_372_254_2097_1693.jpg)

$A B$ is an arc of a circle with centre $C$.

The area of the unshaded segment is to be the same as the area of the shaded triangle.

Angle $A C B$ is $\theta$ radians.

**a** Show that $\theta=2 \sin \theta$.

**b** Showing all your working, use an iterative formula based on the equation in part a, with an initial value of 1.85 , to find $\theta$ correct to 3 significant figures.

**c** Hence find the length of $A B$, given that $A C$ is 8 cm.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{2} r^{2} \sin \theta & =\frac{1}{2} r^{2}(\theta-\sin \theta) \\
\sin \theta & =\theta-\sin \theta \\
\theta & =2 \sin \theta
\end{align}
$$

Divide through by $\frac{1}{2} r^{2}$.

Add $\sin \theta$.

✓

**b**

| $\theta_{1}=1.85$ | $\theta_{n+1}=2 \sin \theta_{n}$ | Are they the same correct to 3 significant figures? |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
|  | $\theta_{2}=2 \sin 1.85=1.92255$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{3}=2 \sin 1.92255 \qquad =1.87753$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{4}=1.90664$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{5}=1.88826$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{6}=1.90005$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{7}=1.89256$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{8}=1.89735$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{9}=1.89430$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{10}=1.89625$ | No |
|  | $\theta_{11}=1.89500$ | Yes |

$\theta$ seems to be 1.90 correct to 3 significant figures.

We can prove this, using a change of sign test with $\mathrm{f}(\theta)=\theta-2 \sin \theta=0$ :

When $\theta=1.90$ then $1.895<\theta<1.905$ and so

$$
\begin{align}
& f(1.895)=1.895-2 \sin 1.895=-0.000809 \qquad \\
& f(1.905)=1.905-2 \sin 1.905=0.01565 \qquad
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root, therefore $\theta=1.90$ correct to 3 significant figures.

**c** Using trigonometry:

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-152_440_318_364_320.jpg)

$$
\sin 0.95=\frac{0.5 A B}{8}
$$

$16 \sin 0.95=A B$

$A B=13.014 \qquad =13 \mathrm{~cm}$ (correct to 2 significant figures) Multiply by 16.

EXPLORE 6.3

The golden ratio

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-153_271_399_1274_665.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-153_270_426_1277_1159.jpg)

When a rectangle with sides in the ratio $1: \phi(\mathrm{phi})$ can be cut into a square of side 1 and a smaller rectangle, also with sides in the ratio $1: \phi$, the original rectangle is called a golden rectangle and the ratio $1: \phi$ is called the golden ratio.

This means that $\frac{\phi-1}{1}=\frac{1}{\phi}$.

By using a suitable iterative formula based on this ratio, with a starting value of 1, find the value of $\phi$ correct to 5 significant figures.

Explore the link between the golden ratio and the sequence of Fibonacci numbers:

$$
1,1,2,3,5,8, \qquad ,
$$

DID YOU KNOW?

The relationship between the Fibonacci numbers and the golden ratio produces the golden spiral.

This can be seen in many natural settings, one of which is the spiralling pattern of the petals in a rose flower.

From a tight centre, petals flow outward in a spiral, growing wider and larger as they do so. Nature has made the best use of an incredible structure. This perfect arrangement of petals is why the small, compact rose bud grows into such a beautiful flower.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-153_460_332_2146_1258.jpg)

## Checklist of learning and understanding

- The process of finding a sequence of values that become closer and closer to a point of intersection of $y=x$ and $y=\mathrm{F}(x)$ is called an iterative process.

- Each time you generate a value of $x$ you carry out an iteration.

- When the point of intersection is at $x=\alpha$ and your iterations are values that are getting closer and closer to $\alpha$, you are converging to $\alpha$.

| - Start with $x_{1}$ | work out $\mathrm{F}\left(x_{1}\right)$. | Is it accurate enough? | Yes → Stop, No → |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| - Let $x_{2}=\mathrm{F}\left(x_{1}\right)$ | work out $\mathrm{F}\left(x_{2}\right)$. | Is it accurate enough? | Yes → Stop, No → |
| - Let $x_{3}=\mathrm{F}\left(x_{2}\right)$ | work out $\mathrm{F}\left(x_{3}\right)$. | Is it accurate enough? | Yes → Stop, No → |
| ⋮ |  |  |  |
| - Let $x_{n+1}=\mathrm{F}\left(x_{n}\right)$ | work out $\mathrm{F}\left(x_{n+1}\right)$. | Is it accurate enough? | Yes → Stop. |

- The relationship $x_{n+1}=\mathrm{F}\left(x_{n}\right)$ is called an iterative formula.

- The sequence of values of $x$ given by this formula are called $x_{1}, x_{2}, x_{3}, \qquad , x_{n}$.

- When the value found is accurate enough, then it is usually given a particular name such as $\alpha$.

- The success and speed of the iterative process depends on the function chosen for $\mathrm{F}\left(x_{n}\right)$ and the value chosen for $x_{1}$.

## Chapter 7 <br> Further algebra

This chapter is for Pure Mathematics 3 students only.

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- recall an appropriate form for expressing rational functions in partial fractions, and carry out the decomposition, in cases where the denominator is no more complicated than:

- $(a x+b)(c x+d)(e x+f)$

- $(a x+b)(c x+d)^{2}$

- $(a x+b)\left(c x^{2}+d\right)$

- use the expansion of $(1+x)^{n}$, where $n$ is a rational number and $|x|<1$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-165_573_1942_2202_136.jpg)

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 6 | Equate coefficients of polynomials. | 1 Find the value of $A, B$ and $C$ for:<br>&#160;&#160;a $A x^{2}-3 x+C \equiv 6 x^{2}+B x-9$<br>&#160;&#160;b $(2-A) x^{2}+5 x+2 C \equiv 3 x^{2}-3 B x+8$<br> |
| Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 6 | Expand $(a+b)^{n}$ where $n$ is a positive integer. | 2 Find the first 3 terms, in ascending powers of $x$, in the expansion of:<br><br>&#160;&#160;a $(1+2 x)^{7}$<br>&#160;&#160;b $(3-2 x)^{5}$<br> |
| Chapter 1 | Divide polynomials. | 3 Find the quotient and remainder when $x^{2}-8 x+4$ is divided by $x-3$. |

## Why do we study algebra?

At IGCSE / O Level we learnt how to add and subtract algebraic fractions. In this chapter we will learn how to do the 'reverse process'. This reverse process is often referred to as splitting a fraction into its partial fractions. In Mathematics it is often easier to deal with two or more simple fractions than it is to deal with one complicated fraction.

In the Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 6, you learnt how to find the binomial expansion of $(a+b)^{n}$ for positive integer values of $n$. After working through this chapter you will be able to expand expressions of the form $(1+x)^{n}$ for values of $n$ that are not positive integers (providing $|x|<1$ ).

Combining your partial fraction and binomial expansion skills will enable you to obtain series expansions of complicated expressions such as $\frac{2 x-1}{2 x^{2}+3 x-20}$.

### 7.1 Improper algebraic fractions

A numerical improper fraction is defined as a fraction where the numerator $\geqslant$ the denominator. For example, $\frac{11}{5}$ is an improper fraction. This fraction can be expressed as $2+\frac{1}{5}$, which is the sum of a positive integer and a proper fraction.

So how do we define an algebraic improper fraction?

KEY POINT 7.1

The algebraic fraction $\frac{\mathrm{P}(x)}{\mathrm{Q}(x)}$, where $\mathrm{P}(x)$ and $\mathrm{Q}(x)$ are polynomials in $x$, is said to be an algebraic improper fraction if the degree of $\mathrm{P}(x) \geqslant$ the degree of $\mathrm{Q}(x)$.

For example, the fraction $\frac{x^{3}-3 x^{2}+7}{x-2}$ is an improper algebraic fraction because the degree of the numerator (3) is greater than the degree of the denominator (1).

We can use long division to write the fraction $\frac{x^{3}-3 x^{2}+7}{x-2}$ as the sum of a polynomial and a proper algebraic fraction.

$$
\begin{align}
& x - 2 \longdiv { x ^ { 2 } - x - 2 } \\
& \frac{x^{3}-2 x^{2}+0 x+7}{-x^{2}+0 x} \\
& \frac{-x^{2}+2 x}{-2 x+7} \\
& \frac{-2 x+4}{3} \\
& \therefore \frac{x^{3}-3 x^{2}+7}{x-2}=x^{2}-x-2+\frac{3}{x-2}
\end{align}
$$

EXPLORE 7.1

**1** Discuss with your classmates which of the following are improper algebraic fractions.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-167_281_1112_1236_308.jpg)

**4** Given that $\frac{2 x^{4}+3 x^{3}+4 x^{2}+5 x+6}{x^{3}+2 x} \equiv A x+B+\frac{C x+D}{x^{3}+2 x}$, find the values of $A, B, C$ and $D$.

### 7.2 Partial fractions

At IGCSE / O Level, we learnt how to add and subtract algebraic fractions. The following table shows three different cases for addition of algebraic fractions.

| Case 1 | Case 2 | Case 3 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $\begin{aligned} & \frac{5}{x+3}+\frac{4}{x-2} \\ & =\frac{5(x-2)+4(x+3)}{(x+3)(x-2)} \\ & =\frac{9 x+12}{(x+3)(x-2)} \end{aligned}$ | $\begin{aligned} & \frac{3}{x+1}+\frac{2}{(x+1)^{2}} \\ & =\frac{3(x+1)+2}{(x+1)^{2}} \\ & =\frac{3 x+5}{(x+1)^{2}} \end{aligned}$ | $\begin{aligned} & \frac{2}{x-1}+\frac{3}{x^{2}+5} \\ & =\frac{2\left(x^{2}+5\right)+3(x-1)}{(x-1)\left(x^{2}+5\right)} \\ & =\frac{2 x^{2}+3 x+7}{(x-1)\left(x^{2}+5\right)} \end{aligned}$ |
| Denominator in answer has: two distinct linear factors. | Denominator in answer has: one linear repeated factor. | Denominator in answer has: one linear factor and one quadratic factor. |

In this section we will learn how to do the reverse of this process. We will learn how to split (or decompose) a single algebraic fraction into two or more partial fractions.

## Case 1: Proper fraction where the denominator has distinct linear factors

If the denominator of a proper algebraic fraction has two distinct linear factors, the fraction can be split into partial fractions.

KEY POINT 7.2

$$
\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)(c x+d)} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B}{c x+d}
$$

This rule can be extended for 3 or more linear factors.

KEY POINT 7.3

$$
\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)(c x+d)(e x+f)} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B}{c x+d}+\frac{C}{e x+f}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.1

Express $\frac{2 x-13}{(2 x+1)(x-3)}$ in partial fractions.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{2 x-13}{(2 x+1)(x-3)} & \equiv \frac{A}{2 x+1}+\frac{B}{x-3} \qquad \text { Multiply throughout by }(2 x+1)(x-3) . \\
2 x-13 & \equiv A(x-3)+B(2 x+1) \qquad \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

Method 1: Choose appropriate values for $x$.

Let $x=3$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
2(3)-13 & =A(3-3)+B(6+1) \\
-7 & =7 B \\
B & =-1
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=-\frac{1}{2}$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
2\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)-13 & =A\left(-\frac{1}{2}-3\right)+B(-1+1) \\
-14 & =-\frac{7}{2} A \\
A & =4
\end{align}
$$

$$
\therefore \frac{2 x-13}{(2 x+1)(x-3)} \equiv \frac{4}{2 x+1}-\frac{1}{x-3}
$$

Method 2: Equating coefficients.

$$
\begin{align}
& 2 x-13 \equiv A(x-3)+B(2 x+1) \\
& 2 x-13 \equiv(A+2 B) x-3 A+B
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& A+2 B=2-\qquad \tag{2} \\
& -3 A+B=-13 \tag{3} \\
& A=4, B=-1 \\
& \therefore \frac{2 x-13}{(2 x+1)(x-3)} \equiv \frac{4}{2 x+1}-\frac{1}{x-3}
\end{align}
$$

## Case 2: Proper fraction with repeated linear factor in the denominator

If the denominator has a repeated linear factor, the fraction can be split into partial fractions using the rule:

KEY POINT 7.4

$$
\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)^{2}} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B}{(a x+b)^{2}}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.2

Express $\frac{2 x-11}{(x-4)^{2}}$ in partial fractions.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{2 x-11}{(x-4)^{2}} & \equiv \frac{A}{x-4}+\frac{B}{(x-4)^{2}} \\
2 x-11 & \equiv A(x-4)+B \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=4$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
2(4)-11 & =A(4-4)+B \\
B & =-3
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=0$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
2(0)-11 & =A(0-4)-3 \\
A & =2 \\
\therefore \frac{2 x-11}{(x-4)^{2}} & \equiv \frac{2}{x-4}-\frac{3}{(x-4)^{2}}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.3

Express $\frac{1-9 x-8 x^{2}}{(x-2)(2 x+3)^{2}}$ in partial fractions.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{1-9 x-8 x^{2}}{(x-2)(2 x+3)^{2}} & \equiv \frac{A}{x-2}+\frac{B}{2 x+3}+\frac{C}{(2 x+3)^{2}} \qquad \infty \qquad \text { Multiply throughout by }(x-2)(2 x+3)^{2} . \\
1-9 x-8 x^{2} & \equiv A(2 x+3)^{2}+B(x-2)(2 x+3)+C(x-2) \qquad (1) \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=2$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
1-9(2)-8(2)^{2} & =A(4+3)^{2}+B(2-2)(4+3)+C(2-2) \\
-49 & =49 A \\
A & =-1
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=-\frac{3}{2}$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
1-9\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)-8\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)^{2} & =A(-3+3)^{2}+B\left(-\frac{3}{2}-2\right)(-3+3)+C\left(-\frac{3}{2}-2\right) \\
-\frac{7}{2} & =-\frac{7}{2} C \\
C & =1
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=0$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{equation*} 1=9 A-6 B-2 C \tag{2} \end{equation*}
$$

Substitute $A=-1$ and $C=1$ in equation (2).

$$
\begin{align}
& B=-2 \\
& \therefore \frac{1-9 x-8 x^{2}}{(x-2)(2 x+3)^{2}} \equiv-\frac{1}{x-2}-\frac{2}{2 x+3}+\frac{1}{(2 x+3)^{2}}
\end{align}
$$

## Case 3: Proper fraction with a quadratic factor in the denominator that cannot be factorised

In this syllabus you will only be asked questions where the quadratic factor is of the form $c x^{2}+d$.

If the denominator of a proper algebraic fraction has a quadratic factor of the form $c x^{2}+d$ that cannot be factorised, the fraction can be split into partial fractions using the following rule.

KEY POINT 7.5

$$
\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)\left(c x^{2}+d\right)} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B x+C}{c x^{2}+d}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.4

Express $\frac{8+2 x-x^{2}}{(x-1)\left(x^{2}+2\right)}$ in partial fractions.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{8+2 x-x^{2}}{(x-1)\left(x^{2}+2\right)} & \equiv \frac{A}{x-1}+\frac{B x+C}{x^{2}+2} \\
8+2 x-x^{2} & \equiv A\left(x^{2}+2\right)+B x(x-1)+C(x-1) \qquad \text { Multiply throughout by }(x-1)\left(x^{2}+2\right) \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=1$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
8+2(1)-(1)^{2} & =A\left(1^{2}+2\right)+B(1)(1-1)+C(1-1) \\
9 & =3 A \\
A & =3
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=0$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
8 & =6-C \\
C & =-2
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=-1$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
5 & =9+2 B+4 \\
B & =-4
\end{align}
$$

$$
\therefore \frac{8+2 x-x^{2}}{(x-1)\left(x^{2}+2\right)} \equiv \frac{3}{x-1}+\frac{-4 x-2}{x^{2}+2} \equiv \frac{3}{x-1}-\frac{4 x+2}{x^{2}+2}
$$

## Case 4: Improper fractions

If an algebraic fraction is improper we must first express it as the sum of a polynomial and a proper fraction and then split the proper fraction into partial fractions.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.5

Express $\frac{2 x^{2}+5 x-11}{x^{2}+2 x-3}$ in partial fractions.

Answer

The fraction is improper. Hence it must first be written as the sum of a polynomial and a proper fraction.

$$
\begin{gathered} x ^ { 2 } + 2 x - 3 \longdiv { 2 x ^ { 2 } + 5 x - 1 1 } \\
\frac{2 x^{2}+4 x-6}{x-5} \\
\frac{2 x^{2}+5 x-11}{x^{2}+2 x-3} \equiv 2+\frac{x-5}{x^{2}+2 x-3} \end{gathered}
$$

Split the proper fraction $\frac{x-5}{x^{2}+2 x-3}$ into partial fractions:

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{x-5}{(x+3)(x-1)} & \equiv \frac{A}{x+3}+\frac{B}{x-1} \\
x-5 & \equiv A(x-1)+B(x+3) \qquad \text { Multiply throughout by }(x+3)(x-1)
\end{align}
$$

(1)

Let $x=1$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
-4 & =4 B \\
B & =-1
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=-3$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
-8= & -4 A \\
A= & 2 \\
\frac{x-5}{(x+3)(x-1)} & \equiv \frac{2}{x+3}-\frac{1}{x-1} \\
\therefore \frac{2 x^{2}+5 x-11}{x^{2}+2 x-3} & \equiv 2+\frac{2}{x+3}-\frac{1}{x-1}
\end{align}
$$

Application of partial fractions

Partial fractions can be used to help evaluate the sum of some sequences.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.6

Given that the algebraic fraction $\frac{1}{x(x+1)}$ can be written in partial fractions as $\frac{1}{x}-\frac{1}{x+1}$, find the sum of the first $n$ terms of the series $\frac{1}{1 \times 2}+\frac{1}{2 \times 3}+\frac{1}{3 \times 4}+\frac{1}{4 \times 5}+$. Hence, state the sum to infinity.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
S_{n} & =\frac{1}{1 \times 2}+\frac{1}{2 \times 3}+\frac{1}{3 \times 4}+\frac{1}{4 \times 5}+\qquad +\frac{1}{(n-1) n}+\frac{1}{n(n+1)} \\
& =\left(\frac{1}{1}-\frac{1}{2}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{3}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{3}-\frac{1}{4}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{5}\right)+\qquad +\left(\frac{1}{n-1}-\frac{1}{n}\right)+\left(\frac{1}{n}-\frac{1}{n+1}\right) \\
& =1-\frac{1}{n+1}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& \text { As } n \rightarrow \infty, \frac{1}{n+1} \rightarrow 0 \\
& \therefore S_{\infty}=1
\end{align}
$$

## i) DID YOU KNOW?

The series in Worked example 7.6 is called a telescoping series, because its partial sums only have a fixed number of terms after cancellation.

### 7.3 Binomial expansion of $(\mathbf{1}+\boldsymbol{x})^{\boldsymbol{n}}$ for values of $\boldsymbol{n}$ that are not positive integers

## REWIND

In the Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 6, we learnt that the binomial expansion of $(1+x)^{n}$, where $n$ is a positive integer is:

$$
(1+x)^{n}=\binom{n}{0}+\binom{n}{1} x+\binom{n}{2} x^{2}+\binom{n}{3} x^{3}+\qquad +\binom{n}{n} x^{n}
$$

and that this terminating series can also be written in the form:

$$
(1+x)^{n}=1+n x+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!} x^{2}+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!} x^{3}+\qquad +x^{n} .
$$

The general binomial theorem can be used for any rational value of $n$. It states that:

## KEY POINT 7.6

$$
(1+x)^{n}=1+n x+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!} x^{2}+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!} x^{3}+\qquad \text { where } n \text { is rational and }|x|<1
$$

There are two very important differences when $n$ is not a positive integer. These are:

- the series is infinite

- the expansion is only valid for $|x|<1$.

The proof of the general binomial theorem is beyond the scope of this course.

Consider, however, the following two examples where $n$ is not a positive integer $\left(n=-3\right.$ and $\left.n=\frac{1}{2}\right)$.

$$
\begin{align}
(1+x)^{-3} & =1+(-3) x+\frac{(-3)(-4)}{2!} x^{2}+\frac{(-3)(-4)(-5)}{3!} x^{3}+\frac{(-3)(-4)(-5)(-6)}{4!} x^{4}+\qquad \\
& =1-3 x+6 x^{2}-10 x^{3}+15 x^{4}+\qquad \\
(1+x)^{\frac{1}{2}} & =1+\left(\frac{1}{2}\right) x+\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)}{2!} x^{2}+\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)}{3!} x^{3}+\frac{\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{5}{2}\right)}{4!} x^{4}+\qquad \\
& =1+\frac{1}{2} x-\frac{1}{8} x^{2}+\frac{1}{16} x^{3}-\frac{5}{128} x^{4}+\qquad
\end{align}
$$

We can see that in each case the sequence of coefficients forms an infinite sequence.

When $|x|<1$, the binomial series converges and has a sum to infinity (in a similar way to a geometric series converging for $|r|<1$, where $r$ is the common ratio).

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.7

Expand $(1-3 x)^{-2}$ in ascending powers of $x$ up to and including the term in $x^{3}$, and state the range of values of $x$ for which the expansion is valid.

Answer

Use $n=-2$ and replace $x$ by $-3 x$ in the binomial formula:

$$
\begin{align}
(1-3 x)^{-2} & =1+(-2)(-3 x)+\frac{(-2)(-3)}{2!}(-3 x)^{2}+\frac{(-2)(-3)(-4)}{3!}(-3 x)^{3}+\qquad \\
& =1+6 x+27 x^{2}+108 x^{3}+\qquad
\end{align}
$$

Expansion is valid for $|-3 x|<1$

$$
\therefore|x|<\frac{1}{3} .
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.8

Find the first four terms in the expansion of $\left(1+2 x^{2}\right)^{-4}$ and state the range of values of $x$ for which the expansion is valid.

Answer

Use $n=-4$ and replace $x$ by $2 x^{2}$ in the binomial formula:

$$
\begin{align}
\left(1+2 x^{2}\right)^{-4} & =1+(-4)\left(2 x^{2}\right)+\frac{(-4)(-5)}{2!}\left(2 x^{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{(-4)(-5)(-6)}{3!}\left(2 x^{2}\right)^{3}+\qquad \\
& =1-8 x^{2}+40 x^{4}-160 x^{6}+\qquad
\end{align}
$$

Expansion is valid for $\left|2 x^{2}\right|<1$

$$
\therefore|x|<\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.9

Find the coefficient of $x^{3}$ in the expansion of $\frac{x-5}{1+2 x}$.

Answer

$$
\frac{x-5}{1+2 x}=(x-5)(1+2 x)^{-1}
$$

Use $n=-1$ and replace $x$ by $2 x$ in the binomial formula:

$$
\begin{align}
(1+2 x)^{-1} & =1+(-1)(2 x)+\frac{(-1)(-2)}{2!}(2 x)^{2}+\frac{(-1)(-2)(-3)}{3!}(2 x)^{3}+\qquad \\
& =1-2 x+4 x^{2}-8 x^{3}+\qquad
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
(x-5)(1+2 x)^{-1} & =(x-5)\left(1-2 x+4 x^{2}-8 x^{3}+\qquad \right) \\
& =x\left(1-2 x+4 x^{2}-8 x^{3}\right)-5\left(1-2 x+4 x^{2}-8 x^{3}+\qquad \right)
\end{align}
$$

Term in $x^{3}$ is $x\left(4 x^{2}\right)+(-5)\left(-8 x^{3}\right)=44 x^{3}$.

∴ The coefficient of $x^{3}$ is 44.

### 7.4 Binomial expansion of $(\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{x})^{\boldsymbol{n}}$ for values of $\boldsymbol{n}$ that are not positive integers

To expand $(a+x)^{n}$, where $n$ is not a positive integer, we take $a$ outside of the brackets:

$$
(a+x)^{n}=a^{n}\left(1+\frac{x}{a}\right)^{n}
$$

Hence the expression is:

## KEY POINT 7.7

$$
(a+x)^{n}=a^{n}\left[1+n\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)^{2}+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)^{3}+\qquad \right] \text { for }\left|\frac{x}{a}\right|<1
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.10

Expand $(2+x)^{-3}$ in ascending powers of $x$, up to and including the term in $x^{3}$ and state the range of values of $x$ for which the expansion is valid.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
(2+x)^{-3} & =(2)^{-3}\left(1+\frac{x}{2}\right)^{-3} \\
& =(2)^{-3}\left[1+(-3)\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)+\frac{(-3)(-4)}{2!}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{(-3)(-4)(-5)}{3!}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)^{3}+\qquad \quad \text { for }\left|\frac{x}{2}\right|<1\right. \\
& =\frac{1}{8}\left[1-\frac{3}{2} x+\frac{3}{2} x^{2}-\frac{5}{4} x^{3}+\qquad \right] \\
& =\frac{1}{8}-\frac{3}{16} x+\frac{3}{16} x^{2}-\frac{5}{32} x^{3}+\qquad \quad \text { valid for }|x|<2
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.11

Find the coefficient of $x^{2}$ in the expansion of $\frac{1-3 x}{\sqrt{4-x}}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{1-3 x}{\sqrt{4-x}} & =(1-3 x)(4-x)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \\
& =(1-3 x)(4)^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(1-\frac{x}{4}\right)^{-\frac{1}{2}} \qquad \text { Factor the } 4 \text { out of }(4-x)^{-\frac{1}{2}} . \\
& =\frac{1}{2}(1-3 x)\left[1+\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{x}{4}\right)+\frac{\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)\left(-\frac{3}{2}\right)}{2!}\left(-\frac{x}{4}\right)^{2}+\qquad \right] \\
& =\frac{1}{2}(1-3 x)\left[1+\frac{1}{8} x+\frac{3}{128} x^{2}+\qquad \right] \\
& =\frac{1}{2}\left[1+\frac{1}{8} x+\frac{3}{128} x^{2}+\qquad \right]-\frac{3}{2} x\left[1+\frac{1}{8} x+\frac{3}{128} x^{2}+\qquad \right]
\end{align}
$$

Coefficient of $x^{2}$ is $\frac{1}{2} \times \frac{3}{128}-\frac{3}{2} \times \frac{1}{8}=-\frac{45}{256}$

### 7.5 Partial fractions and binomial expansions

One of the most common reasons for splitting a fraction into partial fractions is so that binomial expansions can be applied.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 7.12

Given that $\mathrm{f}(x)=\frac{10 x+1}{(1-2 x)(1+x)}$, express f(x) in partial fractions and hence obtain the expansion of $\mathrm{f}(x)$ in ascending powers of $x$, up to and including the term in $x^{3}$. State the range of values of $x$ for which the expansion is valid.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{10 x+1}{(1-2 x)(1+x)} & \equiv \frac{A}{1-2 x}+\frac{B}{1+x} \\
10 x+1 & \equiv A(1+x)+B(1-2 x) \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

Multiply throughout by $(1-2 x)(1+x)$.

Let $x=-1$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
-9 & =3 B \\
B & =-3
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=\frac{1}{2}$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
6 & =\frac{3}{2} A \\
A & =4
\end{align}
$$

$$
\therefore \frac{10 x+1}{(1-2 x)(1+x)} \equiv \frac{4}{1-2 x}-\frac{3}{1+x} \equiv 4(1-2 x)^{-1}-3(1+x)^{-1}
$$

Expanding $(1-2 x)^{-1}$ gives:

$$
\begin{align}
(1-2 x)^{-1} & =1+(-1)(-2 x)+\frac{(-1)(-2)}{2!}(-2 x)^{2}+\frac{(-1)(-2)(-3)}{3!}(-2 x)^{3}+\qquad \\
& =1+2 x+4 x^{2}+8 x^{3}+\qquad \text { valid for }|-2 x|<1 \text { i.e. }-\frac{1}{2}<x<\frac{1}{2}
\end{align}
$$

Expanding $(1+x)^{-1}$ gives:

$$
\begin{align}
(1+x)^{-1} & =1+(-1) x+\frac{(-1)(-2)}{2!} x^{2}+\frac{(-1)(-2)(-3)}{3!} x^{3} \\
& =1-x+x^{2}-x^{3}+\qquad \quad \text { valid for }|x|<1 \quad \text { i.e. }-1<x<1
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\therefore \frac{10 x+1}{(1-2 x)(1+x)} & =4\left(1+2 x+4 x^{2}+8 x^{3}+\qquad \right)-3\left(1-x+x^{2}-x^{3}+\qquad \right) \\
& =1+11 x+13 x^{2}+35 x^{3}+\qquad
\end{align}
$$

The expansion is valid for the range of values of $x$ satisfying both $-\frac{1}{2}<x<\frac{1}{2}$ and $-1<x<1$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-180_140_568_1677_801.jpg)

Hence, the expansion is valid for $-\frac{1}{2}<x<\frac{1}{2}$.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

Partial fractions

- Case 1: If the denominator of a proper algebraic fraction has two distinct linear factors, the fraction can be split into partial fractions using the rule $\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)(c x+d)} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B}{c x+d}$

- Case 2: If the denominator of a proper algebraic fraction has a repeated linear factor, the fraction can be split into partial fractions using the rule $\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)^{2}} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B}{(a x+b)^{2}}$

- Case 3: If the denominator of a proper algebraic fraction has a quadratic factor of the form $c x^{2}+d$ that cannot be factorised, the fraction can be split into partial fractions using the rule

$$
\frac{p x+q}{(a x+b)\left(c x^{2}+d\right)} \equiv \frac{A}{a x+b}+\frac{B x+C}{c x^{2}+d}
$$

- Case 4: If an algebraic fraction is improper we must first express it as the sum of a polynomial and a proper fraction and then split the proper fraction into partial fractions.

Binomial expansion of $(\mathbf{1}+\boldsymbol{x})^{\boldsymbol{n}}$ for values of $\boldsymbol{n}$ that are not positive integers

- $(1+x)^{n}=1+n x+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!} x^{2}+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!} x^{3}+$ for $|x|<1$.

Binomial expansion of $(\mathbf{a}+\boldsymbol{x})^{\boldsymbol{n}}$ for values of $\boldsymbol{n}$ that are not positive integers

- $(a+x)^{n}=a^{n}\left[1+n\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)+\frac{n(n-1)}{2!}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)^{2}+\frac{n(n-1)(n-2)}{3!}\left(\frac{x}{a}\right)^{3}+\qquad \right]$ for $\left|\frac{x}{a}\right|<1$.

## Chapter 8 Further calculus

This chapter is for Pure Mathematics 3 students only.

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- use the derivative of $\tan ^{-1} x$

- extend the ideas of 'reverse differentiation' to include the integration of $\frac{1}{x^{2}+a^{2}}$

- recognise an integrand of the form $\frac{k \mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)}$, and integrate such functions

- use a given substitution to simplify and evaluate either a definite or an indefinite integral

- integrate rational functions by means of decomposition into partial fractions

- recognise when an integrand can usefully be regarded as a product, and use integration by parts.

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

| Where it comes from | What you should be able to do | Check your skills |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Chapter 4 | Differentiate $\mathrm{e}^{x}, \ln x, \sin x, \cos x$ and $\tan x$. | 1 Differentiate with respect to $x$.<br>a $\sin 3 x$<br>b $\mathrm{e}^{x^{2}+1}$<br>c $\quad \ln (5 x-3)$<br>d $\tan 2 x-5 \cos x$<br> |
| Chapter 5 | Integrate $\mathrm{e}^{a x+b}, \frac{1}{a x+b}, \sin (a x+b)$, $\cos (a x+b)$ and $\sec ^{2}(a x+b)$ and use trigonometrical relationships in carrying out integration. | 2 Find each of these integrals.<br>a $\int \mathrm{e}^{5 x+1} \mathrm{~d} x$<br>b $\int \sin ^{2} 2 x \mathrm{~d} x$<br>c $\int \frac{5}{3 x-2} \mathrm{~d} x$<br>d $\quad \int \sec ^{2}(3 x) \mathrm{d} x$<br> |
| Chapter 7 | Express rational functions as partial fractions. | 3 Express in partial fractions.<br>a $\frac{2 x}{(x-1)(x+3)}$<br>b $\frac{4 x^{2}-6}{x\left(x^{2}+2\right)}$<br>c $\frac{5}{x(x-1)^{2}}$<br>d $\frac{3 x^{2}-3 x-3}{x^{2}-x-2}$<br> |

## Why do we study calculus?

This chapter will further extend your knowledge and skills in differentiation and integration.

In particular, we will learn about integration by substitution and integration by parts, which can be considered as the 'reverse process' of the chain rule and the product rule for differentiation that we learnt about earlier in this course. These new rules will enable you to integrate many more functions than you could previously.

The skills that we learn in this chapter will be very important when studying Chapter 10. Chapter 10 is about solving differential equations, many of which model real-life situations.

Integration by parts plays a major role in engineering and its application is often found in problems including electric circuits, heat transfer, vibrations, structures, fluid mechanics, air pollution, electromagnetics and digital signal processing.

### 8.1 Derivative of $\boldsymbol{\operatorname { t a n }}^{-\mathbf{1}} \boldsymbol{x}$

The function $y=\tan ^{-1} x$ can be written as:

$$
\begin{align}
\tan y & =x & & \text { Differentiate both sides with respect to } x . \\
\sec ^{2} y \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =1 & & \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{1}{\sec ^{2} y} & & \text { Use } \sec ^{2} y=1+\tan ^{2} y . \\
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{1}{1+\tan ^{2} y} & & \text { Use } \tan y=x . \\
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\frac{1}{1+x^{2}} & &
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 8.1

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\tan ^{-1} x\right)=\frac{1}{x^{2}+1}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.1

Differentiate with respect to $x$.

**a** $\tan ^{-1} 3 x$

**b** $\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{x}$

**c** $\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{x-2}\right)$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\tan ^{-1} 3 x\right) & =\frac{1}{(3 x)^{2}+1} \times 3 \\
& =\frac{3}{9 x^{2}+1}
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}\left(\tan ^{-1} \sqrt{x}\right) & =\frac{1}{(\sqrt{x})^{2}+1} \times \frac{1}{2} x^{-\frac{1}{2}} \\
& =\frac{1}{x+1} \times \frac{1}{2 \sqrt{x}} \\
& =\frac{1}{2 \sqrt{x}(x+1)}
\end{align}
$$

**c** $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\left[\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{x-2}\right)\right]=\frac{1}{\left(\frac{x}{x-2}\right)^{2}+1} \times \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}\left(\frac{x}{x-2}\right)$ Chain rule and quotient rule.

$$
\begin{align}
& =\frac{(x-2)^{2}}{x^{2}+(x-2)^{2}} \times \frac{(x-2)(1)-(x)(1)}{(x-2)^{2}} \qquad \text { Simplify. } \\
& =\frac{-2}{2 x^{2}-4 x+4} \\
& =\frac{-1}{x^{2}-2 x+2}
\end{align}
$$

### 8.2 Integration of $\frac{\mathbf{1}}{\boldsymbol{x}^{\mathbf{2}}+\boldsymbol{a}^{\mathbf{2}}}$

Since integration is the reverse process of differentiation we can say that:

KEY POINT 8.2

$$
\int \frac{1}{x^{2}+1} \mathrm{~d} x=\tan ^{-1} x+c
$$

This can be extended to:

$$
\int \frac{1}{x^{2}+a^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{a} \tan ^{-1} \frac{x}{a}+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.2

Find $\int \frac{1}{x^{2}+25} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\int \frac{1}{x^{2}+25} \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{1}{5} \tan ^{-1} \frac{x}{5}+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.3

Find $\int \frac{1}{2 x^{2}+3} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\int \frac{1}{2 x^{2}+3} \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{1}{2} \int \frac{1}{x^{2}+\frac{3}{2}} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \times \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}} \tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{\sqrt{\frac{3}{2}}}\right)+c \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \sqrt{\frac{2}{3}} \tan ^{-1}\left(\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}} x\right)+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.4

Find the exact value of $\int_{0}^{2} \frac{1}{x^{2}+4} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{0}^{2} \frac{1}{x^{2}+4} \mathrm{~d} x & =\left[\frac{1}{2} \tan ^{-1} \frac{x}{2}\right]_{0}^{2} \\
& =\left(\frac{1}{2} \tan ^{-1} 1\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2} \tan ^{-1} 0\right) \\
& =\left(\frac{1}{2} \times \frac{\pi}{4}\right)-\left(\frac{1}{2} \times 0\right) \\
& =\frac{\pi}{8}
\end{align}
$$

### 8.3 Integration of $\frac{\boldsymbol{k} \mathbf{f}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x})}{\mathbf{f}(\boldsymbol{x})}$

Since integration is the reverse process of differentiation and $\ln x$ only exists for $x>0$, we can say that:

KEY POINT 8.3

$$
\int \frac{\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)} \mathrm{d} x=\ln |\mathrm{f}(x)|+c
$$

This can be extended to:

$$
\int \frac{k \mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)} \mathrm{d} x=k \ln |\mathrm{f}(x)|+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.5

Find $\int \frac{2 x}{x^{2}+3} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

If $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{2}+3$ then $\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)=2 x$.

$$
\therefore \int \frac{2 x}{x^{2}+3} \mathrm{~d} x=\ln \left|x^{2}+3\right|+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.6

Find $\int \tan 3 x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\int \tan 3 x d x=\int \frac{\sin 3 x}{\cos 3 x} d x
$$

If $\mathrm{f}(x)=\cos 3 x$ then $\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)=-3 \sin 3 x$.

$$
\begin{align}
\int \tan 3 x \mathrm{~d} x & =-\frac{1}{3} \int \frac{-3 \sin 3 x}{\cos 3 x} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =-\frac{1}{3} \ln |\cos 3 x|+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.7

Find $\int \frac{3 x^{2}+5 x}{2 x^{3}+5 x^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

If $\mathrm{f}(x)=2 x^{3}+5 x^{2}$ then $\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)=6 x^{2}+10 x$.

$$
\begin{align}
\int \frac{3 x^{2}+5 x}{2 x^{3}+5 x^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{1}{2} \int \frac{6 x^{2}+10 x}{2 x^{3}+5 x^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2} \ln \left|2 x^{3}+5 x^{2}\right|+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.8

Find $\int_{0}^{1} \frac{2 \mathrm{e}^{x}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{x}} \mathrm{~d} x$

Answer

If $\mathrm{f}(x)=1+\mathrm{e}^{x}$ then $\mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x}$.

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{0}^{1} \frac{2 \mathrm{e}^{x}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{x}} \mathrm{~d} x & =2 \int_{0}^{1} \frac{\mathrm{e}^{x}}{1+\mathrm{e}^{x}} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\left[2 \ln \left|1+\mathrm{e}^{x}\right|\right]_{0}^{1} \\
& =2 \ln (1+\mathrm{e})-2 \ln 2 \\
& =2 \ln \left(\frac{1+\mathrm{e}}{2}\right)
\end{align}
$$

The diagram shows part of the curve $y=\frac{4 x}{x^{2}+1}$. Find the exact value of $p$ for which the shaded region has an area of 4 .

### 8.4 Integration by substitution

Integration by substitution can be considered as the reverse process of differentiation by the chain rule.

This method is used when a simple substitution can be applied that will transform a difficult integral into an easier integral. The integral must be completely rewritten in terms of the new variable.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.9

Use the substitution $u=2 x+1$ to find $\int 4 x \sqrt{2 x+1} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& u=2 x+1 \Rightarrow x=\frac{u-1}{2} \\
& u=2 x+1 \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=2 \Rightarrow \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{\mathrm{d} u}{2}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\int 4 x \sqrt{2 x+1} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int 4\left(\frac{u-1}{2}\right) \sqrt{u} \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{2} \\
& =\int\left(u^{\frac{3}{2}}-u^{\frac{1}{2}}\right) \mathrm{d} u \qquad \text { Sewrite the integral in terms of } u \text { and simplify. } \\
& =\frac{2}{5} u^{\frac{5}{2}}-\frac{2}{3} u^{\frac{3}{2}}+c \\
& =\frac{2}{5}(2 x+1)^{\frac{5}{2}}-\frac{2}{3}(2 x+1)^{\frac{3}{2}}+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.10

Use the substitution $x=2 \tan u$ to find $\int \frac{8}{x^{2}+4} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
x=2 \tan u & \Rightarrow x^{2}=4 \tan ^{2} u \\
x=2 \tan u & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} x}{\mathrm{~d} u}=2 \sec ^{2} u \Rightarrow \mathrm{~d} x=2 \sec ^{2} u \mathrm{~d} u \\
\int \frac{8}{x^{2}+4} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int\left(\frac{8}{4 \tan ^{2} u+4}\right) 2 \sec ^{2} u \mathrm{~d} u \quad \text { Rewrite the integral in terms of } u \text { and simplify. } \\
& =\int \frac{16 \sec ^{2} u}{4 \tan ^{2} u+4} \mathrm{~d} u \quad \text { Se } \tan ^{2} u+1 \equiv \sec ^{2} u . \\
& =\int \frac{16 \sec ^{2} u}{4 \sec ^{2} u} \mathrm{~d} u \quad \text { Simplify. } \\
& =\int 4 \mathrm{~d} u \\
& =4 u+c \\
& =4 \tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.11

Use the substitution $u=\sin x$ to find $\int \sin ^{2} 2 x \cos x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
u=\sin x \Rightarrow \sin ^{2} 2 x & =(2 \sin x \cos x)^{2} \\
& =4 \sin ^{2} x \cos ^{2} x \\
& =4 u^{2}(1-u)^{2} \\
u=\sin x \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}= & \cos x \Rightarrow \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\cos x}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\int \sin ^{2} 2 x \cos x \mathrm{~d} x & =\int \sin ^{2} 2 x \cos x \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\cos x} \quad \text { Replace } \mathrm{d} x \text { by } \frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\cos x} \text { and simplify. } \\
& =\int \sin ^{2} 2 x \mathrm{~d} u \\
& =\int\left(4 u^{2}-4 u^{4}\right) \mathrm{d} u \qquad \text { Replace } \sin ^{2} 2 x \text { by } 4 u^{2}-4 u^{4} . \\
& =\frac{4}{3} u^{3}-\frac{4}{5} u^{5}+c \qquad \text { Integrate with respect to } u . \\
& =\frac{4}{3} \sin ^{3} x-\frac{4}{5} \sin ^{5} x+c \quad \text { Rewrite in terms of } x .
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.12

Use the substitution $u=x+1$ to find $\int_{0}^{3} \frac{x}{\sqrt{x+1}} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& u=x+1 \Rightarrow x=u-1 \\
& u=x+1 \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=1 \Rightarrow \mathrm{~d} x=\mathrm{d} u
\end{align}
$$

Find the new limits for $u$ :

$$
\begin{align}
& x=3 \Rightarrow u=3+1=4 \\
& x=0 \Rightarrow u=0+1=1
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\int_{x=0}^{x=3} \frac{x}{\sqrt{x+1}} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int_{u=1}^{u=4} \frac{u-1}{\sqrt{u}} \mathrm{~d} u \\
& =\int_{1}^{4}\left(u^{\frac{1}{2}}-u^{-\frac{1}{2}}\right) \mathrm{d} u \qquad \text { Write the integral in terms of } u \text { and simplify } \\
& =\left[\frac{2}{3} u^{\frac{3}{2}}-2 u^{\frac{1}{2}}\right]_{1}^{4} \\
& =\left(\frac{16}{3}-4\right)-\left(\frac{2}{3}-2\right) \\
& =\frac{8}{3}
\end{align}
$$

### 8.5 The use of partial fractions in integration

EXPLORE 8.1

Consider $\int \frac{2}{x^{2}-1} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Try using some of the integration techniques that you have learnt so far to find this integral and discuss what happens with your classmates.

In the integral $\int \frac{2}{x^{2}-1} \mathrm{~d} x$, the denominator $x^{2}-1$ can be factorised to $(x-1)(x+1)$.

Hence the fraction $\frac{2}{x^{2}-1}$ can be split into partial fractions.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{2}{x^{2}-1} & \equiv \frac{A}{x-1}+\frac{B}{x+1} \quad \text { Multiply throughout by }(x-1)(x+1) \\
2 & \equiv A(x+1)+B(x-1) \text {...... (1) } \tag{1}
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=1$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
& 2=2 A \\
& A=1
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=-1$ in equation (1):

$$
\begin{align}
2 & =-2 B \\
B & =-1 \\
\therefore \frac{2}{x^{2}-1} & \equiv \frac{1}{x-1}-\frac{1}{x+1}
\end{align}
$$

Hence

$$
\begin{align}
\int \frac{2}{x^{2}-1} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int\left(\frac{1}{x-1}-\frac{1}{x+1}\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\ln |x-1|-\ln |x+1|+c \\
& =\ln \left|\frac{x-1}{x+1}\right|+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.13

Find $\int \frac{3-x}{(x+1)\left(x^{2}+3\right)} \mathrm{d} x$.

Answer

First split into partial fractions.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{3-x}{(x+1)\left(x^{2}+3\right)} & \equiv \frac{A}{x+1}+\frac{B x+C}{x^{2}+3} \\
3-x & \equiv A\left(x^{2}+3\right)+B x(x+1)+C(x+1)
\end{align}
$$

Let $x=-1 \quad 4=4 A \quad \Rightarrow A=1$

Let $x=0 \quad 3=3 A+C \quad \Rightarrow C=0$

Let $x=1 \quad 2=4 A+2 B+2 C \quad \Rightarrow B=-1$

$$
\begin{align}
\therefore \int \frac{3-x}{(x+1)\left(x^{2}+3\right)} \mathrm{d} x & =\int\left(\frac{1}{x+1}-\frac{x}{x^{2}+3}\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& =\int \frac{1}{x+1} \mathrm{~d} x-\frac{1}{2} \int \frac{2 x}{x^{2}+3} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\ln |x+1|-\frac{1}{2} \ln \left|x^{2}+3\right|+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.14

Find $\int \frac{9}{(x+2)(x-1)^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

First split into partial fractions.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{9}{(x+2)(x-1)^{2}} & \equiv \frac{A}{x+2}+\frac{B}{x-1}+\frac{C}{(x-1)^{2}} \\
9 & \equiv A(x-1)^{2}+B(x+2)(x-1)+C(x+2)
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& \text { Let } x=1 \quad \begin{array}{ll} 9=3 C & \Rightarrow C=3 \\
\text { Let } x=-2 & 9=9 A \end{array} \quad \begin{array}{l} x=A=1 \\
9=A-2 B+2 C \quad \\
\therefore \int \frac{9}{(x+2)(x-1)^{2}} \mathrm{~d} x=\int\left(\frac{1}{x+2}-\frac{1}{x-1}+\frac{3}{(x-1)^{2}}\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
\quad=\int\left(\frac{1}{x+2}-\frac{1}{x-1}+3(x-1)^{-2}\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
\quad=\ln |x+2|-\ln |x-1|-3(x-1)^{-1}+c \\
\quad=\ln \left|\frac{x+2}{x-1}\right|-\frac{3}{x-1}+c \end{array}
\end{align}
$$

### 8.6 Integration by parts

Consider $\int x \mathrm{e}^{2 x} \mathrm{~d} x$. The function to be integrated is clearly the product of two simpler functions $x$ and $\mathrm{e}^{2 x}$. If we try using a substitution to simplify the integral we will find that it does not help. To integrate this function we require a new technique.

Integrating both sides of the equation for the product rule for differentiation with respect to $x$ and using the fact that integration is the reverse process of differentiation we can say that:

$$
u v=\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x+\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x
$$

Rearranging gives the formula for integration by parts:

KEY POINT 8.4

$$
\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x
$$

The following Worked examples show how to use this formula.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.15

Find $\int x \mathrm{e}^{2 x} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& u=x \quad \Rightarrow \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=1 \\
& \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{e}^{2 x} \Rightarrow \quad v=\frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{2 x}
\end{align}
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$ :

$$
\begin{align}
\int x \mathrm{e}^{2 x} \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{1}{2} x \mathrm{e}^{2 x}-\int \frac{1}{2} \mathrm{e}^{2 x} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2} x \mathrm{e}^{2 x}-\frac{1}{4} \mathrm{e}^{2 x}+c
\end{align}
$$

It is important to choose our values for $u$ and $\frac{\mathrm{d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ carefully.

Consider trying to find $\int x \ln x \mathrm{~d} x$. If we let $u=x$ and $\frac{\mathrm{d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\ln x$, then finding $\frac{\mathrm{d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ is easy but we do not know how to integrate $\ln x$ to find $v$. Hence we must let $u=\ln x$ and $\frac{\mathrm{d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=x$. This is demonstrated in Worked example 8.16.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.16

Find $\int x \ln x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{array}{ll} u=\ln x \Rightarrow & \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{1}{x} \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=x \Rightarrow & v=\frac{1}{2} x^{2} \end{array}
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$ :

$$
\begin{align}
\int x \ln x \mathrm{~d} x & =\frac{1}{2} x^{2} \ln x-\int \frac{1}{2} x^{2} \cdot \frac{1}{x} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2} x^{2} \ln x-\int \frac{1}{2} x \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =\frac{1}{2} x^{2} \ln x-\frac{1}{4} x^{2}+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.17

Find $\int \ln x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

First write $\int \ln x \mathrm{~d} x$ as $\int 1 \cdot \ln x \mathrm{~d} x$.

$$
u=\ln x \Rightarrow \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{1}{x}
$$

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=1 \Rightarrow \quad v=x
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$ :

$$
\begin{align}
\int \ln x \mathrm{~d} x & =x \ln x-\int \frac{1}{x} \cdot x \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =x \ln x-\int 1 \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =x \ln x-x+c
\end{align}
$$

Sometimes we might need to use integration by parts more than once to integrate a function.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.18

Find $\int x^{2} \sin x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{array}{cl} u=x^{2} & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=2 x \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\sin x & \Rightarrow v=-\cos x \end{array}
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$

$$
\begin{align}
\int x^{2} \sin x \mathrm{~d} x & =-x^{2} \cos x-\int-2 x \cos x \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =-x^{2} \cos x+\int 2 x \cos x \mathrm{~d} x
\end{align}
$$

Use integration by parts a second time to find $\int 2 x \cos x \mathrm{~d} x$

$$
\begin{array}{cl} u=2 x & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=2 \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\cos x & \Rightarrow v=\sin x \end{array}
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$

$$
\begin{align}
\int 2 x \cos x \mathrm{~d} x & =2 x \sin x-\int 2 \sin x \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =2 x \sin x+2 \cos x+c
\end{align}
$$

Substituting in (1) gives:

$$
\int x^{2} \sin x d x=-x^{2} \cos x+2 x \sin x+2 \cos x+c
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.19

Given that $I=\int \frac{\ln x}{x} \mathrm{~d} x$, use integration by parts to show that $I=\frac{1}{2}(\ln x)^{2}+c$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
u=\ln x & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{1}{x} \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{1}{x} & \Rightarrow v=\ln x
\end{align}
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$

$$
\begin{align}
I & =(\ln x)^{2}-\int \frac{\ln x}{x} \mathrm{~d} x \qquad \mathrm{C}^{2} \qquad \text { Rearrange. } \\
2 I & =(\ln x)^{2}+k \\
I & =\frac{1}{2}(\ln x)^{2}+\frac{k}{2} \\
\therefore I & =\frac{1}{2}(\ln x)^{2}+c
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 8.20

Find the exact value of $\int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2} \pi} 4 x \sin 2 x \mathrm{~d} x$.

Answer

$$
\begin{array}{cl} u=4 x & \Rightarrow \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x}=4 \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\sin 2 x & \Rightarrow v=-\frac{1}{2} \cos 2 x \end{array}
$$

Substitute into $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$

$$
\begin{align}
\int 4 x \sin 2 x \mathrm{~d} x & =-2 x \cos 2 x+\int 2 \cos 2 x \mathrm{~d} x \\
& =-2 x \cos 2 x+\sin 2 x+c
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\therefore \int_{0}^{\frac{1}{2} \pi} 4 x \sin 2 x \mathrm{~d} x & =[-2 x \cos 2 x+\sin 2 x]_{0}^{\frac{1}{2} \pi} \\
& =(-\pi \cos \pi+\sin \pi)-(0+\sin 0) \\
& =\pi
\end{align}
$$

### 8.7 Further integration

Some integrals can be found using different integration techniques.

The activities in Explore 8.2 look at why our answers to an indefinite integral might 'appear' to be different when using different integration techniques when they are actually equivalent.

EXPLORE 8.2

**1** Nadia and Richard are asked to find $\int x(x-2)^{7} \mathrm{~d} x$.

Nadia uses the substitution $u=x-2$ and her answer is: $\frac{1}{9}(x-2)^{9}+\frac{1}{4}(x-2)^{8}+c$ Richard uses integration by parts and his answer is: $\quad \frac{1}{8} x(x-2)^{8}-\frac{1}{72}(x-2)^{9}+c$

Use each of their methods to show that they are both correct and then use algebra to explain why their answers are equivalent.

**2** **a** Find $\int \frac{4 x+1}{2 x^{2}+x-1} \mathrm{~d} x$ using:

i the substitution $u=2 x^{2}+x-1$

ii partial fractions.

**b** Suggest another method for finding $\int \frac{4 x+1}{2 x^{2}+x-1} \mathrm{~d} x$.

**c** Use algebra to show that your two answers to part a are equivalent.

Explore 8.3 is designed to make you think about the different integration techniques that might be available to you for a particular integral.

This activity expects you to be able to recognise the appropriate substitution to use when integrating by substitution.

Discuss with your classmates which integration methods can be used to find each of these integrals.

Find each integral.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

Integrating $\frac{\boldsymbol{k} \mathbf{f}^{\prime}(\boldsymbol{x})}{\mathbf{f}(\boldsymbol{x})}$

- $\int \frac{k \mathrm{f}^{\prime}(x)}{\mathrm{f}(x)} \mathrm{d} x=k \ln |\mathrm{f}(x)|+c$

Integration by substitution

- Substitutions can sometimes be used to simplify the form of a function so that its integral can be easily recognised. When using a substitution, we must ensure that the integral is completely rewritten in terms of the new variable before integrating.

Integrating rational functions

- Some rational functions can be split into partial fractions that can then be integrated.

Integration by parts

- $\int u \frac{\mathrm{~d} v}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=u v-\int v \frac{\mathrm{~d} u}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$

## Chapter 9 Vectors

This chapter is for Pure Mathematics 3 students only.

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- use standard notations for vectors, in two dimensions and three dimensions

- add and subtract vectors, multiply a vector by a scalar and interpret these operations geometrically

- calculate the magnitude of a vector and find and use unit vectors

- use displacement vectors and position vectors

- find the vector equation of a line

- find whether two lines are parallel, intersect or are skew

- find the common point of two intersecting lines

- find and use the scalar product of two vectors.

## PREREQUISITE KNOWLEDGE

Why are vectors important?

Vectors are used to model many situations in the world around us. They are used by engineers to ensure that buildings are designed safely. They are used in navigation to ensure that planes and boats, for example, arrive at their destinations in differing winds or water currents. There are countless examples of the use of vectors to model the movement of objects in mathematics and physics. Displacement, velocity and acceleration are all examples of vector quantities. Vectors are essential to describing the position and location of objects. A boat lost at sea can be found if its bearing and distance from each of two fixed points is known. These are just a few examples. Our world is 3-dimensional, as far as we know, and in this chapter we will be studying vectors in two and three dimensions. As the mathematics of vectors can be applied to all vectors, the study of vector geometry and algebra in general is important.

It is helpful if you have a good understanding of simple coordinate geometry before you start working with vectors because you will be able to make comparisons between the coordinate geometry that you have previously studied and the vector geometry introduced in this chapter.

Recall that a vector has both magnitude (size) and direction.

You should have some knowledge of how to work with vectors in 2-dimensional space from your previous studies. You will be reminded of this here, before being introduced to vectors in 3-dimensional space.

### 9.1 Displacement or translation vectors

A movement through a certain distance and in a given direction is called a displacement or a translation. The magnitude or length of a displacement represents the distance moved.

Examples of displacement or translation vectors

- Sami walks 100 m in a straight line on a bearing of 045° from place $A$ to place $B$. Jan is also standing at place $A$. Jan walks directly to Sami. Jan therefore also walks for 100 m on a bearing of 045° from place $A$. Jan follows the same displacement vector as Sami.

- The diagram shows two arrows.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-211_685_610_1770_402.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-211_429_313_1884_1147.jpg)

The arrow from $L$ to $M$ is the same length and has been drawn in the same direction as the arrow from $P$ to $Q$.

This means that the points $L$ and $P$ have been displaced or translated by the same vector to $M$ and $Q$, respectively.

The arrow $L M$ and the arrow $P Q$ represent the same displacement.

## Notation using upper case

A displacement from $L$ to $M$ is written as $\overrightarrow{L M}$.

The order of the letters is important as $\overrightarrow{M L}$ would represent a displacement from $M$ to $L$. The arrow above the upper case letters always goes from left to right. Reverse the letters to show the opposite direction.

In the previous diagram, $\overrightarrow{L M}=\overrightarrow{P Q}$. Even though the arrows are drawn on different parts of the page, the displacement shown by each is the same.

## Components of a displacement in two dimensions

Any displacement can be represented by the displacement in the $x$-direction followed by the displacement in the $y$-direction. These are called the components of the displacement.

In the following example, the displacement vector $\overrightarrow{A B}=\binom{3}{1}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-212_308_433_1276_181.jpg)

**3** is the $x$-component and 1 is the $y$-component of the displacement. As 3 and 1 are both positive, the movement is to the right and up, respectively.

## Components of a displacement in three dimensions

In a 3-dimensional system of coordinates, a point $P$ is located using three coordinates, $(x, y, z)$. The axis for the third coordinate, the $z$-coordinate, intersects with the $x$ - and $y$-axes at the origin and the three axes are perpendicular to each other. The axes may be rotated, as shown.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-212_429_501_1899_220.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-212_411_484_1913_833.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-212_327_351_1944_1434.jpg)

A displacement vector in a 3-dimensional system needs to have three components.

For example, the point $P(1,3,7)$ translates to the point $Q(5,3,8)$ by the displacement vector $\overrightarrow{P Q}=\left(\begin{array}{l}4 \\ 0 \\ 1\end{array}\right)$.

## Notation using lower case

Sometimes lower case letters are used for vectors. In text, these are in bold type.

For example, a or b.

When written with a pen or pencil, they should be underlined with a bar or a short, wavy line instead. For example, a or $\underset{\sim}{b}$. Sometimes the bar or wavy line is written over the top of the lower case letter.

This diagram shows three vectors, $\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{b}$ and $\mathbf{c}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-213_207_683_681_569.jpg)

The vectors a and b have the same direction but not the same magnitude or length. Vectors a and b are parallel but not equal. In fact, a is half the length of b. When the length of a is doubled, the vectors are equal. In other words $\mathbf{b}=2 \mathbf{a}$. When a is scaled so that it is the same length as b, then the vectors are equal.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-213_223_452_1119_691.jpg)

Vector c is $\frac{3}{4}$ of the length of vector b, but it has opposite direction.

In other words $\mathbf{c}=-\frac{3}{4} \mathbf{b}$.

A quantity (usually a number) used to scale a vector is called a scalar. So in '2a' the vector a is being multiplied by the scalar 2.

## Parallel vectors

Two vectors are therefore parallel provided that one is a scalar multiple of the other.

For example, when $\mathbf{a}=\lambda \mathbf{b}$, where $\lambda$ is some scalar, then $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$ are parallel.

## Combining displacements (adding and subtracting)

Starting at point $A$, make the displacement $\overrightarrow{A B}=\binom{4}{2}$ followed by the displacement $\overrightarrow{B C}=\binom{3}{7}$.

What is the result?

The overall displacement shown by $\overrightarrow{A C}$, is $\binom{7}{9}$. This is usually called the resultant vector. resultant vector.

- The $x$-component of the resultant vector is easily found by adding the $x$-components of $\overrightarrow{A B}$ and $\overrightarrow{B C}$.

- The $y$-component of the resultant vector is easily found by adding the $y$-components of $\overrightarrow{A B}$ and $\overrightarrow{B C}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-213_536_499_1899_1531.jpg)

This operation is called vector addition.

KEY POINT 9.1

The formula for vector addition:

$$
\overrightarrow{A B}+\overrightarrow{B C}=\overrightarrow{A C}
$$

The displacement $\overrightarrow{A C}$ followed by the displacement $\overrightarrow{C A}$ must return you to point $A$.

In other words $\overrightarrow{A C}+\overrightarrow{C A}=\binom{0}{0}$, as there is no overall displacement.

$\binom{7}{9}+\binom{-7}{-9}=\binom{0}{0}$, so $\overrightarrow{C A}=\binom{-7}{-9}$.

As -7 and -9 are both negative, the movement is to the left and down, respectively.

Alternatively, we may write $\binom{7}{9}-\binom{7}{9}=\binom{0}{0}$. In other words $\overrightarrow{A C}-\overrightarrow{A C}=\binom{0}{0}$.

It is reasonable, therefore, to state that $\overrightarrow{C A}=-\overrightarrow{A C}$.

KEY POINT 9.2

$\overrightarrow{C A}=-\overrightarrow{A C}$ is always true.

Taking the negative of a vector changes the direction of the vector and does not change its length.

Vectors $\overrightarrow{A C}$ and $\overrightarrow{C A}$ have opposite direction.

As $\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}$ is the same as $\mathbf{a}+-\mathbf{b}$, vector subtraction is defined as the addition of the negative of a vector. The displacement $\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}$ is therefore the displacement $\mathbf{a}$ followed by the displacement -b.

EXPLORE 9.1

a, b and c are displacement vectors such that $\mathbf{c}=\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}$.

Using diagrams to help you, show that $\mathbf{a}=\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{b}$ and that $\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a}$.

KEY POINT 9.3

$\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a}$ means that the addition of vectors is commutative.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.1

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-215_242_558_390_472.jpg)

$P Q R S$ is a quadrilateral. $M$ is the midpoint of $R S$.

$$
\overrightarrow{P Q}=\mathbf{a}, \overrightarrow{P S}=\mathbf{b} \text { and } \overrightarrow{P M}=\frac{1}{4}(2 \mathbf{a}+5 \mathbf{b}) .
$$

**a** Find an expression in terms of $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$ for $\overrightarrow{S M}$.

Give your answer in its simplest form.

**b** Find an expression for $\overrightarrow{Q R}$.

**c** Describe quadrilateral $P Q R S$, justifying your answer.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
& \overrightarrow{S M}=\overrightarrow{S P}+\overrightarrow{P M} \\
& \overrightarrow{S M}=-\overrightarrow{P S}+\overrightarrow{P M} \text { or } \overrightarrow{P M}-\overrightarrow{P S} \qquad ^{} \qquad ^{} \text { Substitute } \overrightarrow{S P}=-\overrightarrow{P S} . \\
& \overrightarrow{S M}=\frac{1}{4}(2 \mathbf{a}+5 \mathbf{b})-\mathbf{b} \\
& \overrightarrow{S M}=\frac{1}{2} \mathbf{a}+\frac{5}{4} \mathbf{b}-\mathbf{b}=\frac{1}{2} \mathbf{a}+\frac{1}{4} \mathbf{b}
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
& \overrightarrow{Q R}=\overrightarrow{Q P}+\overrightarrow{P S}+\overrightarrow{S R} \\
& \overrightarrow{Q R}=-\overrightarrow{P Q}+\overrightarrow{P S}+2 \overrightarrow{S M} \\
& \overrightarrow{Q R}=-\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}+2\left(\frac{1}{2} \mathbf{a}+\frac{1}{4} \mathbf{b}\right) \qquad _{\text {Use }} \overrightarrow{S R}=2 \overrightarrow{S M} \text { and } \overrightarrow{Q P}=-\overrightarrow{P Q} \\
& \overrightarrow{Q R}=\frac{3}{2} \mathbf{b}
\end{align}
$$

**c** $\overrightarrow{Q R}=\frac{3}{2} \overrightarrow{P S}$ so the lines $Q R$ and $P S$ are parallel and $P Q R S$ is a trapezium.

## Magnitude of a vector

The magnitude of a vector is the length of the line segment representing the vector. This is found using the components of the displacement and Pythagoras' theorem.

Notation: The magnitude of a vector $\mathbf{p}$ is written as $|\mathbf{p}|$. This is also known as the modulus of the vector.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.2

**a** Find the magnitude of each of the vectors $\mathbf{a}=\binom{4}{3}, \mathbf{b}=\binom{-3}{4}$ and $\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}$.

**b** $\mathbf{p}=\left(\begin{array}{l}5 \\ 0 \\ 8\end{array}\right)$ and $\mathbf{q}=\left(\begin{array}{r}-1 \\ 8 \\ -3\end{array}\right)$. Find the magnitude of the vector $\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}$.

Answer

**a** ![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-216_324_398_724_323.jpg) $|\mathbf{a}|=\sqrt{4^{2}+3^{2}}=\sqrt{25}=5$ Apply Pythagoras' theorem.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-216_388_302_1147_321.jpg)

$|\mathbf{b}|=\sqrt{(-3)^{2}+4^{2}}=\sqrt{25}=5$

Apply Pythagoras' theorem.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-216_387_630_1650_325.jpg)

The diagram has been given to help you to see the components of the vector $\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}$.

$$
|\mathbf{a}-\mathbf{b}|=\left|\binom{7}{-1}\right|=\sqrt{7^{2}+(-1)^{2}}=\sqrt{49+1}=\sqrt{50}=5 \sqrt{2}
$$

Apply Pythagoras' theorem. (Notice that $|\mathrm{a}-\mathrm{b}|$ is not the same as $|\mathrm{a}|-|\mathrm{b}|$.)

**b** $\mathbf{p}+\mathbf{q}=\left(\begin{array}{l}5 \\ 0 \\ 8\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}{r}-1 \\ 8 \\ -3\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{l}4 \\ 8 \\ 5\end{array}\right)$ and

It is not a good idea to try to draw a diagram here.

$$
\left|\left(\begin{array}{l} 4 \\
8 \\
5 \end{array}\right)\right|=\sqrt{4^{2}+8^{2}+5^{2}}=\sqrt{105}
$$

Apply Pythagoras' theorem in 3 dimensions.

## Unit vectors

A unit vector is any vector of magnitude or length 1.

Notation: $\hat{\mathbf{a}}$ (read as a hat) is the unit vector in the direction of $\mathbf{a}$.

To find a unit displacement vector in a particular direction:

- find the magnitude of the displacement

- then divide the components of the vector by the magnitude.

This means that, for a vectorp $\mathbf{p}=\left(\begin{array}{c}p_{1} \\ p_{2} \\ p_{3}\end{array}\right)$, the corresponding unit vector is $\hat{\mathbf{p}}=\left(\begin{array}{c}\overline{|\mathbf{p}|} \\ \frac{p_{2}}{|\mathbf{p}|} \\ \frac{p_{3}}{|\mathbf{p}|}\end{array}\right)$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.3

Find the unit vector in the direction $\binom{\sqrt{5}}{2}$.

Answer

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-217_335_354_1298_263.jpg)

$$
\left|\binom{\sqrt{5}}{2}\right|=\sqrt{(\sqrt{5})^{2}+2^{2}}=\sqrt{9}=3
$$

Unit vector in the direction $\binom{\sqrt{5}}{2}$ is $\binom{\frac{\sqrt{5}}{3}}{\frac{2}{3}} \qquad c^{2} \qquad c_{\text {mide }}$. Divide component by the magnitude.

Find the unit displacement vector in the direction $\overrightarrow{A C}$.

### 9.2 Position vectors

A position vector is simply a means of locating a point in space relative to an origin, usually $O$. This means a position vector is fixed in space. Position vectors are sometimes called location vectors.

Up to this point, the displacement vectors we have been working with have been free vectors. This means they are not fixed to any particular starting point or origin. Many free vectors can represent the same displacement.

Position vectors are different because they start at a given point or origin. This fixed starting point means there is only one arrow that represents each vector.

Since we can apply the same mathematics to all vectors, the results derived for displacement vectors (free vectors) also apply to position vectors.

The fixed starting point is often called $O$, as in the origin of a system of Cartesian coordinates.

So a point with coordinates $A(1,2,3)$ can be represented by the position vector $\overrightarrow{O A}=\left(\begin{array}{l}1 \\ 2 \\ 3\end{array}\right)$.

## Position vector of any point in a plane

Consider this case in 2-dimensional space.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-220_228_478_1314_355.jpg)

Given that $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$ are position vectors relative to the origin $O$ and that $P$ is any other point in the same plane as a and b, then the position vector of $P$ can be written using a and b.

This is because $\overrightarrow{O P}$ is some scalar multiple of a plus some scalar multiple of $\mathbf{b}$ :

$$
\overrightarrow{O P}=m \mathbf{a}+n \mathbf{b} \text {, where } m \text { and } n \text { are scalars. }
$$

All the other vectors in this plane can be written using combinations of the vectors a and b.

## Cartesian components

When the vectors a and b are

- perpendicular to each other and

- unit vectors

the plane looks like the usual set of $x$ - and $y$-axes (called the $O x y$ plane) we use for Cartesian coordinates.

In this case, the unit vector in the $x$-direction is called i and the unit vector in the $y$-direction is called j.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-221_379_378_277_395.jpg)

$$
\overrightarrow{O P}=x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j} \text {, where } x \text { and } y \text { are scalars. }
$$

In this case, $\overrightarrow{O P}$ is some scalar multiple of $\mathbf{i}$ plus some scalar multiple of $\mathbf{j}$ :

The point $P$ with coordinates $(4,5)$ has position vector $\binom{4}{5}$, which can also be written as $4 \mathbf{i}+5 \mathbf{j}$. $4 \mathbf{i}+5 \mathbf{j}$.

This can be extended to three dimensions.

The unit vector in the $z$-direction is called k.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-221_306_425_1195_395.jpg)

In this case, $\overrightarrow{O Q}$ is some scalar multiple of $\mathbf{i}$ plus some scalar multiple of $\mathbf{j}$ plus some scalar multiple of k:

$$
\overrightarrow{O Q}=x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j}+z \mathbf{k}, \text { where } x, y \text { and } z \text { are scalars. }
$$

The point $Q$ with coordinates $(4,2,3)$ has position vector $\left(\begin{array}{l}4 \\ 2 \\ 3\end{array}\right)$, which can also be written as $4 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k}$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.5

The position vectors of the points $A, B$ and $C$ are given by $\mathbf{a}, \mathbf{b}$ and $\mathbf{c}$, respectively, where

$$
\mathbf{a}=\left(\begin{array}{r} 2 \\
5 \\
-3 \end{array}\right), \mathbf{b}=\left(\begin{array}{l} 0 \\
4 \\
6 \end{array}\right) \text { and } \mathbf{c}=\left(\begin{array}{c} -4 \\
2 \\
24 \end{array}\right) .
$$

**a** Write a,

**b** and

**c** in terms of i, j and k.

b Giving your answers in terms of i, j and k, find the vectors representing:

**i** $\overrightarrow{A B}$

**ii** $\overrightarrow{A C}$

c Describe the relationship between the points $A, B$ and $C$, justifying your answer.

Answer

**a** $\mathbf{a}=2 \mathbf{i}+5 \mathbf{j}-3 \mathbf{k}, \mathbf{b}=4 \mathbf{j}+6 \mathbf{k}$ and $\mathbf{c}=-4 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+24 \mathbf{k}$.

**b**

**i** A simple sketch should help you to calculate the answer correctly.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-222_178_275_454_1241.jpg)

$$
\begin{align}
& \overrightarrow{A B}=\overrightarrow{A O}+\overrightarrow{O B}=\overrightarrow{O B}-\overrightarrow{O A} \\
& \overrightarrow{A B}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{a}=4 \mathbf{j}+6 \mathbf{k}-(2 \mathbf{i}+5 \mathbf{j}-3 \mathbf{k}) \qquad \omega^{\mathbf{C}}-\qquad \text { Substitute the Cartesian form. } \\
& \overrightarrow{A B}=-2 \mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+9 \mathbf{k}
\end{align}
$$

**ii**

$$
\begin{align}
& \overrightarrow{A C}=\overrightarrow{A O}+\overrightarrow{O C}=\overrightarrow{O C}-\overrightarrow{O A} \\
& \overrightarrow{A C}=\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{a}=-4 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+24 \mathbf{k}-(2 \mathbf{i}+5 \mathbf{j}-3 \mathbf{k}) \qquad \text { Substitute the Cartesian form. } \\
& \overrightarrow{A C}=-6 \mathbf{i}-3 \mathbf{j}+27 \mathbf{k}
\end{align}
$$

**c** $\overrightarrow{A C}=3 \overrightarrow{A B}$ and so these vectors are parallel. Since the vectors also have point $A$ in common, then $A, B$ and $C$ must be collinear (so they lie on the same straight line).

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.6

The diagram shows a cube $O A B C D E F G$ with sides of length 4 cm. Unit vectors i, j and k are parallel to $O C, O A$ and $O D$, respectively. The point $M$ lies on $F B$ such that $F M=1 \mathrm{~cm}, N$ is the midpoint of $O C$ and $P$ is the centre of the square face $D E F G$.

Express each of the vectors $\overrightarrow{N P}$ and $\overrightarrow{M P}$ in terms of $\mathbf{i}, \mathbf{j}$ and $\mathbf{k}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-222_356_363_1600_225.jpg)

The cube is of side 4 cm and $P^{\prime}$ is the centre of the square $A O C B . \overrightarrow{N P}$ is parallel to the $y$-direction and so the first part of the vector $\overrightarrow{N P}$ is $2 \mathbf{j}$.

$P$ is directly above this point $P^{\prime}$ and so the second component $P^{\prime} P$ is parallel to the $z$-direction and is $+4 \mathbf{k}$.

Answer

Using the displacement of $P$ from $N$ and $M$ :

$$
\overrightarrow{N P}=2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-222_355_363_2198_224.jpg)

$$
\overrightarrow{M P}=-2 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-223_438_449_405_264.jpg)

Or we can use position vectors:

$$
\overrightarrow{N P}=\overrightarrow{O P}-\overrightarrow{O N}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& \overrightarrow{O P}=2 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k} \text { and } \overrightarrow{O N}=2 \mathbf{i} \\
& \overrightarrow{N P}=\overrightarrow{O P}-\overrightarrow{O N}=2 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}-2 \mathbf{i} \\
& \overrightarrow{N P}=2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k} \\
& \overrightarrow{M P}=\overrightarrow{O P}-\overrightarrow{O M}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\overrightarrow{O P}=2 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k} \text { and } \overrightarrow{O M}=4 \mathbf{i}+4 \mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k}
$$

$$
\overrightarrow{M P}=\overrightarrow{O P}-\overrightarrow{O M}=2 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}-(4 \mathbf{i}+4 \mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k})
$$

$$
\overrightarrow{M P}=-2 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}
$$

Starting with the $x$-direction, move 2i left. This is the opposite to the positive direction indicated by the red arrow, so the first component is -2i.

In the $y$-direction, again we are moving in the negative direction, so the second component is -2j.

In the $z$-direction, we must move a distance equal to $M F$. This is 1 unit and so the third component is +k.

$\overrightarrow{O P}$ is 2 units in $x$-direction, 2 units in $y$-direction and 4 units in the $z$-direction and $\overrightarrow{O N}$ is 2 units in $x$-direction.

Subtract vectors.

Simplify.

$\overrightarrow{O M}$ is 4 units in $x$-direction, 4 units in $y$-direction and 3 units in $z$-direction.

Subtract vectors.

Expand and simplify.

## EXPLORE 9.2

A system of objects with the properties:

- can be represented by a column of numbers

- can be combined using addition
