is called a vector space.

Do quadratic expressions of the form $a x^{2}+b x+c$, where $a, b$ and $c$ are real constants, form a vector space? Explain your answer.

### 9.3 The scalar product

We have seen how to add and subtract vectors and also how to multiply a vector by a scalar. It is also possible to multiply two vectors. It is possible to find two different products by multiplying a pair of vectors. The first product gives an answer that is a vector. This is called the vector product or cross product (for example, the cross product of the vectors a and b is $\mathbf{a} \times \mathbf{b}$ ). The cross product is not required for Pure Mathematics 3. The second product gives an answer that is a scalar. This is called the scalar product or dot product (the dot product of the vectors a and b, for example, is a ⋅ b).

## Introductory ideas

The angle between two vectors is defined to be the angle made

- either when the direction of each vector is away from a point

- or when the direction of each vector is towards a point.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-225_151_483_2220_394.jpg)

Directions both away from a point.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-225_209_216_2162_1216.jpg)

Directions both towards a point.

As vectors have magnitude and direction we can think of the scalar product as being multiplication in relation to a particular direction.

A useful way to understand this is to think about the work done by a person when they pull an object along a flat surface using a rope:

work done by the person = force needed to pull the object × distance the object moves

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-226_228_750_448_257.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-226_126_361_528_1087.jpg)

The diagram shows vector a representing the pull and vector b representing the displacement of the object.

The direction of the pull is not in the same as the direction of the displacement. This means that only some of the force the person uses to pull the object makes it move. In fact, only the $x$-component of the pull is in the direction of the displacement.

| a | is the magnitude of the pull. Using the angle made between the two directions, we can see that the magnitude of the $x$-component of the pull is $x=\vert \mathbf{a}\vert  \cos \theta$.
|b| is the magnitude of the displacement, which is a measure of the distance moved.

Applying this information to the formula, we can see that the work done by the person $=(|\mathbf{a}| \cos \theta)|\mathbf{b}|$.

Finding the scalar (number) that is work done by the person is one application of the scalar product.

KEY POINT 9.4

In general, the geometric definition of the scalar product of two vectors is:

$$
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}| \cos \theta
$$

This formula allows us to calculate the value that represents the impact of one vector on the other, taking account of direction.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.7

$\mathbf{p}=\left(\begin{array}{c}8 \\ -6 \\ -10\end{array}\right), \mathbf{q}=\left(\begin{array}{r}4 \\ -3 \\ 0\end{array}\right)$ and the angle, $\theta$, between the two vectors is $45^{\circ}$. Find the value of the scalar product, $\mathbf{p} \cdot \mathbf{q}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{p} \cdot \mathbf{q}=|\mathbf{p}||\mathbf{q}| \cos \theta \\
& |\mathbf{p}|=\sqrt{8^{2}+(-6)^{2}+(-10)^{2}}=10 \sqrt{2} \text { and }|\mathbf{q}|=\sqrt{4^{2}+(-3)^{2}+0^{2}}=5 \\
& \mathbf{p} \cdot \mathbf{q}=(10 \sqrt{2})(5) \cos 45^{\circ}=(10 \sqrt{2})(5)\left(\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)=50
\end{align}
$$

## Special case 1: Parallel vectors

When two parallel vectors, $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$, have the same direction (not opposite directions), $\theta=0^{\circ}$ or 0 radians.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-227_127_425_309_1608.jpg)

Therefore

$$
\begin{align}
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b} & =|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}| \cos 0 \\
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b} & =|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}|
\end{align}
$$

This also means that, for example: $\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{a}=|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{a}|$ or $|\mathbf{a}|^{2}$

## Special case 2: Perpendicular vectors

When two vectors, $\mathbf{a}$ and $\mathbf{b}$, are perpendicular, $\theta=90^{\circ}$ or $\frac{\pi}{2}$ radians.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-227_218_406_568_1627.jpg)

b

$$
\begin{align}
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b} & =|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}| \cos 90^{\circ} \\
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b} & =0
\end{align}
$$

## KEY POINT 9.5

This is very important. When two vectors are perpendicular, their scalar product is zero. It is also true that when the scalar product is zero, the two vectors are perpendicular.

## EXPLORE 9.3

Show that the scalar product has the following properties:

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=\mathbf{b} \cdot \mathbf{a} \\
& \mathbf{a} \cdot m \mathbf{b}=m \mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=m(\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}), \text { where } m \text { is a scalar } \\
& \mathbf{a} \cdot(\mathbf{b}+\mathbf{c})=\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}+\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{c} \\
& (\mathbf{a}+\mathbf{b}) \cdot(\mathbf{c}+\mathbf{d})=\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{c}+\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{d}+\mathbf{b} \cdot \mathbf{c}+\mathbf{b} \cdot \mathbf{d}
\end{align}
$$

## Scalar product for component form

When we write $\mathbf{a}=a_{1} \mathbf{i}+a_{2} \mathbf{j}+a_{3} \mathbf{k}$ and $\mathbf{b}=b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}$ the properties of the scalar product give an algebraic method of finding the same value that we found using $|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}| \cos \theta$.

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=\left(a_{1} \mathbf{i}+a_{2} \mathbf{j}+a_{3} \mathbf{k}\right)\left(b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}\right) \\
&=a_{1} \mathbf{i}\left(b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}\right)+a_{2} \mathbf{j}\left(b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}\right)+a_{3} \mathbf{k}\left(b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}\right) \\
&=a_{1} b_{1} \mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{i}+a_{1} b_{2} \mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{j}+a_{1} b_{3} \mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{k} \\
& \quad+a_{2} b_{1} \mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{i}+a_{2} b_{2} \mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{j}+a_{2} b_{3} \mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{k} \\
& \quad+a_{3} b_{1} \mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{i}+a_{3} b_{2} \mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{j}+a_{3} b_{3} \mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{k}
\end{align}
$$

However, as i is parallel to i, j is parallel to j and k is parallel to k then:

$$
\mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{i}=\mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{j}=\mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{k}=1
$$

Also, as i, j and k are perpendicular to each other, scalar products such as i ⋅ j, j ⋅ k, etc. are all 0.

$$
\begin{align}
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=a_{1} b_{1} \mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{i} & +a_{1} b_{2} \mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{j}+a_{1} b_{3} \mathbf{i} \cdot \mathbf{k} \\
& +a_{2} b_{1} \mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{i}+a_{2} b_{2} \mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{j}+a_{2} b_{3} \mathbf{j} \cdot \mathbf{k} \\
& +a_{3} b_{1} \mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{i}+a_{3} b_{2} \mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{j}+a_{3} b_{3} \mathbf{k} \cdot \mathbf{k}
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 9.6

In general, the algebraic definition of the scalar product of two vectors is:

$$
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=a_{1} b_{1}+a_{2} b_{2}+a_{3} b_{3}
$$

When written in column form, $\mathbf{a}=\left(\begin{array}{l}a_{1} \\ a_{2} \\ a_{3}\end{array}\right)$ and $\mathbf{b}=\left(\begin{array}{l}b_{1} \\ b_{2} \\ b_{3}\end{array}\right)$, so it is perfectly valid to use the components of column vectors in the algebraic form of the scalar product $\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=a_{1} b_{1}+a_{2} b_{2}+a_{3} b_{3}$.

We now have a way to find the dot product when we do not know the angle between the two vectors.

Sometimes the two versions of the scalar product are written together like this:

$$
\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=a_{1} b_{1}+a_{2} b_{2}+a_{3} b_{3}=|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}| \cos \theta
$$

This can be useful as it allows us to find the angle between the two vectors when we know the components of the vectors.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.8

$$
\mathbf{p}=8 \mathbf{i}-6 \mathbf{j}-10 \mathbf{k} \text { and } \mathbf{q}=4 \mathbf{i}-3 \mathbf{j}
$$

Show that the angle, $\theta$, between the two vectors is $45^{\circ}$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
\mathbf{p} \cdot \mathbf{q} & =p_{1} q_{1}+p_{2} q_{2}+p_{3} q_{3} \\
\mathbf{p} \cdot \mathbf{q} & =8(4)+(-6)(-3)+(-10)(0)=32+18=50 \qquad \infty \qquad \text { Find the value of the scalar product } \mathbf{p} \cdot \mathbf{q} \\
|\mathbf{p}| & =\sqrt{8^{2}+(-6)^{2}+(-10)^{2}}=10 \sqrt{2} \\
|\mathbf{q}| & =\sqrt{4^{2}+(-3)^{2}+0^{2}}=5 \\
50 & =(10 \sqrt{2})(5) \cos \theta \\
\frac{50}{50 \sqrt{2}} & =\cos \theta \\
\theta & =\cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)=45^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.9

Relative to the origin $O$, the position vectors of the points $A$ and $B$ are given by $\overrightarrow{O A}=\left(\begin{array}{r}5 \\ -2 \\ 4\end{array}\right)$ and $\overrightarrow{O B}=\left(\begin{array}{r}-1 \\ 3 \\ -1\end{array}\right)$.

Find angle $O B A$.

Answer

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-229_253_212_798_259.jpg)

$\overrightarrow{O B}$ and $\overrightarrow{A B}$ are the two directions towards the point $B$, so these are the vectors we need to use.

Find $\overrightarrow{O B} \cdot \overrightarrow{A B}$. First of all, find the column vector $\overrightarrow{A B}$.

$$
\overrightarrow{A B}=\overrightarrow{O B}-\overrightarrow{O A}=\left(\begin{array}{r} -1 \\
3 \\
-1 \end{array}\right)-\left(\begin{array}{r} 5 \\
-2 \\
4 \end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{r} -6 \\
5 \\
-5 \end{array}\right) \quad \text { Using the given vector } \overrightarrow{O B}=\left(\begin{array}{r} -1 \\
3 \\
-1 \end{array}\right),
$$

$$
\overrightarrow{O B} \cdot \overrightarrow{A B}=(-1)(-6)+(3)(5)+(-1)(-5)=26 \quad\begin{align}
& \text { Find the magnitude of } \overrightarrow{O B} \text { and of } \\
& \overrightarrow{A B} \text { using Pythagoras' theorem. }
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& |\overrightarrow{A B}|=\left|\left(\begin{array}{r} -6 \\
5 \\
-5 \end{array}\right)\right|=\sqrt{(-6)^{2}+5^{2}+(-5)^{2}}=\sqrt{86} \\
& |\overrightarrow{O B}|=\left|\left(\begin{array}{r} -1 \\
3 \\
-1 \end{array}\right)\right|=\sqrt{(-1)^{2}+3^{2}+(-1)^{2}}=\sqrt{11} \text { Use } \overrightarrow{O B} \cdot \overrightarrow{A B}=|\overrightarrow{O B}||\overrightarrow{A B}| \cos O B A \\
& 26=\sqrt{86} \sqrt{11} \cos O B A \\
& \frac{26}{\sqrt{86} \sqrt{11}}=\cos O B A \\
& \text { Angle OBA }=32.3^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

### 9.4 The vector equation of a line

We already know that, in 2-dimensional space, a line can be represented by an equation of the form $y=m x+c$ or $a x+b y=c$. In 3-dimensional space, we need a new approach because introducing the third dimension gives an equation of the form $a x+b y+c z=d$, which is actually the Cartesian equation of a plane. One way around this problem is to write the equation of the line using vectors.

The equation of a line in vector form is valid in both 2- and 3-dimensional space.

So what information do we need to be able to do this?

When a line passes through a fixed given point and continues in a known direction then it is uniquely positioned in space.

Therefore, to form the vector equation of a straight line, we need:

- a point on the line, $A\left(a_{1}, a_{2}, a_{3}\right)$, and a direction vector, $\mathbf{b}=\left(\begin{array}{l}b_{1} \\ b_{2} \\ b_{3}\end{array}\right)$, that is parallel to the line.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-231_311_608_1783_260.jpg)

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-231_147_315_1715_904.jpg)

$R$ is any point on the line and so $\overrightarrow{O R}$ represents the position vector of any point on the line.

To reach $R$, starting at $O$, take the path $\overrightarrow{O R}=\overrightarrow{O A}+\overrightarrow{A R}$.

$\overrightarrow{O A}$ is a vector we know. To move from $A$ to $R$, we need to travel in the direction of $\mathbf{b}$.

Multiplying $\mathbf{b}$ by different scalars changes the position of $R$ on the line, so simply gives us different points on the line. Using the parameter $t$ to represent the variable scalar

$$
\overrightarrow{O R}=\overrightarrow{O A}+t \mathbf{b}
$$

## KEY POINT 9.7

Using lower case notation for vectors, the vector equation of the line is:

$$
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}+t \mathbf{b}
$$

- Or two points on the line, $A\left(a_{1}, a_{2}, a_{3}\right)$ and $C\left(c_{1}, c_{2}, c_{3}\right)$. One can be used as the fixed given point and the direction can be found from the displacement between the two points.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-232_340_757_645_223.jpg)

The situation is almost the same. In this case, however, we do not know the direction of the line. We can find the direction using the position vectors of the points $A$ and $C$.

Using the parameter $t$ as before,

$$
\overrightarrow{O R}=\overrightarrow{O A}+t \overrightarrow{A C} .
$$

## KEY POINT 9.8

Using lower case notation for vectors, the vector equation of a line is:

$$
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}+t(\mathbf{c}-\mathbf{a})
$$

In each case, the structure is:

$$
\text { general point on line }=\text { position of known point }+ \text { some way along a known direction }
$$

## Parametric form

$R(x, y, z)$ represents any point on the line.

The position vector of this point can be written as $\mathbf{r}=\left(\begin{array}{c}x \\ y \\ z\end{array}\right)$ or $\mathbf{r}=x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j}+z \mathbf{k}$.

In the equation $\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}+t \mathbf{b}$, using the position vectors in component form:

$$
\left(\begin{array}{l} x \\
y \\
z \end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{l} a_{1} \\
a_{2} \\
a_{3} \end{array}\right)+t\left(\begin{array}{l} b_{1} \\
b_{2} \\
b_{3} \end{array}\right) \text { or } x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j}+z \mathbf{k}=a_{1} \mathbf{i}+a_{2} \mathbf{j}+a_{3} \mathbf{k}+t\left(b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}\right)
$$

Simplifying the right-hand side of this:

$$
\left(\begin{array}{l} x \\
y \\
z \end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{l} a_{1}+t b_{1} \\
a_{2}+t b_{2} \\
a_{3}+t b_{3} \end{array}\right) \text { or }\begin{align}
& x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j}+z \mathbf{k}=a_{1} \mathbf{i}+t b_{1} \mathbf{i}+a_{2} \mathbf{j}+t b_{2} \mathbf{j}+a_{3} \mathbf{k}+t b_{3} \mathbf{k} \\
& x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j}+z \mathbf{k}=\left(a_{1}+t b_{1}\right) \mathbf{i}+\left(a_{2}+t b_{2}\right) \mathbf{j}+\left(a_{3}+t b_{3}\right) \mathbf{k}
\end{align}
$$

Comparing the components:

$$
\begin{align}
& x=a_{1}+t b_{1} \\
& y=a_{2}+t b_{2} \\
& z=a_{3}+t b_{3}
\end{align}
$$

These three equations are called the parametric equations of the line.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.10

A line passes through the point with position vector $3 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}$ and is parallel to the vector $\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}$.

Find:

a the vector equation of the line

b the parametric equations of the line.

Answer

Using $t$ as the parameter:

**a** General point $=\mathbf{r}$

General point on line =

Known point $=3 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}$ position of known point Known direction $=\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}$ + some way along a known direction.

$$
\mathbf{r}=\left(\begin{array}{r} 3 \\
-2 \\
4 \end{array}\right)+t\left(\begin{array}{r} 1 \\
-1 \\
1 \end{array}\right)
$$

**b** $\mathbf{r}=\left(\begin{array}{r}3 \\ -2 \\ 4\end{array}\right)+t\left(\begin{array}{r}1 \\ -1 \\ 1\end{array}\right)$ or $\mathbf{r}=3 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}+t(\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k})$

Write r as a column vector and collect together the $x$-, $y$ - and $z$-components of the right-hand side.

$$
\begin{align}
& \left(\begin{array}{c} x \\
y \\
z \end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}{r} 3+t \\
-2-t \\
4+t \end{array}\right) \text { or } \\
& x \mathbf{i}+y \mathbf{j}+z \mathbf{k}=(3+t) \mathbf{i}+(-2-t) \mathbf{j}+(4+t) \mathbf{k} \\
& x=3+t \quad y=-2-t \quad z=4+t
\end{align}
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.11

The position vectors of the points $A$ and $B$ are given by

$$
\mathbf{a}=3 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k} \text { and } \mathbf{b}=\mathbf{i}-4 \mathbf{j}+8 \mathbf{k},
$$

respectively.

**a** Write down the vector $\overrightarrow{A B}$.

**b** Find the vector equation of the line $A B$.

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
& \overrightarrow{A B}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{a} \quad \text { Substitute for } \mathbf{a} \text { and } \mathbf{b} . \\
& \overrightarrow{A B}=\mathbf{i}-4 \mathbf{j}+8 \mathbf{k}-(3 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k}) \qquad \mathrm{c}^{\mathbf{4}} . . \\
& \overrightarrow{A B}=-2 \mathbf{i}-6 \mathbf{j}+15 \mathbf{k}
\end{align}
$$

**b** Using $\mathbf{a}=3 \mathbf{i}+4 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k}$ as the known point, $t$ as the parameter and $\overrightarrow{A B}$ from part a as the known direction:

| General point $=\mathbf{r}$ | General point on line = |
| :--- | :--- |
| Known point $=3 \mathbf{i}+4 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k}$ | position of known point |
| Known direction $=-2 \mathbf{i}-6 \mathbf{j}+15 \mathbf{k}$ | + some way along a known direction. |

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{r}=3 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k}+t \overrightarrow{A B} \\
& \mathbf{r}=3 \mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k}+t(-2 \mathbf{i}-6 \mathbf{j}+15 \mathbf{k})
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 9.9

The angle between two lines is the angle between the two direction vectors of the lines.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.12

The vector equation of the line $L_{1}$ is $\mathbf{r}=3 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}-\mathbf{k}+\lambda(\mathbf{i}-8 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k})$.

**a** The line $L_{2}$ is parallel to the line $L_{1}$ and passes through the point $A(0,2,5)$. Write down the vector equation of the line $L_{2}$.

**b** Find the angle between the line $L_{1}$ and the line $\mathbf{r}=4 \mathbf{i}+3 \mathbf{j}-2 \mathbf{k}+\mu(5 \mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+2 \mathbf{k})$.

Answer

**a** Using $\mu$ as the parameter,

| General point is r | General point on line = |
| :--- | :--- |
| Known point is $2 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k}$ | position of known point |
| The direction of the line $L_{1}$ is $\mathbf{i}-8 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k}$ | + some way along a known direction point. |

The line $L_{2}$ is therefore

$$
\mathbf{r}=2 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k}+\mu(\mathbf{i}-8 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k})
$$

**b** Find the angle between Find the scalar product $\mathbf{d}_{1} \cdot \mathbf{d}_{2}$ $\mathbf{d}_{1}=\mathbf{i}-8 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k}$ and $\mathbf{d}_{2}=5 \mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+2 \mathbf{k}$.

Now find the magnitude of $\mathbf{d}_{1}$ and $\mathbf{d}_{2}$ using

$$
\mathbf{d}_{1} \cdot \mathbf{d}_{2}=(1)(5)+(-8)(-1)+5(2)=23 \quad \text { Pythagoras' theorem }
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& \left|\mathbf{d}_{1}\right|=\sqrt{1^{2}+(-8)^{2}+(5)^{2}}=3 \sqrt{10} \\
& \left|\mathbf{d}_{2}\right|=\sqrt{5^{2}+(-1)^{2}+2^{2}}=\sqrt{30} \qquad _{} \text {. Use } \mathbf{d}_{1} \cdot \mathbf{d}_{2}=\left|\mathbf{d}_{1}\right|\left|\mathbf{d}_{2}\right| \cos \theta \\
& 23=3 \sqrt{10} \sqrt{30} \cos \theta \\
& \cos \theta=\frac{23}{3 \sqrt{10} \sqrt{30}} \\
& \cos ^{-1}\left(\frac{23}{3 \sqrt{10} \sqrt{30}}\right)=63.727 \qquad ^{\circ}=63.7^{\circ}
\end{align}
$$

### 9.5 Intersection of two lines

In 2-dimensional space, two lines are either parallel or they must intersect at a point.

In 3-dimensional space it might be that:

- they are parallel

- or they are not parallel and intersect

- or they are not parallel and do not intersect.

Lines that are not parallel and do not intersect are called skew.

Parallel lines have the same direction vectors and so if a pair of lines is parallel, this can easily be seen by examining the vector equations of the lines.

Non-parallel intersecting lines

In 2-dimensional space, to find the point of intersection of a pair of non-parallel lines using their Cartesian equations ( $a x+b y=c$ ), we solve the equations of the lines simultaneously. In other words, we put the two equations equal to each other.

The process is the same in 3-dimensional space and using the vector equations of the lines.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.13

Given that the lines with vector equations

$$
\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}-4 \mathbf{k}+\lambda(-\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k})
$$

and $\mathbf{r}=2 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}+\mu(\mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k})$ intersect at the point $A$, find the coordinates of $A$.

Answer

The lines are equal at the point of intersection, so write the vector equation in parametric form so that the components can be equated.

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{r}=\mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}-4 \mathbf{k}+\lambda(-\mathbf{i}-\mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}) \\
& x=1-\lambda \\
& y=-2-\lambda \\
& z=-4+\lambda
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{r}=2 \mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}+\mu(\mathbf{i}+2 \mathbf{j}-7 \mathbf{k}) \\
& x=2+\mu \\
& y=-2+2 \mu \\
& z=1-7 \mu
\end{align}
$$

Now equate the components.

$$
\begin{align}
1-\lambda & =2+\mu \tag{1} \\
-2-\lambda & =-2+2 \mu \tag{2} \\
-4+\lambda & =1-7 \mu \tag{3}
\end{align}
$$

Solve these equations to find the value of $\lambda$ and of $\mu$ :

| From (1): | $\lambda=-1-\mu$ |
| :--- | :--- |
| Substitute into (3): | $-4-1-\mu=1-7 \mu$ |
|  | $6 \mu=6 \quad \mu=1$ |

When $\mu=1$ :

$$
\lambda=-1-1=-2
$$

Check:

| $\boldsymbol{\lambda}=-\mathbf{2}$ | $\mu=1$ |
| :--- | :--- |
| $x=1-(-2)=3$ | $x=2+1=3$ |
| $y=-2-(-2)=0$ | $y=-2+2=0$ |
| $z=-4-2=-6$ | $z=1-7=-6$ |

The lines intersect at the point $A(3,0,-6)$.

## Skew lines

Skew lines have no point of intersection.

When two lines are skew, they are not parallel and they do not intersect.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 9.14

Show that the lines with vector equations

$$
\begin{align}
& \mathbf{r}=4 \mathbf{i}+7 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k}+\lambda(-2 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k}) \\
& \mathbf{r}=\mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+10 \mathbf{k}+\mu(-6 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k})
\end{align}
$$

do not intersect.

Answer

As $-2 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k}$ is not a scalar multiple of $-6 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}$ the lines are not parallel.

Writing the equations in parametric form:

$$
\begin{array}{ll} \mathbf{r}=4 \mathbf{i}+7 \mathbf{j}+5 \mathbf{k}+\lambda(-2 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k}) & \mathbf{r}=\mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+10 \mathbf{k}+\mu(-6 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}+4 \mathbf{k}) \\
x=4-2 \lambda & x=1-6 \mu \\
y=7+\lambda & y=1+\mu \\
z=5+3 \lambda & z=10+4 \mu \end{array}
$$

Now equate the components.

$$
\begin{align}
4-2 \lambda & =1-6 \mu- \tag{1} \\
7+\lambda & =1+\mu- \tag{2} \\
5+3 \lambda & =10+4 \mu \tag{3}
\end{align}
$$

Attempt to solve these equations to find the value of $\lambda$ and of $\mu$ that should give a consistent point:

From (2):

$$
\lambda=\mu-6
$$

Substitute into (3):

$$
\begin{gathered} 5+3(\mu-6)=10+4 \mu \\
\mu=-23 \end{gathered}
$$

When $\mu=-23$ :

$$
\lambda=-23-6=-29
$$

Check:

| $\lambda=-29$ | $\mu=-23$ |
| :--- | :--- |
| $x=4-2(-29)=62$ | $x=1-6(-23)=139$ |
| $y=7-29=-22$ | $y=1+(-23)=-22$ |
| $z=5+3(-29)=-82$ | $z=10+4(-23)=-82$ |

The values of $\lambda$ and $\mu$ do not give a consistent point, so there is no point of intersection and the lines are skew.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

- For position vectors $\overrightarrow{O A}=\mathbf{a}$ and $\overrightarrow{O B}=\mathbf{b}, \overrightarrow{A B}=\mathbf{b}-\mathbf{a}$.

- The magnitude (length or size) is worked out using Pythagoras' theorem:

When $\mathbf{a}=a_{1} \mathbf{i}+a_{2} \mathbf{j}+a_{3} \mathbf{k},|\mathbf{a}|=\sqrt{a_{1}^{2}+a_{2}^{2}+a_{3}^{2}}$.

- Two vectors are parallel if one is a scalar multiple of the other.

- Problem solving with angles will involve the use of the scalar product.

When $\mathbf{a}=a_{1} \mathbf{i}+a_{2} \mathbf{j}+a_{3} \mathbf{k}$ and $\mathbf{b}=b_{1} \mathbf{i}+b_{2} \mathbf{j}+b_{3} \mathbf{k}$ $\mathbf{a} \cdot \mathbf{b}=a_{1} b_{1}+a_{2} b_{2}+a_{3} b_{3}=|\mathbf{a}||\mathbf{b}| \cos \theta$

- Vector equation of a line: $\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{a}+t \mathbf{b}$

- To find the point of intersection of two intersecting lines, solve the parametric equations of the lines simultaneously.

## What is a differential equation?

An equation that has a derivative such as $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ or $\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2} y}{\mathrm{~d} x^{2}}$ as one of its terms is called a differential equation. When the equation has the first derivative $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ as one of its terms, it is called a first order differential equation. For example, the equation $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=3 x^{2}$ is an alternative way of representing the relationship $y=x^{3}+C$, where $C$ is a constant.

To solve a differential equation, we integrate to find the direct relationship between $x$ and $y$, such as $y=x^{3}+C$. The direct equation $y=x^{3}+C$ is called the general solution of the differential equation $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=3 x^{2}$. To find a particular solution, we must have further information that will enable us to find the value of $C$.

The solution of an algebraic equation is a number or set of numbers. The solution of a differential equation is a function.

In this chapter, we will learn the initial skills needed to master the solution of one type of first order differential equations. These simple skills will be the foundation for further study of this subject.

You might wonder about the usefulness of such seemingly abstract equations in the real world. In fact, differential equations are excellent mathematical models for many real-life situations and are essential to solve many problems. First order differential equations can model rates of change, in other words, the rate at which one quantity is increasing or decreasing with respect to another quantity. There are many examples in the physical world that can be modelled using such relationships. A few examples are the rate of increase of the number of bacteria in a culture, simple kinematics problems, the rate of decrease of the value of a car and the rate at which a hot liquid cools.

### 10.1 The technique of separating the variables

Solving a differential equation such as $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=6(x+1)^{2}$ can be done using an anti-differentiation approach.

We know, from the Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 9, that if we integrate both sides with respect to $x$, we have:

$$
\begin{align}
& \int \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=\int 6(x+1)^{2} \mathrm{~d} x \\
& y=\frac{6(x+1)^{3}}{3}+C \\
& y=2(x+1)^{3}+C
\end{align}
$$

Notice that the first line on the left-hand side is $\int \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=\int 6(x+1)^{2} \mathrm{~d} x$ and from anti-differentiation we can see that, if we integrate $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ with respect to $x$ we obtain an expression for $y$.

This means that $\int \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=$ is equivalent to $\int \mathrm{d} y=$, which is $y=$ This result is generally true.

## KEY POINT 10.1

Integrating an expression of the form $\mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ with respect to $x$ is equivalent to integrating $\mathrm{f}(y)$ with respect to $y$.

Therefore $\int \mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x$ is equivalent to $\int \mathrm{f}(y) \mathrm{d} y$.

The differential equation $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=6(x+1)^{2}$ is easy to solve because $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ is equal to a function that we know how to integrate. This is not always the case.

How do we solve the differential equation $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=x y$ when $y>0$ ?

As it is written, we cannot integrate both sides with respect to $x$ and find a solution.

Separating the variables in the expression $x y$ means that we are then able to integrate.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =x y & & \text { Separate the variables } x \text { and } y . \\
\frac{1}{y} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =x & & \text { Form integrals of both sides with respect to } x . \\
\int \frac{1}{y} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int x \mathrm{~d} x & & \text { Integrate each side. } \\
\int \frac{1}{y} \mathrm{~d} y & =\int x \mathrm{~d} x & & \begin{array}{l} \text { The modulus of } y \text { is not required as } \\
\text { we know that } y>0 . \end{array} \\
\ln y+A & =\frac{x^{2}}{2}+B & & \begin{array}{l} \text { The constants of integration can be combined } \\
\text { as one term and we only need to write a } \\
\text { constant on one side. } \end{array} \\
y & =\mathrm{e}^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}+C} & & \begin{array}{l} \text { This is the general solution of the differential } \\
\text { equation as we do not know the value of the } \\
\text { arbitrary constant } C . \end{array}
\end{align}
$$

When we have further information, we can find the particular solution of the differential solution given by that information.

For example, solve $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=x y$ given that $y=2$ when $x=0$.

Using this information:

$$
\begin{align}
& \ln y=\frac{x^{2}}{2}+C \text { and so } \ln 2=\frac{0^{2}}{2}+C, C=\ln 2 \\
& y=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\ln 2} \\
& y=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}} \times \mathrm{e}^{\ln 2} \quad \begin{array}{l} \text { This is the particular solution of the differential } \\
\text { equation. } \end{array} \\
& y=2 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{x^{2}}{2}} \quad \begin{array}{l} \text { Note that you often need to write your answer as } \\
\text { an expression for } y \text { in terms of } x . \text { This commonly } \\
\text { means using laws of indices or logarithms. } \end{array}
\end{align}
$$

By separating the variables we are rearranging the differential equation to the form

$$
\mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{g}(x) .
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} & =\mathrm{g}(x) & & \text { (Note: Other variables may be used such as } v \\
\int \mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int \mathrm{g}(x) \mathrm{d} x & & \text { and } t .) \\
\int \mathrm{f}(y) \mathrm{d} y & =\int \mathrm{g}(x) \mathrm{d} x & &
\end{align}
$$

So, in the case where the derivative is $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ :

- form an expression in $x$ only on one side of the equation

- form an expression in $y \times \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ on the other side of the equation.

This gives us a form of the equation that can be integrated on both sides, one side with respect to $x$ and the other side with respect to $y$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 10.1

The variables $x$ and $y$ satisfy the differential equation $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{1+x}{2 y}$.

It is given that $y=3$ when $x=0$.

Solve the differential equation and obtain an expression for $y$ in terms of $x$.

Answer

Even though the question does not ask for it directly, you are expected to use the initial condition that you have been given to find a particular solution.

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{1+x}{2 y}
$$

Separate the variables $x$ and $y$.

Here we have moved the $2 y$. It was not essential to move the 2 , we could have moved the $y$ only. This would be just as valid as it would also give expressions that can be integrated.

$$
2 y \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=1+x
$$

Form integrals of both sides with respect to $x$.

$$
\begin{align}
\int 2 y \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x & =\int(1+x) \mathrm{d} x \\
\int 2 y \mathrm{~d} y & =\int(1+x) \mathrm{d} x
\end{align}
$$

Integrate each side.

$$
y^{2}=x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+C
$$

$$
y=3 \text { when } x=0 .
$$

$$
\begin{align}
9 & =0+\frac{0^{2}}{2}+C, \quad C=9 \\
y^{2} & =x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+9 \\
y & =\left(x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+9\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
\end{align}
$$

You can check this by differentiating.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 10.2

Find the general solution of the differential equation $\left(x^{2}+1\right) y^{3}+x^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=0$.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& \left(x^{2}+1\right) y^{3}+x^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=0 \\
& x^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=-\left(x^{2}+1\right) y^{3} \\
& \frac{1}{y^{3}} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=-\frac{\left(x^{2}+1\right)}{x^{2}} * * \\
& \int y^{-3} \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=\int\left(-x^{-2}-1\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& \int y^{-3} \mathrm{~d} y=\int\left(-x^{-2}-1\right) \mathrm{d} x \\
& -\frac{1}{2 y^{2}}=\frac{1}{x}-x+C
\end{align}
$$

Is this the complete solution?

Earlier in the solution (at line **) we assumed that $y \neq 0$. However, when $y=0$, the original differential equation becomes $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\frac{0}{x^{2}}=0$. Since $y=0$ is the $x$-axis, it is true that its gradient is 0 at all points and so $y=0$ is also a solution of the differential equation and must be included for the general solution to be complete.

The complete general solution is, therefore, $-\frac{1}{2 y^{2}}=\frac{1}{x}-x+C$ or $y=0$.

In Worked example 10.2, we saw that, when rearranging to the form $\mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{g}(x)$, there are cases where $\mathrm{f}(y)$ is a function such as, for example, $\frac{1}{y^{3}}$. In these cases, a value (or values) of $y$ must be excluded for the separation of the variables to take place.

As a final step, when finding the general solution in these cases, we must check if the value (or values) we have excluded when separating the variables gives (or give) a valid solution of the original differential equation. When they do, we should include them.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 10.3

It is given that, for $0<x<300, \frac{\mathrm{~d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{x(300-x)}{600}$ and $x=50$ when $t=0$. Find the particular solution of the differential equation, giving your answer as an expression for $x$ in terms of $t$.

Answer

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{x(300-x)}{600}
$$

Separate the variables. The derivative is with respect to $t$ and so the expression in $x$ needs to be moved to the left-hand side.

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{1}{x(300-x)} \frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t} & =\frac{1}{600} \\
\int \frac{1}{x(300-x)} \frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t} \mathrm{~d} t & =\frac{1}{600} \int \mathrm{~d} t \\
\int \frac{1}{x(300-x)} \mathrm{d} x & =\frac{1}{600} \int \mathrm{~d} t \\
\frac{1}{300} \int\left(\frac{1}{x}+\frac{1}{300-x}\right) \mathrm{d} x & =\frac{1}{600} \int \mathrm{~d} t \\
\frac{1}{300}(\ln x-\ln (300-x)) & =\frac{1}{600} t+C \\
\ln x-\ln (300-x) & =\frac{1}{2} t+C \\
\ln \frac{x}{300-x} & =\frac{1}{2} t+C \\
C & =\ln \frac{50}{300-x} \\
\frac{x}{300-x} & =\frac{1}{2} t+\ln 0.2 \\
\frac{x}{300-x} & =\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t+\ln 0.2} \times \mathrm{e}^{\ln 0.2} \\
x & =0.2 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t}(300-x) \\
x+0.2 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t} x & =60 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t} \\
x\left(1+0.2 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t}\right) & =60 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t} \\
x & =\frac{60 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t}}{1+0.2 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2} t}}
\end{align}
$$

Form integrals of both sides with respect to $t$.

Note that it is simpler to leave the 600 on the right-hand side here.

Write the expression on the left in terms of its partial fractions.

Integrate each side.

The modulus signs are not required as we know that $0<x<300$.

$$
x=50 \text { when } t=0 .
$$

EXPLORE 10.1

Newton's law of cooling or heating

Many differential equations arise from modelling natural laws and relationships. One example is Newton's law of cooling or heating:

The rate of change of temperature of an object is proportional to the difference in temperature of the object and its surroundings.

We define

$T$ as the temperature of the object

$t \quad$ as time

$T_{0}$ as the temperature of the object when $t=0$

$s$ as the constant temperature of the surroundings

$k$ as the constant of proportionality.

Discuss the following.

- Why it is necessary to define the variables and constants.

- Why the differential equation $\frac{\mathrm{d} T}{\mathrm{~d} t}=k(T-s), k>0$ is satisfied by $T$.

- Other possible correct ways to represent Newton's law of cooling or heating as a differential equation with this set of defined variables and constants.

- Why the general solution of the given form of the differential equation may be written as:

$$
|T-s|=A \mathrm{e}^{k t} \text { where } A=\mathrm{e}^{C} .
$$

- How many arbitrary constants there are in the general solution of any first order differential equation.

- Why the modulus in the general solution is required.

- How the general solution can be used to represent the temperature of an object cooling down or warming up.

### 10.2 Forming a differential equation from a problem

The key to forming a differential equation from a problem is the successful interpretation of the language used in the question.

For example, when you are given a problem such as:

The rate of increase of the number of bacteria in a dish is $k$ times the number of bacteria in the dish. The number of bacteria present in the dish at time $t$ is $x$...

Replacing the words the number of bacteria in a dish by $x$ the problem becomes:

> The rate of increase of $x$ is $k$ times $x$.

This rate of increase of $x$ is with respect to time, $t$, and is much easier to translate into a differential equation: $\frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=k x$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 10.4

Velocity is defined to be the rate of change of displacement with respect to time.

The velocity of a particle at time $t$ seconds is inversely proportional to its displacement, $s$ metres, from an origin, $O$.

**a** Form a differential equation to represent this data.

**b** Initially, the particle has a displacement of 2 metres from $O$.

After 1 second, the particle has a displacement of 5 metres from $O$.

Solve the differential equation giving your answer in the form $t=\mathrm{f}(s)$.

**c** Find $t$ when the displacement of the particle from $O$ is 10 metres.

Answer

**a** The velocity of a particle at time $t$ seconds is inversely proportional to its displacement, $s$ metres, from the origin, $O$.

$$
v=\frac{\mathrm{d} s}{\mathrm{~d} t} \text { and so } \frac{\mathrm{d} s}{\mathrm{~d} t} \propto \frac{1}{s} \text {, which means that } \frac{\mathrm{d} s}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{k}{s} \qquad \text { At the moment, you do not have enough }
$$

information to find the constant of proportionality, $k$

**b** $\frac{\mathrm{d} s}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{k}{s}$. Separate the variables $s$ and $t$.

$s \frac{\mathrm{~d} s}{\mathrm{~d} t}=k \qquad _{~}$ Form integrals of both sides with respect to $t$.

$$
\begin{align}
& \int s \frac{\mathrm{~d} s}{\mathrm{~d} t} \mathrm{~d} t=\int k \mathrm{~d} t \\
& \int s \mathrm{~d} s=\int k \mathrm{~d} t \\
& \frac{s^{2}}{2}=k t+C \\
& C=2, \frac{s^{2}}{2}-k t+2 \\
& \frac{5^{2}}{2}=k+2, \quad k=10.5 \\
& \frac{s^{2}}{2}=\frac{21}{2} t+2 \\
& t=\frac{s^{2}-4}{21}
\end{align}
$$

**c** Find $t$ when $s=10$

$$
t=\frac{100-4}{21}=4.5714 \qquad =4.57 \text { seconds correct to } 3 \text { significant figures. }
$$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 10.5

The population density, $P$, of a town can be modelled as a continuous variable.

In the year when records began:

- the population density, $P$, of the town was increasing at the rate of 0.5 people per hectare per year

- the population density of the town was 2 people per hectare.

It is suggested that, $t$ years after records began, the rate of increase of the population density is inversely proportional to the square of the population density.

**a** Form a differential equation to model this information.

**b** Solve the differential equation, giving your answer in the form $P=\mathrm{f}(t)$.

**c** The table shows the actual population density of the town, every 5 years for the first 20 years after records began.

| $\boldsymbol{t}$ | 5 | 10 | 15 | 20 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $P$ | 3.5 | 4 | 5.5 | 7 |

Comment on how good a fit the model is to the actual data in the longer term.

Answer

**a** The rate of increase of $P$ is inversely proportional to $P^{2}$ :

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t} \propto \frac{1}{P^{2}} \quad \frac{\mathrm{~d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{k}{P^{2}}
$$

When $t=0, \frac{\mathrm{~d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=0.5$ and $P=2$, therefore:

$$
\begin{array}{ll} \frac{\mathrm{d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{k}{P^{2}} & 0.5=\frac{k}{4}, k=2 \\
\frac{\mathrm{~d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{2}{P^{2}} & \end{array}
$$

**b** $\frac{\mathrm{d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{2}{P^{2}}$. Separate the variables $P$ and $t$.

$$
P^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=2 \quad \text { Form integrals of both sides with respect to } t .
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& \int P^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t} \mathrm{~d} t=\int 2 \mathrm{~d} t \\
& \int P^{2} \mathrm{~d} P=2 \int \mathrm{~d} t \\
& \qquad P \qquad \text { Integrate each side. } t=0, P=2
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{P^{3}}{3} & =2 t+C \\
\frac{8}{3} & =C \\
\frac{P^{3}}{3} & =2 t+\frac{8}{3} \\
P^{3} & =6 t+8 \\
P & =\sqrt[3]{6 t+8}
\end{align}
$$

**c**

| $\boldsymbol{t}$ | 5 | 10 | 15 | 20 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| $P$ | 3.5 | 4 | 5.5 | 7 |
| Model prediction (to 2 significant figures) | 3.4 | 4.1 | 4.6 | 5.0 |

In the longer term, the model is not a particularly good fit as the values it generates when time increases are under-estimates of the known data.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 10.6

The value, $\$ V$, of a motorbike as it gets older can be modelled as a continuous variable.

A model is suggested to estimate the rate of decrease of the value of the motorbike. This model is that the value $V$ when the motorbike is $t$ months old decreases at a rate proportional to $V$.

**a** Write down a differential equation that is satisfied by $V$.

**b** The motorbike had a value of $\$ 10000$ when it was new.

Solve the differential equation, showing that $V=10000 \mathrm{e}^{-k t}$, where $k$ is a positive constant.

**c** After 3 years the motorbike has lost half of its value when new.

Find the value of the motorbike when it was 2 years old.

Answer

**a** The rate of decrease of $V$ is proportional to $V$.

$\frac{\mathrm{d} V}{\mathrm{~d} t} \propto V$ and so $\frac{\mathrm{d} V}{\mathrm{~d} t}=-k V$, where $k$ is a positive constant.

This describes exponential decay. The rate is decreasing and so the constant of proportionality is negative.

**b** $\quad \frac{\mathrm{d} V}{\mathrm{~d} t}=-k V$ Separate the variables $V$ and $t$.

$\frac{1}{V} \frac{\mathrm{~d} V}{\mathrm{~d} t}=-k$

Form integrals of both sides with respect to $t$.

$\int \frac{1}{V} \frac{\mathrm{~d} V}{\mathrm{~d} t} \mathrm{~d} t=\int-k \mathrm{~d} t$ $\int \frac{1}{V} \mathrm{~d} V=\int-k \mathrm{~d} t$ Integrate each side.

$\ln |V|=-k t+C$

$t=0, V=10000$

$\ln 10000=C$

For this particular solution, $V$ is positive and so the modulus is not required.

$\ln V=-k t+\ln 10000$

Write in the form $V=\mathrm{f}(t)$.

$V=\mathrm{e}^{-k t+\ln 10000}$

$V=\mathrm{e}^{-k t} \times \mathrm{e}^{\ln 10000}$

$V=10000 \mathrm{e}^{-k t}$

**c** $V=10000 \mathrm{e}^{-k t}$

$t=3, V=5000$

$5000=10000 \mathrm{e}^{-3 k}$

$\frac{5000}{10000}=\mathrm{e}^{-3 k}$

$\ln 0.5=-3 k$

$k=\frac{-\ln 0.5}{3}$

$V=10000 \mathrm{e}^{-2\left(\frac{-\ln 0.5}{3}\right)}$

$V=\$ 6299.605 \qquad =\$ 6300$

correct to 3 significant figures

## EXPLORE 10.2

Modelling growth

Many of the models we have been using have assumed exponential growth. This type of growth is unlimited as the rate of growth of a population is proportional to the size of the population at that time.

This means that $\frac{\mathrm{d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=k P$.

Is an exponential growth model appropriate for modelling the population of the planet Earth in the long term?

It is thought that the maximum capacity of the Earth is about 10 billion people.

In 1987, the population of the Earth was about 5 billion people and the growth rate of the population was about 1.84\%.

Some people think that the problem of the Earth's increasing population can be solved by sending some people to live on other planets, such as Mars. In 2031, the first people to live on Mars will launch into space. The environment on Mars is different to Earth and many challenges must be overcome. This will all take time.

When a population is modelled as growing exponentially at a constant rate of $r \%$ per year, it takes about $\frac{70}{r}$ years for the population to double.

- Use the data given so far to investigate whether going to live on Mars is a good solution to the population problem.

- Comment on any assumptions you have made in your investigation.

There is statistical evidence that the population of the Earth is not growing at a constant rate each year. In 2017, the growth rate had decreased to 1.11\% per year. The population needs resources to survive and so the growth rate has decreased as resources have decreased.

A different model, logistic growth, can be used when resources are limited. This model takes into account the changing growth rates of the population.

Here are sketch graphs of exponential and logistic growth models:

Exponential growth

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-260_587_588_477_569.jpg)

Logistic growth

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-260_578_587_1214_576.jpg)

For example, for a population of size $P$,

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} P}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\frac{r P(k-P)}{k}
$$

where the constant $r$ is the maximum natural growth rate (birth rate - death rate) and the constant $k$ is the maximum capacity of the limited environment.

- Show that as time increases this model suggests that $P$ tends to $k$.

- Comment on the limitations of this model.

## Checklist of learning and understanding

General procedure to solve a differential equation using the technique of separating the variables

- Separate the variables $\mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}=\mathrm{g}(x)$.

- Integrate both sides $\int \mathrm{f}(y) \frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x} \mathrm{~d} x=\int \mathrm{g}(x) \mathrm{d} x$

$$
\int \mathrm{f}(y) \mathrm{d} y=\int \mathrm{g}(x) \mathrm{d} x .
$$

- Solve to find a general solution.

- Substitute any given conditions to find a particular solution.

- Check your answer by differentiating.

Other points to remember

- Take care with modulus signs in expressions such as $\ln |\mathrm{f}(x)|$.

- When finding a general solution, check to see if any values excluded when the variables are separated are possible solutions.

## Chapter 11 <br> Complex numbers

This chapter is for Pure Mathematics 3 students only.

In this chapter you will learn how to:

- understand the idea of a complex number, recall the meaning of the terms real part, imaginary part, modulus, argument and conjugate and use the fact that two complex numbers are equal if and only if both real and imaginary parts are equal

- carry out operations of addition, subtraction, multiplication and division of two complex numbers expressed in Cartesian form $x+\mathrm{i} y$

- use the result that, for a polynomial equation with real coefficients, any non-real roots occur in conjugate pairs

- represent complex numbers geometrically by means of an Argand diagram

- carry out operations of multiplication and division of two complex numbers expressed in polar form $r(\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta)=r \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta}$

- find the two square roots of a complex number

- understand in simple terms the geometrical effects of conjugating a complex number and of adding, subtracting, multiplying and dividing two complex numbers

- illustrate simple equations and inequalities involving complex numbers by means of loci in an Argand diagram.

## What are complex numbers?

To understand the structure of a complex number we must first introduce a new set of numbers, the set of imaginary numbers.

The number system we use today has developed over time as new sets of numbers have been needed to model new situations. Early mathematicians used natural numbers for counting. Later, zero and negative numbers were accepted as valid numbers and the set of integers was established. In Pythagoras' time, mathematicians understood that rational numbers existed as they were the values in between integers. Pythagorean mathematicians thought that all numbers could be written as rational numbers. These same mathematicians were shocked by the discovery of irrational numbers such as $\sqrt{2}$ and they did not trust them as valid quantities at the time. Even as late as the 17th and 18th centuries, some famous mathematicians still doubted that negative numbers were of any use and thought that subtracting something from nothing was an impossible operation!

We will start with some simple facts.

- In the set of natural or counting numbers $(\mathbb{N})$ there are no negatives and no zero.

- In the set of integers $(\mathbb{Z})$ there are no part or fractional values.

- In the set of rational numbers $(\mathbb{Q})$ there are no irrational quantities such as $\pi$.

- In the set of real numbers ( $\mathbb{R}$ ) there are no numbers that when squared result in a negative value.

Until now, the numbers with which we have been working have all been real numbers. That is, they belong to the set of real numbers, which includes the rational and irrational numbers. Real numbers can be represented on a 1-dimensional diagram called a number line.

In the 17th century, the set of imaginary numbers (I) was accepted as a valid number set. René Descartes described the collection of number sets that had been accepted as valid before that point as the set of real numbers. The new set, which he did not like very much, he described as imaginary. He intended this to be an insult as he thought they were not of great importance. Many other famous mathematicians agreed with him. They were, however, quite wrong about that. This name has often confused students who are new to this area of study.

## So what is an imaginary number and how is it related to a complex number?

In the new set of imaginary numbers there are numbers that when squared result in a negative value.

## KEY POINT 11.1

The set of complex numbers combines the real numbers and the imaginary numbers into a 2-dimensional rather than a 1-dimensional number system. Descartes described the numbers in the set of complex numbers as the sum of a real part and an imaginary part. Complex numbers are represented as points on a plane rather than points on a number line because of their 2-dimensional nature.

Clearly, accepting new sets of numbers as being valid is something mathematicians have struggled with over time. You might not feel comfortable with this new idea. The set of real, imaginary and complex numbers does have application in the real world. Without imaginary and, therefore, complex numbers there would be no mobile telephones, for example, as they are used to find audio signals. Complex numbers are used to generate fractals, which are used in the study of weather systems and earthquakes. Fractals are also used to make realistic computer-generated images for movies.

### 11.1 Imaginary numbers

In the set of imaginary numbers we introduce a new number i and we define $\mathrm{i}^{2}=-1$.

This means that $\mathrm{i}=\sqrt{-1}$.

Note that:

- Engineers call this quantity j as i is used in engineering for a different quantity.

- Just as with some other important numbers, i is described as a letter rather than a digit.

Consider the equation $x^{2}+1=0$.

How many solutions does this equation have in the set of real numbers?

$$
x^{2}+1=0 \quad x^{2}=-1 \quad x= \pm \sqrt{-1} \quad \text { No solutions in the set of real numbers. }
$$

How many solutions does this equation have in the set of imaginary numbers?

$$
x^{2}+1=0 \quad x^{2}=-1 \quad x= \pm \sqrt{-1}= \pm \mathrm{i} \quad \text { Two solutions in the set of imaginary numbers. }
$$

We can use laws of surds to extend the use of i to find other imaginary numbers.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.1

Write the following numbers in their simplest form.

**a** $\sqrt{-9}$

**b** $\sqrt{-5}$

**c** $\sqrt{-18}$

Answer

**a**

$$
\begin{align}
\sqrt{-9} & =\sqrt{9 \times-1} \\
& =\sqrt{9} \times \sqrt{-1}=3 \mathrm{i}
\end{align}
$$

When solving $x^{2}+1=0$ we needed ± in the solution. Why do we not need it here?

**b**

$$
\begin{align}
\sqrt{-5} & =\sqrt{5 \times-1} \\
& =\sqrt{5} \times \sqrt{-1}=\mathrm{i} \sqrt{5}
\end{align}
$$

When the multiplier is a simple surd, it is better to write the i before the surd to avoid any confusion with $\sqrt{5 \mathrm{i}}$ or we can write $(\sqrt{5}) \mathrm{i}$.

**c**

$$
\begin{align}
\sqrt{-18} & =\sqrt{2 \times 9 \times-1} \\
& =\sqrt{2} \times \sqrt{9} \times \sqrt{-1} \\
& =\sqrt{2} \times 3 \times \mathrm{i}=(3 \sqrt{2}) \mathrm{i}
\end{align}
$$

The imaginary number i is called the unit imaginary number as it has a coefficient of 1 .

All other imaginary numbers are multiples of i.

Imaginary numbers are therefore of the form $b$ i or

**i** $y$, where $b$ is a real number or $y$ is a real number.

We calculate with imaginary numbers in the same way that we calculate with real numbers.

TIP

Many calculators are able to calculate in the set of complex numbers. You must practise without a calculator but can use one to check your answer.

You need to demonstrate your ability to work with these numbers without a calculator. It is therefore important to practise writing down your method steps. Working without a calculator will also help to improve your understanding and recall of this subject (and do not forget that not all calculators are able to calculate with complex numbers).

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.2

Without using a calculator, find:

**a** $\left(3 \mathrm{i}^{2}\right)+(3 \mathrm{i})^{2}$

**b** $-8 \mathrm{i}+(-4 \mathrm{i})^{3}$

**c** $\sqrt{\frac{9 \mathrm{i}+16 \mathrm{i}}{4 \mathrm{i}}}$

**d** $\mathrm{i}^{-6}$.

Answer

**a** $\left(3 \mathrm{i}^{2}\right)+(3 \mathrm{i})^{2}=3 \mathrm{i}^{2}+9 \mathrm{i}^{2}=12 \mathrm{i}^{2}=-12$

**b** $-8 \mathrm{i}+(-4 \mathrm{i})^{3}=-8 \mathrm{i}+64 \mathrm{i}=56 \mathrm{i}$

**c** $\sqrt{\frac{9 \mathrm{i}+16 \mathrm{i}}{4 \mathrm{i}}}=\sqrt{\frac{25 \mathrm{i}}{4 \mathrm{i}}}=\sqrt{\frac{25}{4}}=\frac{5}{2}$

**d** $\quad \mathrm{i}^{-6}=\frac{1}{\mathrm{i}^{6}}=\frac{1}{\left(\mathrm{i}^{2}\right)^{3}}=\frac{1}{(-1)^{3}}=-1$

### 11.2 Complex numbers

A complex number is defined as a number of the form $x+\mathrm{i} y$, where $x$ and $y$ are real values and i is the unit imaginary number.

Note: Other general cases are common too, such as $a+b \mathrm{i}$, where $a$ and $b$ are real.

A complex number is often denoted by the letter $z$ so $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$ :

- the real number $x$ is called the real part of $z, \operatorname{Re} z$

- the real number $y$ is called the imaginary part of $z, \operatorname{Im} z$.

When $x=0$, then $z=\mathrm{i} y$ and $z$ is completely imaginary.

When $y=0$, then $z=x$ and $z$ is completely real.

The relationship between the set of complex numbers $(\mathbb{C})$ and other sets of numbers is often represented using nested rings.

Equal complex numbers

Two complex numbers are equal if and only if their real parts are equal and their imaginary parts are equal.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-270_297_615_272_1381.jpg)

So when $z_{1}=x+\mathrm{i} y$ and $z_{2}=a+b \mathrm{i}$ then $z_{1}=z_{2}$ means that $x=a$ and $y=b$.

Complex conjugate

The complex conjugate of $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$ is defined as $z^{*}=x-\mathrm{i} y$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.3

Solve the equation $5 z^{2}+14 z+13=0$.

Answer

Using the quadratic formula $a=5, b=14, c=13$.

$$
\begin{align}
& z=\frac{-14 \pm \sqrt{14^{2}-4(5)(13)}}{10} \\
& z=\frac{-14 \pm \sqrt{-64}}{10} \\
& z=\frac{-14 \pm \sqrt{64 \times-1}}{10} \\
& z=\frac{-14 \pm 8 \mathrm{i}}{10} \\
& z=-\frac{7}{5}+\frac{4 \mathrm{i}}{5} \text { or }-\frac{7}{5}-\frac{4 \mathrm{i}}{5}
\end{align}
$$

Note that the two roots are complex conjugates of each other.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.4

$$
(2 x+y)+\mathrm{i}(y-5)=0
$$

Find the value of $x$ and the value of $y$.

Answer

Two complex numbers are equal if and only if their real parts are equal and their imaginary parts are equal.

$$
(2 x+y)+\mathrm{i}(y-5)=0+0 \mathrm{i}
$$

Therefore

$$
\begin{equation*} 2 x+y=0 \tag{1} \end{equation*}
$$

and

$$
\begin{equation*} y-5=0 \tag{2} \end{equation*}
$$

From (2): $y=5$

Substituting into (1): $2 x+5=0, x=-\frac{5}{2}$

## Calculating with complex numbers

The processes we need to use are the same as those we use when manipulating expressions involving surds.

For example, when $z_{1}=2+3 \mathrm{i}$ and $z_{2}=8-\mathrm{i}$ we have:

| Addition | $\begin{aligned} & z_{1}+z_{2}=2+3 \mathrm{i}+8-\mathrm{i} \\ & z_{1}+z_{2}=10+2 \mathrm{i} \end{aligned}$ | Collect like terms. |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Subtraction | $\begin{aligned} & z_{1}-z_{2}=2+3 \mathrm{i}-(8-\mathrm{i}) \\ & z_{1}-z_{2}=-6+4 \mathrm{i} \end{aligned}$ | Collect like terms. |
| Multiplication | $\begin{aligned} & z_{1} z_{2}=(2+3 \mathrm{i})(8-\mathrm{i}) \\ & z_{1} z_{2}=16-2 \mathrm{i}+24 \mathrm{i}-3 \mathrm{i}^{2} \\ & z_{1} z_{2}=16+22 \mathrm{i}+3 \\ & z_{1} z_{2}=19+22 \mathrm{i} \end{aligned}$ | Expand the brackets and simplify. $i^{2}=-1$ <br> It is important to show all your working out clearly. |
|  | $\begin{aligned} & z_{1} z_{1}^{*}=(2+3 \mathrm{i})(2-3 \mathrm{i}) \\ & z_{1} z_{1}^{*}=4-6 \mathrm{i}+6 \mathrm{i}-9 \mathrm{i}^{2} \\ & z_{1} z_{1}^{*}=4+9 \\ & z_{1} z_{1}^{*}=13 \end{aligned}$ | Expand the brackets and simplify. $i^{2}=-1$ |
| Division | $\frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}=\frac{8-\mathrm{i}}{2+3 \mathrm{i}}$ | Multiply the numerator and denominator by the complex conjugate of $z_{1}$. |
|  | $\begin{aligned} & \frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}=\frac{(8-\mathrm{i})(2-3 \mathrm{i})}{(2+3 \mathrm{i})(2-3 \mathrm{i})} \\ & \frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}=\frac{16-24 \mathrm{i}-2 \mathrm{i}+3 \mathrm{i}^{2}}{13} \end{aligned}$ | Expand the brackets and simplify. $i^{2}=-1$ |
|  | $\begin{aligned} & \frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}=\frac{16-24 \mathrm{i}-2 \mathrm{i}-3}{13} \\ & \frac{z_{2}}{z_{1}}=\frac{13-26 \mathrm{i}}{13}=1-2 \mathrm{i} \end{aligned}$ | We have already found 13 so we did not show full working. You should usually show all your working to demonstrate you understand the method you are using. |

## KEY POINT 11.2

When we multiply a complex number by its conjugate the result is always a real number as, if we let $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$, and so $z^{*}=x-\mathrm{i} y$, then $z z^{*}=(x+\mathrm{i} y)(x-\mathrm{i} y)=x^{2}+y^{2}$. Since $x$ and $y$ are real, $x^{2}+y^{2}$ is also real.

### 11.3 The complex plane

As complex numbers have two dimensions, one real and one imaginary, a diagrammatical representation must have two dimensions. This means it has to be a plane and not a line. Jean-Robert Argand (1768-1822) is credited for the diagram used to do this.

Argand diagrams

The complex number $x+\mathrm{i} y$ can be represented by the Cartesian coordinates $(x, y)$.

The horizontal axis is called the real axis and the vertical axis is called the imaginary axis.

For example, we can represent the numbers

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-273_568_508_701_1093.jpg)

$z_{1}=2+3 \mathrm{i}$ and $z_{2}=5-3 \mathrm{i}$ by the points $A(2,3)$ and $B(5,-3)$ on an Argand diagram.

We can also represent the numbers $z_{1}=2+3 \mathrm{i}$ and $z_{2}=5-3 \mathrm{i}$ by the position vectors $\overrightarrow{O A}=\binom{2}{3}$ and $\overrightarrow{O B}=\binom{5}{-3}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-273_565_509_1116_220.jpg)

Modulus-argument form

When a complex number is written as $x+\mathrm{i} y$ where $x$ and $y$ are real values, we say it is in Cartesian form. There are other ways of writing complex numbers.

The vector representation of a complex number on an Argand diagram helps us to understand a different representation called the modulus-argument form.

Before we do this, we need to define the modulus and the argument of a complex number.

The modulus of a complex number $x+\mathrm{i} y$ is the magnitude of the position vector $\binom{x}{y}$.

Therefore, the modulus of $x+\mathrm{i} y$ is defined to be $\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}$.

Notation: The modulus of $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$ is $|z|$.

Also note that the plural of modulus is moduli.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-273_443_417_2030_1177.jpg)

The argument of a complex number $x+\mathrm{i} y$ is the direction of the position vector $\binom{x}{y}$.

More precisely, it is the angle made between the positive real axis and the position vector. The principal argument, $\theta$, of a complex number is an angle such that $-\pi<\theta \leqslant \pi$.

Sometimes it might be more convenient to give $\theta$ as an angle such that $0 \leqslant \theta<2 \pi$. Usually, the argument is given in radians.

Notation: The argument of $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$ is arg $z$.

We can use trigonometry to find the argument. For example, when $\theta$ is acute, $\tan \theta=\frac{y}{x}$.

A diagram must be drawn first to check the position of the complex number.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.5

Find the modulus and argument of each of the following complex numbers.

a 5 + 12i

b $-3+4 \mathrm{i}$

c $\quad 12-5 \mathrm{i}$

d -4-3i

Answer

A simple sketch of the Argand diagram is always necessary when finding the argument.

**a** $|5+12 \mathrm{i}|=\sqrt{5^{2}+12^{2}}=\sqrt{169}=13$. From the definition of the modulus.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-274_446_415_1310_427.jpg)

The diagram shows that the angle is in the first quadrant and so the angle is acute and positive.

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (5+12 \mathrm{i}) & =\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{12}{5}\right)=1.1760 \qquad \\
& =1.18 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

**b** $|-3+4 \mathrm{i}|=\sqrt{(-3)^{2}+4^{2}}=\sqrt{25}=5$. From the definition of the modulus.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-274_354_502_2082_542.jpg)

The diagram shows that the angle is in the second quadrant and is obtuse and positive. This is calculated by subtracting the value of the acute angle $\alpha$ from $\pi$. Remember that the principal argument is $-\pi<\theta \leqslant \pi$.

$$
\theta=\pi-\alpha
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (-3+4 \mathrm{i}) & =\pi-\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{4}{3}\right)=2.214 \qquad \\
& =2.21 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

**c**

$$
|12-5 \mathrm{i}|=\sqrt{12^{2}+(-5)^{2}}=\sqrt{169}=13 \qquad \text { From the definition of the modulus. }
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-275_548_562_601_356.jpg)

$$
\theta=-\alpha
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (12-5 \mathrm{i}) & =-\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{5}{12}\right)=-0.3947 \qquad \infty \qquad \text { From the definition of the argument. } \\
& =-0.395 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

**d** $|-4-3 \mathrm{i}|=\sqrt{(-4)^{2}+(-3)^{2}}=\sqrt{25}=5$ From the definition of the modulus.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-275_478_478_1539_570.jpg)

$$
\theta=-\pi+\alpha
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (-4-3 \mathrm{i}) & =-\pi+\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{3}{4}\right)=-2.498 \qquad \\
& =-2.50 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

Using trigonometry, we can now write a complex number in modulus-argument form. $\cos \theta=\frac{x}{r}$ and $\sin \theta=\frac{x}{r}$.

Therefore, $x=r \cos \theta$ and $y=r \sin \theta$.

Substituting these expressions into the Cartesian form of a complex number, we have

$$
\begin{align}
& z=x+\mathrm{i} y \\
& z=r \cos \theta+\mathrm{i}(r \sin \theta) \\
& z=r(\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta)
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-276_450_424_270_1564.jpg)

where $r$ is $|z|$ and $\theta$ is $\arg z$.

When a complex number is written as $z=r(\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta)$ where $r$ is $|z|$ and $\theta$ is $\arg z$, we say it is in modulus-argument form.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.6

$$
u=6-3 \mathrm{i} \text { and } w=-7+5 \mathrm{i}
$$

Write each of $u$ and $w$ in modulus-argument form.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& u=6-3 \mathrm{i} \\
& r=|u|=\sqrt{6^{2}+(-3)^{2}} \\
& =\sqrt{45}=3 \sqrt{5}
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-276_478_481_1445_468.jpg)

The diagram shows the angle to be acute and negative.

$$
\theta=-\alpha
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (6-3 \mathrm{i}) & =-\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{3}{6}\right) \\
& =-0.4636 \qquad \\
& =-0.464 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& u=3 \sqrt{5}(\cos (-0.464)+\mathrm{i} \sin (-0.464)) \\
& w=-7+5 \mathrm{i}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
r=|w| & =\sqrt{(-7)^{2}+5^{2}} \qquad \text { From the definition of the modulus } \\
& =\sqrt{74}
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-277_349_489_321_258.jpg)

The diagram shows the angle to be obtuse and positive.

$$
\begin{align}
& \theta=\pi-\alpha \\
&\begin{align}
\arg (-7+5 \mathrm{i}) & =\pi-\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{5}{7}\right) \\
& =2.5213 \qquad \\
& =2.52 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align} \\
&\begin{align}
w & =\sqrt{74}(\cos (2.52)+\mathrm{i} \sin (2.52))
\end{align}
\end{align}
$$

Exponential form

The mathematician Leonhard Euler (1707-1783) discovered the relationship $\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta=\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta}$.

This is the basis for another representation, called the exponential form.

$$
\begin{align}
& z=r(\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta) \\
& z=r \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta}
\end{align}
$$

where $r$ is $|z|$ and $\theta$ is $\arg z$.

The coordinates $(r, \theta)$, where $r$ is $|z|$ and $\theta$ is arg $z$, are called the polar coordinates.

When a complex number is written as $z=r \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta}$, where $r$ is $|z|$ and $\theta$ is $\arg z$, we say it is in exponential form.

The modulus-argument and exponential forms of a complex number are polar forms.

A polar form uses polar coordinates;

**a** Cartesian form uses Cartesian coordinates.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.7

$$
z_{1}=1+\mathrm{i}
$$

**a** Write $z_{1}$ in

- modulus-argument form

- exponential form.

$$
z_{2}=5 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{i \pi}{3}}
$$

**b** Write $z_{2}$ in

- modulus-argument form

- Cartesian form.

$$
z_{3}=2\left(\cos \frac{\pi}{12}+i \sin \frac{\pi}{12}\right)
$$

**c** Write $z_{3}$ in

- exponential form

- Cartesian form.

Answer

**a** ![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-278_430_447_360_321.jpg) The diagram shows the angle to be acute and positive.

$$
\begin{align}
& \left|z_{1}\right|=\sqrt{1^{2}+1^{2}}=\sqrt{2} \\
& \arg (1+\mathrm{i})=\tan ^{-1}(1)=\frac{\pi}{4}
\end{align}
$$

Modulus-argument form:

$$
z_{1}=\sqrt{2}\left(\cos \frac{\pi}{4}+i \sin \frac{\pi}{4}\right)
$$

Exponential form:

$$
z_{1}=\sqrt{2} \mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i} \pi}{4}}
$$

**b** $\left|z_{2}\right|=5 \quad \theta=\frac{\pi}{3}$

Modulus-argument form:

$$
z_{2}=5\left(\cos \frac{\pi}{3}+i \sin \frac{\pi}{3}\right)
$$

Comparing the modulus-argument form with $x+\mathrm{i} y$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-278_488_451_1665_321.jpg)

$$
\begin{align}
& x=5 \cos \frac{\pi}{3} \quad y=5 \sin \frac{\pi}{3} \qquad \text { Evaluate } \\
& x=\frac{5}{2} \quad y=\frac{5 \sqrt{3}}{2}
\end{align}
$$

Cartesian form:

$$
z_{2}=\frac{5}{2}+\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{5 \sqrt{3}}{2}\right)
$$

**c** $\left|z_{3}\right|=2 \quad \theta=\frac{\pi}{12}$

Exponential form: $z_{3}=2 \mathrm{e}^{\frac{\mathrm{i} \pi}{12}}$ Comparing the modulus-argument form with $x+\mathrm{i} y$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-279_371_502_521_360.jpg)

$$
\begin{align}
& x=2 \cos \frac{\pi}{12} \quad y=2 \sin \frac{\pi}{12} \\
& x=\frac{\sqrt{6}+\sqrt{2}}{2} \quad y=\frac{\sqrt{6}-\sqrt{2}}{2} \qquad \text { Evaluate } . \\
& \text { A. In Cartesian form }, x+\mathrm{i} y .
\end{align}
$$

Cartesian form: $z_{3}=\frac{\sqrt{6}+\sqrt{2}}{2}+\mathrm{i}\left(\frac{\sqrt{6}-\sqrt{2}}{2}\right)$

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.8

$$
u=5 \mathrm{e}^{2 \mathrm{i}} \text { and } w=10 \mathrm{e}^{-3 \mathrm{i}}
$$

**a** Find the modulus and argument of:

**i** $u w$

**ii** $\frac{w}{u}$

**b** Show $u, w, u w$ and $\frac{w}{u}$ on a single Argand diagram.

Answer

a i

$$
\begin{align}
& u w=5 \times \mathrm{e}^{2 \mathrm{i}} \times 10 \times \mathrm{e}^{-3 \mathrm{i}} \\
& u w=50 \times \mathrm{e}^{2 \mathrm{i}-3 \mathrm{i}} \\
& u w=50 \mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}}
\end{align}
$$

ii

$$
\begin{align}
\frac{w}{u} & =\frac{10 \times \mathrm{e}^{-3 \mathrm{i}}}{5 \times \mathrm{e}^{2 \mathrm{i}}} \\
\frac{w}{u} & =2 \times \mathrm{e}^{-3 \mathrm{i}-2 \mathrm{i}} \\
\frac{w}{u} & =2 \mathrm{e}^{-5 \mathrm{i}}
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-279_450_526_2111_1040.jpg)

-5 is less than $-\pi$ and so this argument is not a principal argument.

The equivalent principal argument, in this example, is $\theta=2 \pi-5$

$$
\frac{w}{u}=2 \mathrm{e}^{(2 \pi-5) \mathrm{i}}
$$

b

$$
\begin{align}
u=5(\cos 2+\mathrm{i} \sin 2) & =-2.08+4.55 \mathrm{i} & & A \approx(-2,4.5) \\
w=10(\cos (-3)+\mathrm{i} \sin (-3)) & =-9.90-1.41 \mathrm{i} & & B \approx(-10,1.5) \\
u w=50(\cos (-1)+\mathrm{i} \sin (-1)) & =27.0-42.1 \mathrm{i} & & C \approx(27,42) \\
\frac{w}{u}=2(\cos (2 \pi-5)+\mathrm{i} \sin (2 \pi-5)) & =0.567+1.92 \mathrm{i} & & D \approx(0.5,2)
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-280_472_431_600_324.jpg)

Using laws of indices and the exponential form of a complex number, we can derive some very important and useful results.

Let the complex number $z_{1}$ be such that $\left|z_{1}\right|=r_{1}$ and $\arg z_{1}=\theta_{1}$ and let the complex number $z_{2}$ be such that $\left|z_{2}\right|=r_{2}$ and $\arg z_{2}=\theta_{2}$ then:

$$
z_{1}=r_{1} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta_{1}}, z_{2}=r_{2} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta_{2}}, z_{1} z_{2}=r_{1} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta_{1}} \times r_{2} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta_{2}}=r_{1} r_{2} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}\right)} \text { and } \frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}=\frac{r_{1} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta_{1}}}{r_{2} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta_{2}}}=\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}} \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i}\left(\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\right)}
$$

Therefore, the complex number $z_{1} z_{2}$ is such that:

$$
\left|z_{1} z_{2}\right|=r_{1} r_{2}=\left|z_{1}\right|\left|z_{2}\right| \text { and } \arg \left(z_{1} z_{2}\right)=\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}=\arg z_{1}+\arg z_{2},
$$

and the complex number $\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}$ is such that:

$$
\left|\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\right|=\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}=\frac{\left|z_{1}\right|}{\left|z_{2}\right|} \text { and } \arg \left(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\right)=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=\arg z_{1}-\arg z_{2} .
$$

These results greatly simplify the multiplication and division of complex numbers.

We can see that, when in exponential form,

- to multiply two complex numbers we multiply the moduli and add the arguments

- to divide two complex numbers we divide the moduli and subtract the arguments.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.9

The complex numbers $u$ and $w$ are given by $u=9+12 \mathrm{i}$ and $w=-5-3 \mathrm{i}$.

Find the modulus and argument of:

**a** $u w$

**b** $\frac{u}{w}$

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& |u|=\sqrt{9^{2}+12^{2}}=\sqrt{225}=15 \\
& |w|=\sqrt{(-5)^{2}+(-3)^{2}}=\sqrt{34}
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-281_435_402_310_359.jpg)

From the diagram, the angle for $u$ is acute and positive.

$$
\begin{align}
\arg u & =\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{12}{9}\right)=0.9272 \qquad \\
& =0.927 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-281_365_429_915_360.jpg)

$$
\begin{align}
& \theta=-\pi+\alpha \\
& \arg w=-\pi+\tan ^{-1}\left(\frac{3}{5}\right)=-2.601 \\
& =-2.60 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

**a**

$$
|u w|=|u||w|=15 \sqrt{34} \qquad \text { Using }\left|z_{1} z_{2}\right|=r_{1} r_{2}=\left|z_{1}\right|\left|z_{2}\right|
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (u w) & =\arg u+\arg w=0.9272 \qquad +(-2.601 \qquad ) \qquad \cup \operatorname{Using} \arg \left(z_{1} z_{2}\right)=\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}=\arg z_{1}+\arg z_{2} \\
& =-1.67 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

**b**

$$
\left|\frac{u}{w}\right|=\frac{|u|}{|w|}=\frac{15}{\sqrt{34}}=\frac{15 \sqrt{34}}{34} \qquad \text { Using }\left|\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\right|=\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}=\frac{\left|z_{1}\right|}{\left|z_{2}\right|}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
\arg \left(\frac{u}{w}\right) & =\arg u-\arg w=0.9272 \qquad -(-2.601 \qquad ) \qquad \quad \text { Using } \arg \left(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\right)=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=\arg z_{1}-\arg z_{2} \\
& =3.528 \text { radians }
\end{align}
$$

However, $3.528$ is more than $\pi$ and so this argument is not a principal argument. The equivalent principal argument, in this example, is

$$
\begin{align}
\theta & =-(2 \pi-3.528 \qquad ) \\
& =-2.754 \qquad =-2.75 \text { radians correct to } 3 \text { significant figures }
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-281_460_732_2115_1256.jpg)
