### 11.4 Solving equations

We have already solved quadratic equations with complex roots.

From the Pure Mathematics 1 Coursebook, Chapter 1, we know that when solving quadratic equations for solutions that are real:

| $\boldsymbol{b}^{\mathbf{2}} \boldsymbol{-} \mathbf{4} \boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{c}$ | Nature of roots | Roots |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| > 0 | 2 real and distinct roots | $\alpha$ and $\beta$ |
| = 0 | 2 real and equal roots (repeated roots) | $\alpha$ and $\beta, \alpha=\beta$ |
| < 0 | 0 real roots |  |

We can now update this for solving quadratic equations for solutions that are complex:

| $\boldsymbol{b}^{\mathbf{2}} \boldsymbol{-} \mathbf{4} \boldsymbol{a} \boldsymbol{c}$ | Nature of roots | Roots |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| > 0 | 2 real and distinct roots | $\alpha$ and $\beta$ |
| = 0 | 2 real and equal roots (repeated roots) | $\alpha$ and $\beta, \alpha=\beta$ |
| < 0 | 2 complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$ (a complex conjugate pair). <br> This means that one root is the conjugate of the other root. | $\alpha$ and $\alpha^{*}$ |

Using the set of complex numbers to find solutions, a quadratic equation always has two roots.

## Higher order polynomial equations

We solved cubic and quartic equations for real roots in Chapter 1.

We will now consider solving these equations for complex roots.

The Fundamental Theorem of Algebra states that a polynomial equation of degree $n$ has $n$ complex roots.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.10

**a** Show that $z=4$ is a root of the equation $z^{3}-3 z^{2}-3 z-4=0$.

**b** Find all complex solutions of $z^{3}-3 z^{2}-3 z-4=0$.

Answer

**a** Let $\mathrm{f}(z)=z^{3}-3 z^{2}-3 z-4$ then

$$
\begin{align}
f(4) & =4^{3}-3\left(4^{2}\right)-3(4)-4 \\
& =64-48-12-4=64-64=0
\end{align}
$$

**b** As $z=4$ is a root then $z-4$ is a factor, therefore

$$
z^{3}-3 z^{2}-3 z-4=(z-4)\left(z^{2}+a z+1\right) \qquad \text { Multiply out and compare. }
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& z^{3}-3 z^{2}-3 z-4= z^{3}+\quad a z^{2}+\quad z \\
& \frac{-\quad 4 z^{2}-\quad 4 a z-4}{z^{3}+(a-4) z^{2}+(1-4 a) z-4} \qquad \text { This can also be done by synthetic or long } \\
& \text { division. }
\end{align}
$$

$$
z^{3}-3 z^{2}-3 z-4=z^{3}+(a-4) z^{2}+(1-4 a) z-4 \qquad \text { Compare the coefficients of } z^{2} .
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& -3=a-4, \text { therefore } a=1 \\
& -3=1-4(1) ✓ \\
& (z-4)\left(z^{2}+z+1\right)=0 \\
& \quad z=4
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
z^{2}+z+1 & =0 \\
z & =\frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1^{2}-4(1)(1)}}{2} \\
z & =\frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{-3}}{2} \\
z & =\frac{-1 \pm i \sqrt{3}}{2}
\end{align}
$$

KEY POINT 11.3

When a polynomial equation with real coefficients has complex roots, they occur in complex conjugate pairs.

A cubic equation has three roots (by the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra).

A cubic equation can always be written as the product of a linear factor and a quadratic factor.

This means that a cubic equation must always have at least one real root.

Cubic equations either have

- three real roots or

- one real root and two complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$ (a complex conjugate pair).

A quartic equation has four roots (by the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra).

Quartic equations either have

- four real roots or

- two real and two complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$ (a complex conjugate pair) or

- four complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$ (two complex conjugate pairs).

A quartic equation can never have one real and three complex roots. Make sure you understand why this is true.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.11

It is given that $z-2 \mathrm{i}$ is a factor of the equation $z^{4}+13 z^{2}+36=0$. Solve this equation.

Answer

As $z-2 \mathrm{i}$ is a factor $z+2 \mathrm{i}$ must also be a factor

Complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$ occur in pairs.

$$
\begin{align}
(z-2 \mathrm{i})(z+2 \mathrm{i}) & =z^{2}+2 \mathrm{i} z-2 \mathrm{i} z-4 \mathrm{i}^{2} \\
& =z^{2}+4
\end{align}
$$

$$
z^{4}+13 z^{2}+36=\left(z^{2}+4\right)\left(z^{2}+k z+9\right) \quad \text { Multiply out and compare. }
$$

$$
\begin{align}
z^{4}+13 z^{2}+36= & z^{4}+k z^{3}+9 z^{2} \\
& \frac{+4 z^{2}+4 k z+36}{z^{4}+k z^{3}+13 z^{2}+4 k z+36}
\end{align}
$$

This can also be done by synthetic or long division.

$$
z^{4}+13 z^{2}+36=z^{4}+k z^{3}+13 z^{2}+4 k z+36
$$

Compare the coefficients of $z^{3}$.

$$
\begin{align}
& k=0 \\
& 0=4(0) \\
& z^{4}+13 z^{2}+36=\left(z^{2}+4\right)\left(z^{2}+9\right)=0
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& z= \pm 2 \mathrm{i} \\
& z^{2}+9=0 \text { so } z= \pm \sqrt{-9} \\
& z= \pm 3 \mathrm{i}
\end{align}
$$

$$
z= \pm 2 \mathrm{i}, z= \pm 3 \mathrm{i}
$$

## Finding the square roots of a complex number

We can find the square roots of a complex number.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.12

Find the square roots of $-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i}$ and show the position of these roots on an Argand diagram.

Answer

Find $x+\mathrm{i} y$, where $x$ and $y$ are real, such that $x+\mathrm{i} y= \pm \sqrt{-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i}}$.

$$
\begin{align}
& x+\mathrm{i} y= \pm \sqrt{-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i}} \\
& (x+\mathrm{i} y)^{2}=-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i} \\
& x^{2}+2 x y \mathrm{i}+\mathrm{i}^{2} y^{2}=-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i} \qquad \text { Square both sides. } \\
& \text { Multiply out the left-hand side. } \\
& \left(x^{2}-y^{2}\right)+(2 x y) \mathrm{i}=-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i} \tag{1} \\
& x^{2}-y^{2}=-2 \qquad \text { Eqllect real and imaginary parts. }
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{equation*} 2 x y=2 \sqrt{3} \tag{2} \end{equation*}
$$

Solve simultaneously.

From (2): $y=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{x}$ and so $x^{2}-\frac{3}{x^{2}}=-2$ Multiply by $x^{2}$.

$$
\begin{align}
x^{4}-3 & =-2 x^{2} & & \text { Rearrange and let } u=x^{2} . \\
u^{2}+2 u-3 & =0 & & \\
(u+3)(u-1) & =0 & & \text { Solve. } \\
u=-3, u & =1 & & \\
x^{2}=-3, x^{2} & =1 & & x \text { is real, so disregard } x^{2}=-3 .
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
x & = \pm 1 \qquad \text { Substitute into } y=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{x} . \\
y=\frac{\sqrt{3}}{ \pm 1} & = \pm \sqrt{3}
\end{align}
$$

Therefore, the square roots of $-2-(2 \sqrt{3}) \mathrm{i}$. Notice that the modulus of the are $1+\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}$ and $-1-\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}$.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-286_305_307_2270_225.jpg)

The cube roots of one (unity)

By the Fundamental Theorem of Algebra, $z^{3}-1=0$ has three roots.

We know that one of these roots is 1 , since $z=\sqrt[3]{1}=1$ is the real solution we are familiar with.

To find the two complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$, we can use this method:

As $z=1$ is a root then $z-1$ is a factor and so

$$
\begin{align}
z^{3}-1 & =(z-1)\left(z^{2}+k z+1\right) & & \text { Multiply out and compare. } \\
z^{3}-1 & =z^{3}+k z^{2}+z & & \text { This can also be done by synthetic or } \\
& \frac{-z^{2}-k z-1}{z^{3}+(k-1) z^{2}+(1-k) z-1} & & \text { long division. } \\
z^{3}-1 & =z^{3}+(k-1) z^{2}+(1-k) z-1 & & \text { Compare the coefficients of } z^{2} . \\
k-1 & =0, k=1 & & \text { Check, using the coefficients of } z . \\
0 & =1-1 ✓ & \text { Solve. } &
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& z=1, z=\frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{1^{2}-4(1)(1)}}{2}, z=\frac{-1 \pm \sqrt{-3}}{2} \\
& z=1, z=\frac{-1+\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}}{2}, z=\frac{-1-\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}}{2}
\end{align}
$$

These roots can be very useful when solving related equations.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.13

Solve $z^{3}=8$.

Answer

$$
\begin{array}{r} z=\sqrt[3]{8}=2 \sqrt[3]{1} \quad \text { As } z \text { is } 2 \times \sqrt[3]{1} \text { replace } \sqrt[3]{1} \text { by each of the cube } \\
\text { roots of } 1 \text { we have already found. } \end{array}
$$

$$
z=2 \times 1, z=2 \times \frac{-1+\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}}{2}, z=2 \times \frac{-1-\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}}{2} \qquad \text { Simplify. }
$$

$$
z=2, z=-1+\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}, z=-1-\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}
$$

### 11.5 Loci

A locus is a path traced out by a point as it moves following a particular rule. The rule is expressed as an inequality or an equation.

In order to draw the correct locus on an Argand diagram, we need to interpret the inequality or equation we have been given.

Circles with centre $(0,0)$

- $|z|=r, \quad|z|<r, \quad|z| \leqslant r \quad$ where $r$ is a constant.

In this case, the length of the position vector representing the complex number, $z$, is the constant value $r$. The argument of $z$ can vary.

A point that moves so that is it always the same distance from a fixed point is a circle. The fixed point is the centre of the circle. The fixed distance is the radius of the circle.

Recall that a position vector is simply a means of locating a point in space relative to an origin, usually $O$.

For the complex number $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$, where $x$ and $y$ are real, the locus of the point $\mathrm{P}(x, y)$ that satisfies:

- $|z|=r$ is the circumference of a circle with centre $(0,0)$ and radius $r$

- $|z|<r$ is all points within a circle with centre $(0,0)$ and radius $r$, but the circumference is not included

- $|z| \leqslant r$ is all points within a circle with centre $(0,0)$ and radius $r$ or the circumference of the circle.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.14

On separate Argand diagrams, sketch these loci.

**a** $|z|=5$

**b** $|z|<3$

**c** $|z| \leqslant 4$

Answer

**a** $|z|=5$ is the circumference of a circle, centre (0, 0), radius 5.

It is only the circle, not the points within the circle.

The circle is drawn as a solid line as it is included.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-289_808_830_1524_1115.jpg)

**b** $|z|<3$ is a circle, centre (0, 0), radius 3.

It is only the points within the circle.

The circumference of the circle is drawn as a dotted line as it is not included.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-290_700_732_313_1156.jpg)

**c** $|z| \leqslant 4$ is a circle, centre $(0,0)$, radius 4.

It is all points within the circle and on the circumference.

The circumference of the circle is drawn as a solid line as it is included.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-290_697_757_1073_1148.jpg)

Circles with centre $(\boldsymbol{a}, \boldsymbol{b})$

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right|=r,\left|z-z_{1}\right|<r,\left|z-z_{1}\right| \leqslant r$ where $r$ is a constant and $z_{1}$ is the fixed point $a+b \mathrm{i}$.

We need to understand the vector representation of the complex number $z-z_{1}$.

Let $P(x, y)$ be the moving point that represents $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$ on an Argand diagram.

Let $Q(a, b)$ be the fixed point that represents $z_{1}=a+b \mathrm{i}$ on the Argand diagram.

$$
z=\overrightarrow{O P} \text { and } z_{1}=\overrightarrow{O Q}
$$

Therefore, $\overrightarrow{Q P}=-z_{1}+z$

$$
\overrightarrow{Q P}=z-z_{1}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-290_605_599_2021_1329.jpg)

We now interpret $\left|z-z_{1}\right|=r$ as:

the length of the vector $\overrightarrow{Q P}$ is $r$.

For the complex number $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$, where $x$ and $y$ are real, and the fixed point $z_{1}=a+b \mathrm{i}$, where $a$ and $b$ are real, the locus of the point $P(x, y)$ that satisfies

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right|=r$ is the circumference of a circle with centre $(a, b)$ and radius $r$

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right|<r$ is all points within a circle with centre $(a, b)$ and radius $r$, but the circumference is not included

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right| \leqslant r$ is all points within a circle with centre $(a, b)$ and radius $r$ or the circumference of the circle.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.15

On separate Argand diagrams, sketch these loci.

**a** $|z-(2+4 \mathrm{i})|=3$

**b** $|z-3 \mathrm{i}|<4$

**c** $|z+5| \leqslant 5$

Answer

**a** $z_{1}=2+4 \mathrm{i}$

$|z-(2+4 \mathrm{i})|=3$ is the circumference of a circle, centre (2, 4), radius 3.

It is only the circle, not the points within the circle.

The circle is drawn as a solid line as it is included.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-291_601_659_1161_1255.jpg)

**b** $z_{1}=3 \mathrm{i}$

$|z-3 \mathrm{i}|<4$ is a circle, centre (0, 3), radius 4.

It is only the points within the circle.

The circumference of the circle is drawn as a dotted line as it is not included.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-291_682_702_1771_1240.jpg)

**c** $z-z_{1}=z+5=z-(-5) \quad$ Take care with signs.

$$
\begin{align}
& z_{1}=-5 \\
& |z+5| \leqslant 2 \text { is a circle, centre }(-5,0), \text { radius } 2 .
\end{align}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-292_500_590_432_1357.jpg)

It is all points within the circle and on the circumference.

The circumference of the circle is drawn as a solid line as it is included.

## Half-lines and part-lines

- $\arg \left(z-z_{1}\right)=\alpha$, where $\alpha$ is a fixed angle and $z_{1}$ is the fixed point $a+b \mathrm{i}$.

With $z-z_{1}$ defined as previously

$$
z=\overrightarrow{O P}, z_{1}=\overrightarrow{O Q} \text { and } \overrightarrow{Q P}=z-z_{1}
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-292_511_519_1071_1438.jpg)

We now define $\arg \left(z-z_{1}\right)$ as the angle made between the direction parallel to the real axis and the direction of the vector $\overrightarrow{Q P}$.

Points below $Q$ are not part of the locus as the angle made between these points and the relevant horizontal line is $\pi-\alpha$, not $\alpha$.

- $\arg \left(z_{1}-z\right)=\beta$, where $\beta$ is a fixed angle and $z_{1}$ is the fixed point $a+b \mathrm{i}$.

With $z_{1}-z=-\left(z-z_{1}\right)$,

$$
z=\overrightarrow{O P}, z_{1}=\overrightarrow{O Q} \text { and } \overrightarrow{P Q}=z_{1}-z
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-292_508_519_1820_1416.jpg)

We now define $\arg \left(z_{1}-z\right)$ as the angle made between the direction parallel to the real axis and the direction of the vector $\overrightarrow{P Q}$.

Points above $P$ are not part of the locus as the angle made between these points and the relevant horizontal line is $\pi-\beta$, not $\beta$.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.16

On a single Argand diagram, sketch the loci $|z|=4$ and $\arg (z+2+3 \mathrm{i})=\frac{\pi}{4}$.

Show that there is only one complex number, $z$, that satisfies both loci.

Label this point as $P$ on your diagram.

Answer

$$
|z|=4
$$

Circle, centre (0, 0), radius 4

$$
\begin{align}
\arg (z+2+3 \mathrm{i}) & =\frac{\pi}{4} \\
z-z_{1} & =z+2+3 \mathrm{i} \\
& =z-(-2-3 \mathrm{i}) \\
z_{1} & =-2-3 \mathrm{i}
\end{align}
$$

Half-line from (-2, -3) at an angle of $\frac{\pi}{4}$ radians.

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-293_800_899_1191_656.jpg)

## Perpendicular bisectors

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right|=\left|z-z_{2}\right|,\left|z-z_{1}\right|<\left|z-z_{2}\right|,\left|z-z_{1}\right| \leqslant\left|z-z_{2}\right|$ where $z_{1}$ is the fixed point $a+b \mathrm{i}$ and $z_{2}$ is the fixed point $c+d \mathrm{i}$.

Let $P(x, y)$ be the moving point that represents $z=x+\mathrm{i} y$ on an Argand diagram, $Q(a, b)$ be the fixed point that represents $z_{1}=a+b \mathrm{i}$ on the Argand diagram, $R(c, d)$ be the fixed point that represents $z_{2}=c+d \mathrm{i}$ on the Argand diagram, then

- when $\left|z-z_{1}\right|=\left|z-z_{2}\right|$, the point $P(x, y)$ moves so that it is always the same distance from $Q$ as it is from $R$. The locus is, therefore, the perpendicular bisector of the line $Q R$.

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right|<\left|z-z_{2}\right|$ is all points such that $P$ is nearer to $Q$ than to $R$. The perpendicular bisector marks the boundary of this region but is not included.

- $\left|z-z_{1}\right| \leqslant\left|z-z_{2}\right|$ is all points such that $P$ is nearer to $Q$ than to $R$ or equidistant from $Q$ and $R$. The perpendicular bisector marks the boundary of this region and is included.

#### WORKED EXAMPLE 11.17

On an Argand diagram, sketch the locus $|z-4+\mathrm{i}|=|z+5 \mathrm{i}|$.

Find the Cartesian equation of this locus.

Answer

$$
\begin{align}
& |z-4+\mathrm{i}|=|z-(4-\mathrm{i})| \\
& z_{1}=4-\mathrm{i}
\end{align}
$$

$$
\begin{align}
& |z+5 i|=|z-(-5 i)| \\
& z_{2}=-5 i
\end{align}
$$

Midpoint of $Q P,(4,-1)$ and (0, -5),

is $\left(\frac{4+0}{2}, \frac{-1+(-5)}{2}\right)=(2,-3)$

The locus is the perpendicular bisector of (4, -1) and (0, -5).

Gradient of $Q P=1$ and so the gradient of perpendicular bisector $=-1$.

Cartesian equation is $y+3=-1(x-2)$

$$
y=-x-1
$$

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-294_637_627_1760_864.jpg)

## Checklist of learning and understanding

$$
i^{2}=-1
$$

Cartesian form: $x+\mathrm{i} y$ where $x$ and $y$ are real values.

Arithmetic operations on $\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathbf{1}}=\boldsymbol{a}+\boldsymbol{b} \mathbf{i}$ and $\boldsymbol{z}_{\mathbf{2}}=\boldsymbol{c}+\boldsymbol{d} \mathbf{i}$ :

- Addition:

$$
z_{1}+z_{2}=(a+c)+(b+d) \mathrm{i}
$$

- Subtraction:

$$
z_{1}-z_{2}=(a-c)+(b-d) \mathrm{i}
$$

- Multiplication:

$$
z_{1} z_{2}=(a c-b d)+(a d+b c) \mathrm{i}
$$

- Division:

$$
\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}=\frac{(a c+b d)+(b c-a d) \mathrm{i}}{c^{2}-d^{2}}
$$

- Modulus:

$$
|z|=\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}}
$$

- Argument: Found using a diagram with $\tan \theta=\frac{y}{x}$

$$
\begin{align}
& \left|z_{1} z_{2}\right|=r_{1} r_{2}=\left|z_{1}\right|\left|z_{2}\right| \text { and } \arg \left(z_{1} z_{2}\right)=\theta_{1}+\theta_{2}=\arg z_{1}+\arg z_{2}, \\
& \left|\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\right|=\frac{r_{1}}{r_{2}}=\frac{\left|z_{1}\right|}{\left|z_{2}\right|} \text { and } \arg \left(\frac{z_{1}}{z_{2}}\right)=\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}=\arg z_{1}-\arg z_{2}
\end{align}
$$

Polar forms

- Modulus-argument form: $r(\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta)$

- Exponential form: $r \mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{i} \theta}$

Roots of equations occur in complex conjugate pairs

| - Quadratic equations have | 2 real | or | 2 complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$. |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| - Cubic equations have | 3 real | or | 1 real and 2 complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$. |
| - Quartic equations have | 4 real | or | 2 real and 2 complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$, |
|  |  | or | 4 complex roots of the form $x \pm \mathrm{i} y$ with $y \neq 0$. |

The cube roots of one are: $z=1, z=\frac{-1+\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}}{2}, z=\frac{-1-\mathrm{i} \sqrt{3}}{2}$

a Find the real values $x$ and $y$ such that $z^{*}=(2 x+1)+(4 x+y) \mathrm{i}$. [2]

i in the form $x+\mathrm{i} y$ where $x$ and $y$ are real [3]

ii in the form $r(\cos \theta+\mathrm{i} \sin \theta)$, where $r>0$ and $-\pi<\theta \leqslant \pi$. [3]

**b**

## CROSS-TOPIC REVIEW EXERCISE 4

This exercise is for Pure Mathematics 3 students only.

**1** Relative to the origin $O$, the position vectors of the points $A$ and $B$ are given by

$$
\overrightarrow{O A}=\left(\begin{array}{r} -5 \\
0 \\
3 \end{array}\right) \text { and } \overrightarrow{O B}=\left(\begin{array}{l} 1 \\
7 \\
2 \end{array}\right) .
$$

**a** Find a vector equation of the line $A B$. [3]

**b** The line $A B$ is perpendicular to the line $L$ with vector equation:

$$
\mathbf{r}=\left(\begin{array}{r} 4 \\
2 \\
-3 \end{array}\right)+\mu\left(\begin{array}{c} m \\
3 \\
9 \end{array}\right) .
$$

**i** Find the value of $m$. [3]

**ii** Show that the line $A B$ and the line $L$ do not intersect. [4]

$$

$$

obtaining an expression for $y^{2}$ in terms of $x$. [6]

$$

**a** Find the value of each of the constants $p$ and $q$. [2]

**b** The position vector of $Q$, relative to the origin $O$, is $\overrightarrow{O Q}=-10 \mathbf{i}+\mathbf{j}-5 \mathbf{k}$.

**i** Find the unit vector in the direction $\overrightarrow{O Q}$. [2]

**ii** Find angle $P O Q$ and hence find the exact area of triangle $P O Q$. [5]

**a** $2 z+z^{*}$,

**b** $\frac{\mathrm{i} z^{*}}{z}$. [4]

iii On a sketch of an Argand diagram with origin $O$, show the points $A$ and $B$ representing the complex numbers $z$ and $\mathrm{i} z^{*}$ respectively. Prove that angle $A O B=\frac{1}{6} \pi$. [3]

**9** A certain substance is formed in a chemical reaction. The mass of substance formed $t$ seconds after the start of the reaction is $x$ grams. At any time the rate of formation of the substance is proportional to $(20-x)$. When $t=0, x=0$ and $\frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=1$.

**i** Show that $x$ and $t$ satisfy the differential equation

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=0.05(20-x) .
$$

[2]

\frac{\mathrm{d} R}{\mathrm{~d} x}=R\left(\frac{1}{x}-0.57\right),
$$

where $R$ and $x$ are taken to be continuous variables. When $x=0.5, R=16.8$.

**i** Solve the differential equation and obtain an expression for $R$ in terms of $x$. [6]

**30** Liquid is flowing into a small tank which has a leak. Initially the tank is empty and $t$ minutes later, the volume of liquid in the tank is $V \mathrm{~cm}^{3}$. Because of the leak, liquid is being lost from the tank at a rate which, at any instant, is equal to $k V \mathrm{~cm}^{3}$ per minute where $k$ is a positive constant.

## PURE MATHEMATICS 2 PRACTICE EXAM-STYLE PAPER

Time allowed is 1 hour 15 minutes (50 marks)

**1** By writing $\cot x$ as $\frac{\cos x}{\sin x}$ show that $\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d} x}(\cot x)=-\operatorname{cosec}^{2} x$. [3]

**2** Solve the inequality $|3 x-1| \geqslant|2 x|$. [4]

**3** Solve the equation $3^{2 x}-3^{x+1}=10$ giving the value of $x$ correct to 3 significant figures. [5]

**4** The polynomial $x^{3}+8 x^{2}+p x-25$ leaves a remainder of $R$ when divided by $x-1$ and a remainder of $-R$ when divided by $x+2$.

**a** Find the value of $p$. [4]

**b** Hence, find the remainder when the polynomial is divided by $x+3$. [2]

**5** The sequence of values given by the formula

$$
x_{n+1}=\sqrt{\frac{8 x_{n}^{2}}{3 \sec x_{n}}},
$$

with initial value $x_{1}=1$, converges to $\alpha$.

**a** Use this formula to calculate $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places, showing the result of each iteration to 4 decimal places. [3]

**b** State an equation satisfied by $\alpha$ and hence find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 7 decimal places. [3]

**6** The parametric equations of a curve are $x=\ln (2 t+1), y=t-\mathrm{e}^{2 t}$.

**a** Find an expression for $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ in terms of $t$.

**b** Find the equation of the normal to the curve at the point where $t=0$.

**7** **a** Express $8 \sin \theta+6 \cos \theta$ in the form $R \sin (\theta+\alpha)$, where $R>0$ and $0^{\circ}<\alpha<90^{\circ}$, giving the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 decimal places. [3]

**b** Hence solve the equation $8 \sin \theta+6 \cos \theta=7$ giving all solutions in the interval $0^{\circ}<\theta<360^{\circ}$. [4]

**c** Write down the greatest value of $8 \sin \theta+6 \cos \theta+3$ as $\theta$ varies. [1]

**8** **a** Show that $\cos 3 x \equiv 4 \cos ^{3} x-3 \cos x$.

**b** Hence show that $\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}} \cos ^{3} x \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{2}{3}$. [5]

## PURE MATHEMATICS 3 PRACTICE EXAM-STYLE PAPER

Time allowed is 1 hour 50 minutes (75 marks)

**1** Use logarithms to solve the equation $\mathrm{e}^{2 x}=2^{x+5}$, giving your answers correct to 3 decimal places. [3]

**2** Show that $\int_{0}^{5} x \ln x \mathrm{~d} x=\frac{25}{4}(\ln 25-1)$. [5]

**3** The polynomial $2 x^{3}-9 x^{2}+a x+b$, where $a$ and $b$ are constants, is denoted by $\mathrm{f}(x)$. It is given that $x-4$ is a factor of $\mathrm{f}(x)$, and that when $\mathrm{f}(x)$ is divided by ( $x-1$ ) the remainder is -12.

Find the value of $a$ and the value of $b$. [5]

**4** Solve the equation $2 \sin \left(x-60^{\circ}\right)=3 \cos x$ for $-180^{\circ} \leqslant x \leqslant 180^{\circ}$. [5]

**5** ![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-308_393_594_870_828.jpg) The diagram shows the curve $y^{2}=4 x^{2}-x^{4}+5$. The curve is symmetrical about both axes.

The point $P$ is one of the curve's maximum points and the point $Q$ is one of the curve's minimum points. Find the exact distance between the points $P$ and $Q$. [7]

$6 \mathrm{f}(x)=\frac{7 x^{2}-5 x+27}{(x-2)\left(x^{2}+5\right)}$

**a** Express f( $x$ ) in partial fractions. [5]

**b** Hence obtain the expansion of $\mathrm{f}(x)$ in ascending powers of $x$, up to and including the term in $x^{2}$. [5]

**7** ![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-308_287_620_1685_814.jpg) The diagram shows the curve $y=x \sin 2 x$ for $0 \leqslant x \leqslant \frac{\pi}{2}$.

**a** Find $\frac{\mathrm{d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}$ and show that $x^{2} \frac{\mathrm{~d}^{2} y}{\mathrm{~d} x^{2}}-2 x \frac{\mathrm{~d} y}{\mathrm{~d} x}+2\left(1+2 x^{2}\right) y=0$. [5]

**b** Find the area of the region enclosed by this part of the curve and the $x$-axis. [5]

**8** **a** The complex numbers $u$ and $w$ are such that:

$$
\begin{align}
3 u-\mathrm{i} w & =15 \\
u+w & =5+10 \mathrm{i} .
\end{align}
$$

Without using a calculator, solve these equations to find $u$ and $w$, giving each answer in the form $x+\mathrm{i} y$, where $x$ and $y$ are real. [5]

**b** The complex number $z$ is defined by $z=\sqrt{2} \mathrm{e}^{-\frac{\pi}{4} \mathrm{i}}$. On an Argand diagram, show the points $A, B$ and $C$ representing the complex numbers $z, z^{*}$ and $z^{2}$, respectively and find the length of the longest side of triangle $A B C$. [4]

**9** **a** Given the vectors $5 \mathbf{i}+7 \mathbf{j}+p \mathbf{k}$ and $\mathbf{i}-2 \mathbf{j}+p \mathbf{k}$ are perpendicular, find the possible values of the constant $p$. [3]

**b** The line $L_{1}$ passes through the point $(5,0,2)$ and is parallel to the vector $\mathbf{i}-6 \mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k}$.

**i** Write down a vector equation of the line $L_{1}$. [2]

**ii** The line $L_{2}$ has vector equation:

$$
\mathbf{r}=4 \mathbf{j}+3 \mathbf{k}+\mu(3 \mathbf{i}+9 \mathbf{j}+\mathbf{k})
$$

Show that $L_{1}$ and $L_{2}$ do not intersect. [4]

**10** A liquid is heated so that its temperature, $x^{\circ} \mathrm{C}$, at time $t$ seconds satisfies the differential equation

$$
\frac{\mathrm{d} x}{\mathrm{~d} t}=\alpha(100-x)
$$

where $\alpha$ is a positive constant. The temperature at $t=0$ is 25°C.

**a** Show that $\ln \left(\frac{75}{100-x}\right)=\alpha t$. [6]

**b** It is given that $x=500 \alpha$ when $t=2$. Show that $\alpha=0.2-0.15 \mathrm{e}^{-2 \alpha}$. [2]

**c** Use an iterative formula based on the equation in part b to find the value of $\alpha$ correct to 2 significant figures. Use a starting value of 0.1 and show the result of each iteration to 4 significant figures. [3]

**d** Find the temperature of the liquid when $t=30$. [1]

$$
\begin{align}
& \ln 7 x=\ln 7+\ln x \\
& \frac{\mathrm{~d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 3 x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 3+\ln x)=0+\frac{1}{x} \text { and } \\
& \frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 7 x)=\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{~d} x}(\ln 7+\ln x)=0+\frac{1}{x}
\end{align}
$$

**1** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-327_442_573_1881_350.jpg)

b 2 points of intersection so 2 roots.

c Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{2}-\sqrt{1+x}=0$ then $\mathrm{f}(-1)=(-1)^{2}-\sqrt{1+(-1)}=1$ and $\mathrm{f}(0)=(0)^{2}-\sqrt{1+0}=-1$. Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**2** **a**

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-327_509_637_275_1291.jpg)

**3** points of intersection, so 3 roots.

b Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{3}+5 x^{2}+2 x-5=0$ then $\mathrm{f}(0)=0^{3}+5(0)^{2}+2(0)-5=-5$ and $\mathrm{f}(2)=2^{3}+5(2)^{2}+2(2)-5=27$. Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**3** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-327_505_604_1115_1291.jpg)

One point of intersection so one solution only of $x^{3}+5 x-1=0$.

b Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{3}+5 x-1$ then $\mathrm{f}(0.1)=0.1^{3}+5(0.1)-1=-0.499$ and $\mathrm{f}(0.5)=0.5^{3}+5(0.5)-1=1.625$. Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**4** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-327_449_552_1999_1287.jpg)

b $\ln (x+1)=3 x-4$ in 2 places and so $\ln (x+1)-3 x+4=0$ has 2 roots.

**5** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-328_444_544_277_308.jpg)

Graphs intersect at 2 points, so 2 roots.

b Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=\mathrm{e}^{x}-x-6$ then

$$
\begin{align}
& f(2.0)=e^{2}-2-6=-0.610 \qquad \text { and } \\
& f(2.1)=e^{2.1}-2.1-6=0.0661 \qquad \text { Change of }
\end{align}
$$

sign indicates presence of root.

**6** **a** Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=(x+2) \mathrm{e}^{5 x}-1=0$ then $\mathrm{f}(0)=(0+2) \mathrm{e}^{0}-1=1$ and

$$
f(-0.2)=(-0.2+2) e^{-1}-1=-0.337
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**b** ![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-328_450_552_1284_308.jpg) Graphs intersect at 1 point, so 1 root only.

**7** **a** Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=\cos ^{-1} 2 x-1+x=0$ then

$$
\begin{align}
& f(0.4)=\cos ^{-1}(0.8)-1+0.4=0.0435 \qquad \text { and } \\
& f(0.5)=\cos ^{-1} 1-1+0.5=-0.5 . \text { Change of }
\end{align}
$$

sign indicates presence of root.

**b** ![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-328_600_615_2062_311.jpg) One point of intersection for $-0.5 \leqslant x \leqslant 0.5$ and so only one root in this domain.

**8** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-328_372_524_393_1249.jpg)

One point of intersection for $-2 \pi<x<-\frac{\pi}{2}$ and so only one root of $1=\frac{\sin x}{2 x+3}$ on this domain. Also, should $x$ be less than $-2 \pi$ or should $x$ be greater than $-\frac{\pi}{2}$, the line and curve will not intersect again and so this is the only point of intersection of $y=\frac{\sin x}{2 x+3}$ and $y=1$.

b Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=\frac{\sin x}{2 x+3}-1=0$ then $f(-2)=\frac{\sin (-2)}{-4+3}-1=-0.09070$ and $\mathrm{f}(-1.9)=\frac{\sin (-1.9)}{-3.8+3}-1=0.1828$ Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**9** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-328_472_574_1576_1251.jpg)

One point of intersection for $0 \leqslant x \leqslant 5$ and so only one root.

b Let $\mathrm{f}(x)=x^{3}-3 x-4$ then

$$
\begin{align}
& f(2)=2^{3}-3(2)-4=-2 \text { and } \\
& f(3)=3^{3}-3(3)-4=14
\end{align}
$$

Change of sign indicates presence of root.

**10** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-329_433_524_272_348.jpg)

**11** a

![](./images/6c997e27-652f-45af-9aa3-c3a1b39841c3-329_413_576_1024_349.jpg)

The distance home is $10 \sqrt{169+64}=153 \mathrm{~cm}$, correct to the nearest cm.
